Time-Frequency Analysis Lecture Notes
Markus Faulhuber

Abstract




These lecture notes accompanied the course
Time-Frequency Analysis
given at the Faculty of Mathematics of the University of Vienna in the summer term 2021. The material is suitable for an advanced undergraduate course in mathematics or a mathematics class for PhD students. Besides standard linear algebra and calculus only some basics from functional analysis are needed. A course in Fourier analysis may be of advantage, but is not needed. The course contained 4 academic units per week. The appendices and Section
11
were not presented in class.

I gratefully acknowledge the feedback from my students throughout the course and for pointing out several typos in the lecture notes. In particular, the comments of L. Köhldorfer, F. Moscatelli, I. Shafkulovska, and E. Stefanescu helped to improve these lecture notes.

Hitherto communication theory was based on two alternative methods of signal analysis. One is the description of the signal as a function of time; the other is Fourier analysis. Both are idealizations, as the first method operates with sharply defined instants of time, the second with infinite wave-trains of rigorously defined frequencies. But our everyday experiences — especially our auditory sensations — insist on a description in terms of
both
time and frequency.

–
[
14
]
D. Gabor. Theory of Communication,
Journal of the Institution of the Electrical Engineers
, 93(26):429–457 (1946).

Short biography
. Dennis Gabor (originally Dénes Gábor, June 5, 1900 – February 8, 1979) was born in Budapest, Austria-Hungary at that time. He was an electrical engineer and physicist. After gaining his doctorate from the Charlottenburg Technical University in Berlin, now known as the Technical University of Berlin, he worked for Siemens AG. In 1933 he left Nazi Germany, as he was considered to be Jewish. Britain invited him to work at the development department of the British Thomson-Houston company (BTH) and he also became a British citizen. In 1947 he invented holography, for which he was awarded the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1971:

“
For his invention and development of the holographic method.
”

https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/physics/1971/gabor/biographical/

Information.

The main sources for these lecture notes are the textbooks by Folland
[
13
]
, de Gosson
[
16
]
and Gröchenig
[
19
]
. Whenever a lack of references occurs, one of these sources may be consulted.

Some Engineering Terminology

In this section we are going to introduce some important notions from sampling theory. We mainly sum up the introduction from
[
55
]
and quote some passages verbatim. One aim of this section is to introduce some engineering terms and ‘translate’ them into mathematical terms.

The most important term in communication engineering and signal analysis is the term
signal
. Actually, there are several types of signals which we need to distinguish, namely continuous (analog) signals and discrete (digital) signals. Whenever we only speak of a signal, we mean the first type. Mathematically, a signal is nothing but a (continuous) function, which is why we may use the words signal and function interchangeably. From a physical point of view, a signal
f(t)
may represent the voltage difference at time
t
between two points in an electrical circuit or the pressure of a sound wave. A digital signal, on the other hand, is just a sequence of numbers
(c_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

A prototype task in communication might be to convert an analog signal into a digital signal, transmit the digital signal to a receiver and convert it back to an analog signal (maybe even the original one). By processing a signal
f
we mean operating on it in some fashion and usually also require that this operation is reversible. Mathematically, this means we apply some transformation (linear or not) to the function
f
which we require to be invertible.

Lastly, we want to transmit information in a “cheap” fashion, meaning that we want to extract as little as possible data from a signal in order to reconstruct it at the receiver. This is where sampling theory begins.

By sampling a signal
f
, i.e., evaluating
f
at discrete points, we apply a (linear) transformation to convert an analog signal into a digital signal. Ways of converting the discrete signal back to the continuous signal usually go under the name
sampling theorem
. We will study a specific sampling theorem (actually
the
sampling theorem par excellence) later on. There, it will be of importance that the signal is
band-limited
. This simply means that the so-called
spectrum
of the signal has finite support. On the other hand, a signal which only lasts for a finite duration is called
time-limited
. The classical uncertainty principle tells us that it is impossible for a signal to be both, time- and band-limited. This would pose great problems for digital communication, but (luckily) signals can be almost time- and band-limited at the same time.

Throughout the manuscript, we will also assign more physical and engineering terminology to certain mathematical concepts.

Notation and Recap

One of the most essential tools in time-frequency analysis is the Fourier transform of a function
f
. The values
f(x)
represent the temporal behavior of a signal and its Fourier transform
\widehat{f}(\omega)
describes the amplitudes of the occurring frequencies.

The variables
x
and
\omega
will usually be in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For the special case
d=1
,
x
is a point in time and
\omega
is a specific frequency. More generally,
x
can be chosen from a locally compact Abelian group
G
and
\omega
is then a character from the dual group
\widehat{G}
. In the case of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the dual group
\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and we will not distinguish between them. Another prominent example of a locally compact Abelian group is the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
which has dual group
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
(and vice versa).

We start with introducing some notation and a collection of essential results.

Notation

Vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are always considered to be column vectors;

x=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{d}\end{pmatrix}

(0.1)

However, for convenience reasons we will usually write

x=(x_{1},\ \ldots,\ x_{d}).

(0.2)

Another notation we will use is the following

x^{2}=x\cdot x=x^{T}x.

(0.3)

Hence,
\cdot
denotes the standard Euclidean inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
x^{T}
is, of course, the transpose of
x
. The Euclidean norm is denoted by
|.|
and given by

|x|=\sqrt{x\cdot x}\ .

(0.4)

If
M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is a square matrix, we will also use the notation

Mx^{2}=x\cdot Mx\quad\text{ and }\quad M^{-T}=(M^{-1})^{T}=(M^{T})^{-1}.

(0.5)

In the latter case,
M
needs to be invertible (of course).

The complex vector
z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
consists of a real and imaginary part denoted by

z=\Re(z)+i\ \Im(z).

(0.6)

We will use the same notation in
\mathbb{C}^{d}
as in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
;

z^{2}=z\cdot z=z^{T}z,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}.

(0.7)

However, note that this is not the inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
. The norm of an element in
\mathbb{C}^{d}
is given by

|z|^{2}=\overline{z}\cdot z=z^{*}z,\quad z^{*}=\overline{z}^{T}.

(0.8)

The integral

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dots\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x_{1},\ \ldots,\,x_{d})\,dx_{1}\dots dx_{d}

(0.9)

is the Lebesgue integral of
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the measure of a measurable set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is given by

|\Omega|=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\Omega}(x)\,dx,

(0.10)

where
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\Omega}
is the indicator function on the set
\Omega
. For a number
1\leq p<\infty
, the
L^{p}
-norm, or simply
p
-norm, of a function
f
is denoted by

\lVert f\rVert_{p}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x)|^{p}dx\right)^{1/p}.

(0.11)

The Banach space of all functions with finite
p
-norm is denoted by
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Of course, a function is then only defined up to an equivalence class and
f
is actually a representative of the class. The usual adjustment for the case
p=\infty
is

\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}=\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x)|.

(0.12)

At this point we re-call Hölder’s inequality. For
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
g\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
, we have

\lVert f\,g\rVert_{1}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{p}\lVert g\rVert_{q}.

(0.13)

For
p=2
, we actually get a Hilbert space with inner product

\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\,\overline{g(x)}\,dx,\qquad\langle f,f\rangle=\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(0.14)

In particular, Hölder’s inequality for the case
p=q=2
now implies the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality by using the triangle inequality.

|\langle f,g\rangle|=|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\overline{g(t)}\,dt|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(t)g(t)|\,dt=\lVert fg\rVert_{1}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}.

(0.15)

A signal
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is said to have finite energy, as
\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}
is considered as the energy of a signal. Thus, the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is also called the space of finite-energy signals.

The Fourier Transform

The Fourier transform is usually first defined for functions in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and then extended to all of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by a density argument. We will skip the technical details here and define the Fourier transform right away for functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We stress the fact that there are several different normalizations for the Fourier transform and that the choice of normalization affects all further normalizations which we need to make in the sequel.

Definition 0.1
.

The Fourier transform of
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is given by

\mathcal{F}f(\omega)=\widehat{f}(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\,e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\,dx.

(0.16)

In engineering terms, the function
\widehat{f}
is called the amplitude spectrum of the signal
f
. The notation
\mathcal{F}f
reflects the fact that the Fourier transform is a linear operator acting on a function space, whereas the notation
\widehat{f}
refers to the fact that the Fourier transform of an
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
function is again a function (in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
).

A signal
f
is completely described by its amplitude spectrum
\widehat{f}
(and vice versa) and can be written as a continuous superposition of its spectral values;

f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widehat{f}(\omega)e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}\,d\omega

(0.17)

We identify the above formula as the inverse Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
. For the formula to hold point-wise,
\widehat{f}
needs to be integrable and
f
needs to be continuous and integrable (continuity is necessary for the point-wise statement and integrability to assure that we can define
\widehat{f}
). The inversion formula holds on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(a.e.) by using the inversion formula on the dense subspace
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and a density argument.

For an integrable function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we note the fact

\lVert\widehat{f}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{1},

(0.18)

which is refined in the following lemma.

Lemma 0.2

(Riemann-Lebesgue)
.

If
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
\widehat{f}
is uniformly continuous and

\lim_{|\omega|\to\infty}|\widehat{f}(\omega)|\to 0.

(0.19)

The next result shows that the Fourier transform is actually a unitary operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Theorem 0.3

(Plancherel)
.

For
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\lVert f\rVert_{2}=\lVert\widehat{f}\rVert_{2}.

(0.20)

More generally, we have Parseval’s formula.

Theorem 0.4

(Parseval)
.

For
f,\,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\langle f,g\rangle=\langle\widehat{f},\widehat{g}\rangle.

(0.21)

As a next result, we will give the extension of equation (
0.18
).

Theorem 0.5

(Hausdorff-Young)
.

Let
1\leq p\leq 2
and let
q
be such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Then

\mathcal{F}\colon L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\qquad\textnormal{ and }\qquad\lVert\widehat{f}\rVert_{q}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{p}.

(0.22)

Definition 0.6
.

The convolution of two functions
f
and
g
is defined as

f*g\,(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(y)g(x-y)\,dy

(0.23)

We recall that convolution is taken to point-wise multiplication under the Fourier transform.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(f*g)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(y)g(x-y)\,dy\right)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\,dx

(0.24)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(y)g(x-y)\,dy\right)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot y}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot(x-y)}\,dx

(0.25)

\displaystyle\hskip-7.11317pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{Fubini}}}{{=}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(y)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot y}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(x-y)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot(x-y)}\,dx\right)\,dy

(0.26)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{F}f\,\mathcal{F}g)(\omega).

(0.27)

Also, we will use Young’s convolution inequality.

Theorem 0.7

(Young’s Inequality)
.

Let
1\leq p,q\leq r\leq\infty
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{r}+1
and suppose
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
g\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

\lVert f*g\rVert_{r}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{p}\lVert g\rVert_{q}.

(0.28)

In particular, if
f,\ g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then the convolution satisfies

\lVert f*g\rVert_{1}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{1}\lVert g\rVert_{1}\ ,

(0.29)

We remark that (
0.29
) shows that
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a Banach algebra under convolution and, by employing the Lemma of Riemann-Lebesgue, that the Fourier transform maps
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into a (dense) subalgebra of
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The following theorem and its proof are taken from
[
13
, App. A]
and states that the Fourier transform of a Gaussian is another Gaussian.

Theorem 0.8

(Fourier Transform of a Gaussian)
.

Let
M
be a
d\times d
matrix with real entries and the properties

M^{T}=M\quad\text{ and }\quad M>0,

(0.30)

where the second assertion means that
M
is positive definite. Then, for any
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\pi Mx^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\,dx=\det(M)^{-1/2}e^{-\pi M^{-1}\omega^{2}}

(0.31)

Proof.

The proof proceeds in 3 steps. First, we prove the case for
d=1
, then for the case that
M
is a diagonal matrix and, lastly, the general case stated in the theorem.

Step 1
. Let
d=1
so
M
is a real, positive scalar. Let

I(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\pi Mx^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega x}\,dx.

(0.32)

Then, we are allowed to differentiate under the integral and we have

\displaystyle I^{\prime}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(-2\pi ix)e^{-\pi Mx^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega x}\,dx=\frac{i}{M}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{d}{dx}(e^{-\pi Mx^{2}})e^{-2\pi i\omega x}\,dx

(0.33)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Int.~by parts}}}{{=}}-\frac{i}{M}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\pi Mx^{2}}\dfrac{d}{dx}(e^{-2\pi i\omega x})\,dx=-\frac{2\pi\omega}{M}I(\omega).

(0.34)

Therefore, we have that
\frac{I^{\prime}(\omega)}{I(\omega)}=-\frac{2\pi\omega}{M}
, or
\frac{d}{d\omega}\log(I(\omega))=-\frac{2\pi\omega}{M}
. Therefore,

\log(I(\omega))=-\frac{\pi\omega^{2}}{M}+C,

(0.35)

or, equivalently

I(\omega)=e^{-\frac{\pi\omega^{2}}{M}+C}=C_{1}\,e^{-\frac{\pi\omega^{2}}{M}}.

(0.36)

Now,

C_{1}=I(0)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\pi Mx^{2}}\,dx=M^{-1/2},

(0.37)

which shows the result for the first case.

Step 2
. Suppose
M
is a
d\times d
diagonal matrix. Then the integral under consideration factors;

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\pi Mx^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\,dx=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\pi M_{1,1}x_{1}^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega_{1}x_{1}}\,dx_{1}\right)\ldots\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\pi M_{d,d}x_{d}^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega_{d}x_{d}},\,dx_{d}\right).

(0.38)

Hence, the result follows by employing the result from the first step.

Step 3
. Now, suppose
M
is not a diagonal matrix. Since
M^{T}=M
there is a rotation
R
such that
R^{T}MR=D
is diagonal. Setting
x=Ry
and using the fact that
R^{T}=R^{-1}
, by step 2 we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\pi Mx^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\,dx

\displaystyle=\underbrace{|\det(R)|}_{=1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\pi(Ry)\cdot M(Ry)}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot Ry}\,dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\pi Dy^{2}}e^{-2\pi i(R^{T}\omega)\cdot y}\,dy

(0.39)

\displaystyle=\det(D)^{-1/2}e^{-\pi(R^{-1}\omega)\cdot D^{-1}(R^{-1}\omega)}=\det(M)^{-1/2}e^{-\pi M^{-1}\omega^{2}}.

(0.40)

∎

Another prominent function in this manuscript is the indicator function of the interval
[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
. Its Fourier transform is the
\sinc
function, also called cardinal sine.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(x)e^{-2\pi i\omega x}\,dx=\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}e^{-2\pi i\omega x}\,dx

(0.41)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-2\pi i\omega x}}{-2\pi i\omega}\Big|_{x=-1/2}^{1/2}=-\frac{e^{-\pi i\omega}-e^{\pi i\omega}}{2i\pi\omega}=-\frac{\sin(-\pi\omega)}{\pi\omega}=\frac{\sin(\pi\omega)}{\pi\omega}=\sinc(\omega).

(0.42)

We note that the value at 0 equals 1 (by using l’Hospital’s rule) and that the
\sinc
-function is not Lebesgue-integrable. However, it is a finite energy signal (since
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
). Thus,
\sinc
possesses a Fourier transform as an
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-function. Since it is an even function, its Fourier transform and its inverse Fourier transform
1
1

1

Note that the Fourier transform of the
\sinc
-function cannot be computed by real integration methods, but that it can be computed using complex methods (using the homotopy version of Cauchy’s integral theorem and the residue theorem). The advantage of the complex method is that the singularity at
x=0
(which is a removable singularity) can be avoided by choosing a “roundabout” contour near 0.
coincide;

\mathcal{F}\sinc(\omega)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\sinc(\omega)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]})(\omega)=\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(\omega).

(0.43)

Fourier Series

By

e_{k}(t)=e^{2\pi ik\cdot t},\quad t\in\mathbb{T}^{d}

(0.44)

we denote the complex exponential with frequency
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. We say that
e_{k}
2
2

2

More generally, if
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we say that
e_{\omega}(t)=e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot t}
is a pure frequency of frequency
\omega
. However, note that, in general,
e_{\omega}
need not be periodic with period 1.
is a pure (integer) frequency. Recall that the set of pure integer frequencies, i.e.,

\{e^{2\pi ik\cdot t}\mid k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}

(0.45)

is an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
or
L^{2}([0,1]^{d})
. It is easy to see that the system is orthonormal by a simple calculation (which we perform only for
d=1
for notational convenience, the
d
-dimensional case follows by iterated integration.);

\langle e_{k},e_{l}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}e^{2\pi ikt}e^{-2\pi ilt}\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{T}}e^{2\pi i(k-l)t}\,dt=\begin{cases}1,&k=l\\
0,&\text{ else}\end{cases}

(0.46)

By the Weierstrass approximation theorem, the trigonometric polynomials
\sum_{k=-N}^{N}c_{k}e_{k}(t)
are dense in
C(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and, therefore, in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Therefore, the set of pure integer frequencies is an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. As a consequence, a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
can be expanded as

f(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}c_{k}e^{2\pi ik\cdot t},

(0.47)

with coefficients

c_{k}=\langle f,e_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(0.48)

Plancherel’s theorem holds in this case as well;

\lVert(c_{k})\rVert_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})}=\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(0.49)

Also, for
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\supset L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
3
3

3

Note that we do not have such an inclusion for
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-spaces because the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is not finite.
the Lemma of Riemann-Lebesgue holds;

\langle f,e_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\to 0,\quad|k|\to\infty.

(0.50)

Furthermore, using the multi-index convention
|k|=k_{1}+\ldots+k_{d}
now, if we denote the space of trigonometric polynomials of order not larger than
N
by

T_{N}=\{p_{N}(t)=\sum_{|k|=-N}^{N}c_{k}e^{2\pi ik\cdot t}\mid t\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\,c_{k}\in\mathbb{C},\,N\in\mathbb{N}\},

(0.51)

then the truncated Fourier series is the best approximating element in
T_{N}
for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
;

\lVert f-\sum_{|k|=-N}^{N}\langle f,e_{k}\rangle e_{k}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leq\lVert f-p_{N}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})},\quad\forall p_{N}\in T_{N}.

(0.52)

Equation (
0.47
) admits the following interpretation. A periodic signal
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
can be decomposed into pure waves of integer frequencies with amplitudes
(c_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
. The amplitudes
(c_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
are derived from taking linear measurements of the signal
f
with respect to the orthonormal system
\{e_{k}\}
.

A goal of time-frequency analysis, following the ideas in
[
14
]
, is the decomposition of non-periodic signals into “simple building blocks” similar to (
0.47
), such that the occurring coefficients can be interpreted as the amplitudes of the building blocks. In particular, the coefficients should contain some information on how “strong” a certain frequency is at a certain point in time.

1
Basic Concepts in Time-Frequency Analysis

We consider functions from the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and define the following operators;

T_{x}f(t)=f(t-x)\qquad\textnormal{ and }\qquad M_{\omega}f(t)=f(t)\,e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot t}.

(1.1)

These operators (illustrated in Figure
1
) are usually called translation operator and modulation operator, respectively. However, in the field of time-frequency analysis we say
T_{x}
is a time-shift by
x
and
M_{\omega}
is called a frequency-shift by
\omega
. These operators are unitary on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e.,

\langle T_{x}f,T_{x}g\rangle=\langle f,g\rangle\qquad\textnormal{ and }\qquad\langle M_{\omega}f,M_{\omega}g\rangle=\langle f,g\rangle.

(1.2)

(a)

A Gauss function.

(b)

A time-shifted Gauss function.

(c)

A frequency shifted Gauss function (real part) with its Gaussian envelop.

(d)

A time-frequency shifted Gauss function (real part) with its Gaussian envelop.

Figure 1:
The standard Gauss function
g_{0}(t)=2^{1/4}e^{-\pi t^{2}}
and its time-, frequency- and time-frequency-shifted copies (real part)
T_{2}g_{0}
,
M_{2}g_{0}
,
M_{2}T_{2}g_{0}
, respectively.

In general, they do not commute. We have the canonical commutation relation

M_{\omega}T_{x}=e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\,T_{x}M_{\omega},

(1.3)

which is at the heart of time-frequency analysis. Equation (
1.3
) shows that time-shifts and frequency-shifts do not commute in general. However, they commute up to a phase factor. A phase factor is a complex number of modulus 1 (
|c|=1
,
c\in\mathbb{C}
). The commutation relation for time- and frequency-shifts (
1.3
) will accompany us throughout the course. Sometimes the non-commutativity may simply be ignored (e.g., because we are interested in absolute values), other times the bookkeeping of the exponential factors might be annoying but important and again other times the non-commutativity will be crucial to obtain (beautiful) results right at the heart of time-frequency analysis.

Equation (
1.3
) follows from a direct computation;

\displaystyle M_{\omega}T_{x}f(t)=f(t-x)\,e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot t}=f(t-x)\,e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot(t-x)}e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot x}=e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\ T_{x}M_{\omega}f(t).

(1.4)

It is immediate from (
1.3
) that time-shifts and frequency shifts commute if and only if
\omega\cdot x\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Furthermore, for any
1\leq p\leq\infty
, time- and frequency-shifts (and hence time-frequency shifts) are isometries on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e.,

\lVert M_{\omega}T_{x}f\rVert_{p}=\lVert f\rVert_{p}\ .

(1.5)

Next, we study the behavior of
T_{x}
and
M_{\omega}
under the Fourier transform. By a direct calculation, we will see that

\mathcal{F}T_{x}=M_{-x}\mathcal{F}\qquad\textnormal{ and }\qquad\mathcal{F}M_{\omega}=T_{\omega}\mathcal{F}.

(1.6)

We start with the first assertion.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(T_{x}f)(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t-x)\,e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot t}\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\,e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot(t+x)}\,dt=M_{-x}(\mathcal{F}f)(\xi).

(1.7)

The second formula follows analogously.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(M_{\omega}f)(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\,e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot t}e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot t}\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\,e^{-2\pi i(\xi-\omega)\cdot t}\,dt=T_{\omega}(\mathcal{F}f)(\xi).

(1.8)

This shows that the use of the word frequency-shift is justified for the operator
M_{\omega}
.

By combining (
1.3
) and (
1.6
) we get

\mathcal{F}M_{\omega}T_{x}=T_{\omega}M_{-x}\mathcal{F}=e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot x}M_{-x}T_{\omega}\mathcal{F}

(1.9)

The composition of
T_{x}
and
M_{\omega}
is called a time-frequency shift, which we denote by

\pi(\lambda)=M_{\omega}T_{x},\qquad\lambda=(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\,.

(1.10)

In the sequel, we will also make use of other, familiar operators. We start with the isotropic dilation operator, which we denote by
D_{a}
. It acts on a function in the following way;

D_{a}f(t)=a^{-d/2}f(a^{-1}t),\qquad a>0,\ t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(1.11)

The factor
a^{-d/2}
makes the operator unitary. We are also interested in the operator’s behavior under the Fourier transform. This will be helpful to get an intuitive understanding for uncertainty principles, introduced later on.

\mathcal{F}D_{a}=D_{\frac{1}{a}}\mathcal{F}\ .

(1.12)

This can easily be seen by the following calculation. First, note that
\Phi_{a}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
t\mapsto a^{-1}t
is a linear diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
a>0
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(D_{a}f)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a^{-d/2}f(a^{-1}t)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(1.13)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a^{-d/2}f\left(\Phi_{a}^{-1}(a^{-1}t)\right)\underbrace{\left|\det\left(D\Phi_{a}^{-1}\right)\right|}_{=a^{d}}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot\Phi_{a}^{-1}(t)}\,dt

(1.14)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a^{d/2}f(t)e^{-2\pi ia\omega\cdot t}\,dt=D_{\frac{1}{a}}(\mathcal{F}f)(\omega).

(1.15)

We will also encounter a more general (anisotropic) dilation operator, which acts on functions in a similar way.

Furthermore, we note that the Fourier transform takes differentiation to multiplication with a monomial and vice versa. By
\partial_{k}
, we denote the partial derivative in the
k
-th component. Then

\mathcal{F}\left(\partial_{k}^{n}f\right)(\omega)=(2\pi i\omega_{k})^{n}\,\mathcal{F}f(\omega)\qquad\textnormal{ and }\qquad\mathcal{F}\Big((-2\pi it_{k})^{n}f\Big)(\omega)=\partial_{k}^{n}(\mathcal{F}f)(\omega).

(1.16)

It suffices to carry out the proof for
n=1
. First, assume that
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(the Schwartz space of rapidly decreasing functions). Then, by using integration by parts, we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\left(\partial_{k}f(t)\right)(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{k}f(t)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt=\underbrace{f(t)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\Big|_{t=-\infty}^{\infty}}_{=0}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)(-2\pi i\omega_{k})e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(1.17)

\displaystyle=(2\pi i\omega_{k})\mathcal{F}f(\omega).

(1.18)

The result for higher order derivatives follows by induction and the result for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
follows by a density argument. For the second assertion we use the differential quotient and denote the unit vector in the
k
-th direction by
\mathbf{e}_{k}
.

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{\widehat{f}(\omega+\delta\mathbf{e}_{k})-\widehat{f}(\omega)}{\delta}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{e^{-2\pi i\delta\mathbf{e}_{k}\cdot t}-1}{\delta}\,dt

(1.19)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}(-2\pi it_{k})\,dt.

(1.20)

The exchange of integration and taking the limit is justified by the dominated convergence theorem and for taking the limit we used l’Hospital’s rule. Again, the result for
n>1
follows by induction and the result for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by a density argument.

2
The Short-Time Fourier Transform

The short-time Fourier transform is the fundamental tool in time-frequency analysis. In a distributional sense, it can be seen as a generalization of the Fourier transform, as we will see later. It aims to overcome the drawback that
f(t)
only gives temporal information of a signal
f
while under the Fourier transform we obtain only information about its frequency distribution
\widehat{f}(\omega)
. We will start with an illustrative example of a multi-component signal.

Example 2.1
.

Let
f
be the following multi-component signal.

\displaystyle f(t)=\begin{cases}e^{4\pi it}+e^{8\pi it},&-3\leq t\leq-1\\
e^{2\pi it},&-1\leq t\leq 1\\
e^{2\pi it^{2}},&1\leq t\leq 4\\
0,&\text{else}\end{cases}.

(2.1)

(a)

Real part of the multi-component signal.

(b)

Imaginary part of the multi-component signal.

Figure 2:
A multi-component signal consisting of a superposition of two pure frequencies with 2 and 4 Hz, followed by a pure frequency of 1 Hz and a linear chirp, i.e., the frequency increases linearly.

(a)

Absolute value of the positive frequencies of
\widehat{f}(\omega)
.

Figure 3:
The positive part of the Fourier transform of the multi-component signal. There are peeks around the major occurring frequencies at 1,2 and 4 Hz. Also, the contributions of the chirp are apparent throughout the frequency band
[2,8]
Hz.

In order to obtain local information of the signal
f
, we can use a window function
g
. We will use the standard box function
b_{0}
, which is the generator of all
b
-splines, and the standard Gaussian window
g_{0}
.

\displaystyle b_{0}(t)=\begin{cases}1,&-\frac{1}{2}\leq t\leq\frac{1}{2}\\
0,&\text{else}\end{cases}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad g_{0}(t)=2^{1/4}e^{-\pi t^{2}}.

(2.2)

We note that both windows are normalized
\lVert b_{0}\rVert_{2}=1
and
\lVert g_{0}\rVert_{2}=1
.

(a)

Real part of the signal localized around 0 with the box function
b_{0}
.

(b)

Real part of the signal localized around 0 with the standard Gaussian
g_{0}
.

Figure 4:
Localization of the multi-component signal with the box function and the standard Gaussian.

(a)

Absolute value of the Fourier transform of the localized signal using
b_{0}
.

(b)

Absolute value of the Fourier transform of the localized signal using
g_{0}
.

Figure 5:
The Fourier transform of the windowed signals reveals a local behavior as well.

However, a joint time-frequency distribution should yield a picture in the time-frequency plane which reveals the local behavior of the signal. We illustrate such a representation with 2 spectrograms. The precise definition of a spectrogram will be given in Section
3.1
. As can already be seen from Figure
4
and Figure
5
, the choice of the window crucially affects the localization properties of the signal. This fact will also become apparent in Figure
6
.

(a)

A spectrogram using the window
b_{0}
.

(b)

A spectrogram using the window
g_{0}
.

Figure 6:
The spectrograms of the multi-component signal with different localization windows. The
x
-direction indicates the position to which the window is shifted and the
\omega
-direction shows the values of the energy distribution of the local Fourier transfroms.

We note that the use of the box function
b_{0}
does not yield a satisfying localization of the signal in the Fourier domain, as smoothness of a function affects the decay of its Fourier transform. Therefore, the localized Fourier transform with window
b_{0}
decays only like
\frac{1}{\omega}
. This can also be seen in Figure
6
(a).

\diamond

After this motivating example, we come to the definition of the short-time Fourier transform (STFT) and discuss some of its properties. We mainly follow the textbook by Gröchenig
[
19
, Chap. 3]
.

Definition 2.2
.

For a (non-zero) function
g
, called a window function, the short-time Fourier transform of a function
f
with respect to the window
g
is given by

V_{g}f(x,\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\overline{g(t-x)}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt=\langle f,\pi(\lambda)g\rangle,\qquad\lambda=(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}.

(2.3)

For the moment, we only give this formal definition without further specification of
f
and
g
. The STFT is also called sliding-window Fourier transform or voice transform. The notation
V_{g}f
comes from the latter name. The name sliding window Fourier transform comes from the interpretation of
V_{g}f(x,.)
(
x
fixed) as a local Fourier transform of
f
around the point
x
. As
x
varies, the window
g
slides along the
x
-axis to all possible positions. Written as an inner product, the STFT is often interpreted as a linear measurement, taken at the point
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

Example 2.3
.

We will compute the STFT of the standard Gaussian

g_{0}(t)=2^{d/4}e^{-\pi t^{2}},

(2.4)

so
\lVert g_{0}\rVert_{2}=1
, with itself as a window.

\displaystyle V_{g_{0}}g_{0}(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=2^{d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\pi t^{2}}e^{-\pi(t-x)^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(2.5)

\displaystyle=2^{d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\pi(t+\frac{x}{2})^{2}}e^{-\pi(t-\frac{x}{2})^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot(t+\frac{x}{2})}\,dt

(2.6)

\displaystyle=2^{d/2}e^{-\pi i\omega\cdot x}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-2\pi(t^{2}+\frac{x^{2}}{4})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(2.7)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i\omega\cdot x}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}x^{2}}2^{d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-2\pi t^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(2.8)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i\omega\cdot x}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}x^{2}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\pi t^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\frac{\omega}{2^{1/2}}\cdot t}\,dt}_{\mathcal{F}g_{0}\left(\frac{\omega}{2^{1/2}}\right)}

(2.9)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i\omega\cdot x}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(x^{2}+\omega^{2})}.

(2.10)

Thus, the STFT of a Gaussian is again a Gaussian (in the time-frequency plane with a precisely determined phase factor).

\diamond

We will now study the basic properties of the STFT. Besides writing the STFT as an inner product of
f
and
M_{\omega}T_{x}g
, we can also write it in various other ways, which can be useful at times.

\displaystyle V_{g}f(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(f\,T_{x}\overline{g})(\omega)

(2.11)

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{f},T_{\omega}M_{-x}\widehat{g}\rangle

(2.12)

\displaystyle=e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\omega}\mathcal{F}(\widehat{f}\,T_{\omega}\overline{\widehat{g}})(-x)

(2.13)

\displaystyle=e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\omega}(f*M_{\omega}\overline{g}^{\vee})(x)

(2.14)

\displaystyle=(\widehat{f}*M_{-x}\widehat{\overline{g}}^{\vee})(\omega),

(2.15)

where
g^{\vee}(x)=g(-x)
is the flip operation.

So far, we have not discussed when the STFT is well-defined. Clearly, all of the above formulas make sense for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
functions because if
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then the product
f\,T_{x}\overline{g}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and
V_{g}f(x,\omega)=\mathcal{F}(f\,T_{x}\overline{g})(\omega)
is defined point-wise. For most of the time, we will be happy with the assumption that
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(because then we may use Parseval’s theorem and Plancherel’s theorem). However, by Hölder’s inequality (
0.13
)
f\,T_{x}\overline{g}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
whenever
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
g\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
, so the STFT is again defined point-wise in this case.

The following result can partially be seen as a “Riemann-Lebesgue-like” statement for the STFT.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
V_{g}f
is uniformly continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

Proof.

This follows mainly from the continuity of the operator groups
\{T_{x}\}
and
\{M_{\omega}\}
, i.e.,

\lim_{x\to 0}\lVert T_{x}f-f\rVert_{2}=0

(2.16)

and

\lim_{\omega\to 0}\lVert M_{\omega}f-f\rVert_{2}=\lim_{\omega\to 0}\lVert T_{\omega}\widehat{f}-\widehat{f}\rVert_{2}=0.

(2.17)

The statement follows by combining the two results.
∎

Using the Lemma of Riemann-Lebesgue, we also conclude that
\lim_{|\omega|\to\infty}V_{g}f(x,\omega)=0
for any
x
(because
f\,T_{x}\overline{g}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
).

The following property of the STFT shows a fundamental concept in time-frequency analysis. The usual interpretation is that, up to a phase factor, the Fourier transform rotates the time-frequency plane (by
90^{\circ}
in the case
d=1
). Also, it shows that if we localize a signal
f
with a window
g
, we localize its spectrum
\widehat{f}
with the Fourier transform
\widehat{g}
of the original window.

Proposition 2.5

(Fundamental Identity of Time-Frequency Analysis)
.

For
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the following holds;

V_{g}f(x,\omega)=e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{\widehat{g}}\widehat{f}(\omega,-x).

(2.18)

Proof.

The proof is a straight-forward computation.

\displaystyle V_{g}f(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\langle f,M_{\omega}T_{x}g\rangle=\langle f,e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot x}T_{x}M_{\omega}g\rangle

(2.19)

\displaystyle=e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\langle f,T_{x}M_{\omega}g\rangle=e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\langle\mathcal{F}f,\mathcal{F}T_{x}M_{\omega}g\rangle

(2.20)

\displaystyle=e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\langle\mathcal{F}f,M_{-x}T_{\omega}\mathcal{F}g\rangle=e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}V_{\widehat{g}}\widehat{f}(\omega,-x).

(2.21)

∎

The identity is sometimes also referred to as the
basic identity of time-frequency analysis
. Also, we conclude that
\lim_{|x|\to\infty}V_{g}f(x,\omega)=0
for any
\omega
because
|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|=|V_{\widehat{g}}\widehat{f}(\omega,-x)|
and we may apply the Lemma of Riemann-Lebesgue to
\widehat{f}\,T_{\omega}\widehat{g}
, for
\widehat{f},\widehat{g}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The next formula is known as the covariance principle. Its interpretation is that a time-frequency shift by
(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
simply translates the STFT in the time-frequency plane (again, up to a phase factor).

Proposition 2.6

(Covariance Principle)
.

For
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

V_{g}(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f)(x,\omega)=e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot(\omega-\eta)}V_{g}f(x-\xi,\omega-\eta),\qquad x,\xi,\omega,\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(2.22)

Proof.

The proof is a straight-forward computation.

\displaystyle V_{g}(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\langle M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f,M_{\omega}T_{x}g\rangle

(2.23)

\displaystyle=\langle f,T_{-\xi}M_{-\eta}M_{\omega}T_{x}g\rangle

(2.24)

\displaystyle=\langle f,e^{2\pi i\xi\cdot(-\eta+\omega)}M_{\omega-\eta}T_{x-\xi}g\rangle

(2.25)

\displaystyle=e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot(\omega-\eta)}V_{g}f(x-\xi,\omega-\eta)

(2.26)

∎

We remark that the above proof can be adapted and that the result stays true whenever
V_{g}f
is defined. The only
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
specific property we used this time was the inner product
\langle.,.\rangle
, so whenever this bracket is well-defined (e.g., by duality) the proof holds (e.g., for
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
or
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
).

The STFT also enjoys a property which is quite similar to Parseval’s identity (
0.21
).

Theorem 2.7

(Orthogonality Relations)
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
V_{g_{k}}f_{k}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
for
k\in\{1,2\}
and

\langle V_{g_{1}}f_{1},V_{g_{2}}f_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}=\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle\overline{\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle}.

(2.27)

Proof.

For technical reasons, we first assume that
g_{k}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(dense). Therefore,
f_{k}\,T_{x}g_{k}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and Parseval’s formula (
0.21
) applies to the integral in
\omega
.

\displaystyle\langle V_{g_{1}}f_{1},V_{g_{2}}f_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V_{g_{1}}f_{1}(x,\omega)\overline{V_{g_{2}}f_{2}(x,\omega)}\,d\omega\,dx

(2.28)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}(f_{1}\,T_{x}\overline{g_{1}})(\omega)\,\overline{\mathcal{F}(f_{2}\,T_{x}\overline{g_{2}})(\omega)}\,d\omega\right)\,dx

(2.29)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{1}(t)\overline{f_{2}(t)}\;\overline{g_{1}(t-x)}g_{2}(t-x)\,dt\right)\,dx\,.

(2.30)

As
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},dt)
, the product
f_{1}\,\overline{f_{2}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},dt)
and, by assumption, the product
\overline{g_{1}}g_{2}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},dx)
. We may therefore exchange the order of integration. We have

\displaystyle\langle V_{g_{1}}f_{1},V_{g_{2}}f_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{1}(t)\overline{f_{2}(t)}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\overline{g_{1}(t-x)}g_{2}(t-x)\,dx\right)\,dt

(2.31)

\displaystyle=\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle\overline{\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle}.

(2.32)

By a density argument the result extends to
g_{k}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
k=1,2
.
∎

A shorter proof can be obtained by introducing the following unitary operators for functions of
2d
variables and the tensor product of two functions;

\mathcal{T}_{a}F(x,t)=F(t,t-x)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{F}_{2}F(x,\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(x,t)e^{-2\pi it\cdot\omega}\,dt.

(2.33)

The operators
\mathcal{T}_{a}
is an asymmetric coordinate change and the operator
\mathcal{F}_{2}
is the partial Fourier transform in the second (
d
) variable(s). The tensor product of two functions is denoted by

(f\otimes g)(x,\omega)=f(x)g(\omega)

(2.34)

Second Proof of the Orthogonality Relations
. If
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

\displaystyle\langle V_{g_{1}}{f_{1}},V_{g_{2}}f_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{T}_{a}(f_{1}\otimes\overline{g_{1}}),\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{T}_{a}(f_{2}\otimes\overline{g_{2}})\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

(2.35)

\displaystyle=\langle f_{1}\otimes\overline{g_{1}},f_{2}\otimes\overline{g_{2}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

(2.36)

\displaystyle=\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle\overline{\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle}.

(2.37)

\square

As a corollary, we obtain a Plancherel-like result for the STFT.

Corollary 2.8
.

For
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{2}=\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}\,.

(2.38)

In particular, if
\lVert g\rVert_{2}=1
then

\lVert f\rVert_{2}=\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{2},\qquad\forall f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(2.39)

Hence, in this case the STFT is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.

We note that (
2.39
) tells us that
f
is completely determined by
V_{g}f
. Furthermore, we have the following implication

\langle f,M_{\omega}T_{x}g\rangle=0,\,\forall(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad f=0.

(2.40)

This means that the set of time-frequency shifts of the window
g
is complete in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and, hence, spans a dense subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Remark
.

In particular, the last result tells us that

X=\text{span }\{M_{\omega}T_{x}g_{0}\mid(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\}

(2.41)

is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
g_{0}(t)=2^{d/4}e^{-\pi t^{2}}
is the
d
-dimensional standard Gaussian. We used Plancherel’s theorem in order to prove this result (for general
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
), but there are other proofs as well (e.g., using Fourier series
[
19
, Chap. 1.5]
). Now, we will prove that Plancherel’s theorem is also a consequence of the fact that
X
is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

For
f\in X
we have

f(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}M_{\omega_{k}}T_{x_{k}}g_{0}.

(2.42)

When computing
\lVert f\rVert_{2}=\langle f,f\rangle
, we need to compute inner products of time-frequency shifted Gaussians. We recall that

\displaystyle\langle g_{0},M_{\omega}T_{x}g_{0}\rangle=e^{-\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(x^{2}+\omega^{2})}

(2.43)

We compute

\displaystyle\langle M_{\eta}T_{\xi}g_{0},M_{\omega}T_{x}g_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle g_{0},T_{-\xi}M_{\omega-\eta}T_{x}g_{0}\rangle

(2.44)

\displaystyle=\langle g_{0},e^{2\pi i\xi\cdot(\omega-\eta)}M_{\omega-\eta}T_{x-\xi}g_{0}\rangle

(2.45)

\displaystyle=e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot(\omega-\eta)}e^{-\pi i(x-\xi)\cdot(\omega-\eta)}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}((x-\xi)^{2}+(\omega-\eta)^{2})}

(2.46)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i(x+\xi)\cdot(\omega-\eta)}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}((x-\xi)^{2}+(\omega-\eta)^{2})}

(2.47)

Now, we perform the same computation for the Fourier transforms of the time-frequency shifted Gaussians.

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{F}(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}g_{0}),\mathcal{F}(M_{\omega}T_{x}g_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle e^{2\pi i\eta\cdot\xi}M_{-\xi}T_{\eta}\mathcal{F}g_{0},e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot x}M_{-x}T_{\omega}\mathcal{F}g_{0}\rangle

(2.48)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi i\eta\cdot\xi}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\langle g_{0},T_{-\eta}M_{\xi-x}T_{\omega}g_{0}\rangle

(2.49)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi i\eta\cdot\xi}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}\langle g_{0},e^{2\pi i\eta\cdot(\xi-x)}M_{\xi-x}T_{\omega-\eta}g_{0}\rangle

(2.50)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi i\eta\cdot\xi}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot x}e^{-2\pi i\eta\cdot(\xi-x)}e^{-\pi i(\xi-x)\cdot(\omega-\eta)}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}((\xi-x)^{2}+(\omega-\eta)^{2})}

(2.51)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i(\omega-\eta)\cdot(x+\xi)}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}((x-\xi)^{2}+(\omega-\eta)^{2})}.

(2.52)

In the last step we used the fact that
(x-\xi)^{2}=(\xi-x)^{2}
. Hence, these computations show that

\langle M_{\eta}T_{\xi}g_{0},M_{\omega}T_{x}g_{0}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{F}(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}g_{0}),\mathcal{F}(M_{\omega}T_{x}g_{0})\rangle

(2.53)

Therefore

\displaystyle\langle f,f\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}M_{\omega_{k}}T_{x_{k}}g_{0},\sum_{l=1}^{n}c_{l}M_{\omega_{l}}T_{x_{l}}g_{0}\rangle

(2.54)

\displaystyle=\langle\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}\mathcal{F}(M_{\omega_{k}}T_{x_{k}}g_{0}),\sum_{l=1}^{n}c_{l}\mathcal{F}(M_{\omega_{l}}T_{x_{l}}g_{0})\rangle

(2.55)

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{f},\widehat{f}\rangle,

(2.56)

by linearity of the inner product. Summing up, this shows that Plancherel’s theorem is equivalent to the completeness of the set of time-frequency shifted standard Gaussians.

\diamond

After knowing that the information of
f
is completely contained in
V_{g}f
, the question that pops up is: How can we recover
f
from
V_{g}f
?

Before we can answer this question, we need a quick excursion in vector-valued integrals. In time-frequency analysis, superpositions of time-frequency shifts, such as

f=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)(M_{\omega}T_{x}g)\,d(x,\omega).

(2.57)

are of utmost importance. Note that
g
is time-frequency shifted by
(x,\omega)
, but it also depends on its argument. Hence, the integral gives us back a vector (i.e., a function) in the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which depends on the same argument as
g
. Therefore, we may also speak of a function-valued integral. More generally, integrals may also be matrix- or operator-valued. Later on, we will use a weak formulation for such integrals. Furthermore, consider the following example. If
F\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, then the conjugate-linear functional

\ell(h)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)\overline{\langle h,M_{\omega}T_{x}g\rangle}\,d(x,\omega)

(2.58)

is a bounded functional on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. This can be seen by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the Plancherel-like result from Corollary
2.8
;

|\ell(h)|\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\lVert V_{g}h\rVert_{2}=\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\lVert g\rVert_{2}\lVert h\rVert_{2}.

(2.59)

This means that
\ell
defines a unique function
f=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g\,d(x,\omega)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with norm
\lVert f\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\lVert g\rVert_{2}
and satisfying
\ell(h)=\langle f,h\rangle
.

With this notion at hands, we will be able to prove the inversion formula for the STFT.

Theorem 2.9

(Inversion formula for the STFT)
.

Let
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and choose
\widetilde{g}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle\neq 0
. Then we have for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

f=\frac{1}{\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g}\,d(x,\omega).

(2.60)

Proof.

By Corollary
2.8

V_{g}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
. Therefore, the following vector-valued integral is a well defined function
\widetilde{f}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
;

\widetilde{f}=\frac{1}{\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g}\,d(x,\omega).

(2.61)

By using the orthogonality relations, we see that

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{f},h\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(x,\omega)\underbrace{\langle M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g},h\rangle}_{\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}h}}\,d(x,\omega)

(2.62)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle}\langle V_{g}f,V_{\widetilde{g}}h\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}=\frac{1}{\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle}\langle f,h\rangle\overline{\langle g,\widetilde{g}\rangle}

(2.63)

\displaystyle=\langle f,h\rangle.

(2.64)

This formula holds for any
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and we conclude that
\widetilde{f}=f
.
∎

Note that, as
g\neq 0
, we may actually choose
\widetilde{g}=g
in the inversion formula. Hence, in this case the inversion formula reads

f=\frac{1}{\lVert g\rVert_{2}^{2}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g\,d(x,\omega)=\frac{1}{\lVert g\rVert_{2}^{2}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(\lambda)\,\pi(\lambda)g\,d\lambda,\qquad\lambda=(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}.

(2.65)

Of course, this formula simplifies a bit if we assume that
\lVert g\rVert_{2}=1
,

f=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(\lambda)\,\pi(\lambda)g\,d\lambda.

(2.66)

Hence, a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be written as a continuous superposition of a time-frequency shifted window with weights obtained from its STFT. In this sense, (
2.60
) is similar to the inversion formula of the Fourier transform. The main difference is that the elementary building blocks, i.e., the complex exponential
e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}
, are not in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
whereas the elementary functions
M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g}
are usually picked to be particularly nice
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-functions.

The time-frequency analysis of a signal now usually consists of three steps.

Analysis.
Given a signal
f
, its STFT
V_{g}f
is computed. We will interpret it as a joint time-frequency distribution of
f
. The window
g
crucially influences the analysis and one usually demands that
g
and
\widehat{g}
decay sufficiently fast.

Processing.
The STFT
V_{g}f(x,\omega)
is then transformed into a new function
F(x,\omega)
. Some typical processing steps are truncation of
V_{g}f
to a region where something interesting seems to happen or where
|V_{g}f|
is above a given threshold. In an engineering language such processing steps are called feature extraction, signal segmentation and signal compression, depending on the purpose. Furthermore, the STFT is also used as a pre-processing tool in the area of machine learning.

Synthesis.
The processed signal is then reconstructed by using the modified inversion formula

\widetilde{f}=\frac{1}{\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g}\,d(x,\omega).

(2.67)

Note that signal
\widetilde{f}
is reconstructed from
F(x,\omega)
and not from
V_{g}f(x,\omega)
. Hence,
\widetilde{f}
may naturally differ from
f
. Also we remark again that distinct windows might be used for the analysis and the synthesis.

It is also customary to write the inversion formula as a superposition of rank-one operators. Consider a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and let
u\otimes\overline{v}
denote the rank-one operator defined by

(u\otimes\overline{v})(h)=\langle h,v\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}u,\quad u,v,h\in\mathcal{H}.

(2.68)

Then, the inversion formula (
2.60
) is the following continuous resolution of the identity operator

I_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\frac{1}{\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g}\otimes\overline{M_{\omega}T_{x}g}\,d(x,\omega).

(2.69)

3
Quadratic Time-Frequency Representations

Again, for most of this chapter we will follow the textbook by Gröchenig
[
19
]
.

Quadratic representations are often interpreted as joint (probability) densities of a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and its Fourier transform
\widehat{f}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. The space
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
might be called time-frequency plane or phase space, depending on the specific situation.

Such joint time-frequency representations were investigated by E. Wigner
[
53
]
in the context of quantum mechanics with the goal of finding a joint probability distribution for the position and momentum variables. Now, quadratic representations are also popular in engineering and we will call them (quadratic) time-frequency representations. Mathematically, we are looking for a sesquilinear form
G(f,g)(x,\omega)
; that is
G
is linear in the first argument
f
and conjugate linear in the second argument
g
. Then, there are two ways to make
G
“quadratic” in
f
. We either consider
Cf=|G(f,g)|^{2}
with
g
fixed or
Cf=G(f,f)
. In both cases the quadratic form satisfies

C(c_{1}f+c_{2}h)=|c_{1}|^{2}\,Cf+|c_{2}|^{2}\,Ch+c_{1}\overline{c_{2}}\,G(f,h)+\overline{c_{1}}c_{2}\,G(h,f),\quad c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{C}.

(3.1)

The non-linearity in (
3.1
) causes problems because the superposition of two signals
f
and
h
introduces the cross-terms
G(f,h)
and
G(h,f)
. These are often hard to separate from the main components of interest
Cf
and
Ch
. Also, the analysis and interpretation of these cross-terms is difficult.

On the positive side, a quadratic time-frequency representation of the form
G(f,f)
does not depend on a window
g
and should display the time-frequency content of
f
in a pure, unobstructed form.

3.1
The Spectrogram

In this section we are going to briefly discuss the spectrogram and some of its properties. We have seen examples already at the beginning of Section
2
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a window with the property
\lVert g\rVert_{2}=1
. Then, the spectrogram of
f
with respect to the window
g
is given by

\text{SPEC}_{g}f(x,\omega)=|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|^{2}.

(3.2)

As already apparent from its definition, the spectrogram
\text{SPEC}_{g}f
inherits properties from the STFT. In particular, it is covariant and preserves the energy and, furthermore, is non-negative (by definition);

•

\text{SPEC}_{g}f(x,\omega)\geq 0,\qquad\forall(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,

•

\text{SPEC}_{g}\left(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f\right)(x,\omega)=\text{SPEC}_{g}f(x-\xi,\omega-\eta)
by Proposition
2.6
, which we also called the Covariance Principle,

•

\lVert\text{SPEC}_{g}f\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}=\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}
by Corollary
2.8
.

The last point demonstrates the fact that, up to normalization of
f
, the spectrogram may serve as a probability density on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
of the joint time-frequency content of a signal
f
.

3.2
The Rihaczek Distribution

We will now study the Rihaczek distribution as described in
[
20
]
, which is a rather simple time-frequency representation. It is more or less simply the tensor product of
f
and its Fourier transform
\widehat{f}
.

Rf(x,\omega)=f(x)\overline{\widehat{f}(\omega)}e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\omega}.

(3.3)

The Rihaczek distribution is intimately connected to the STFT by the Fourier transform (on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
)

Rf(x,\omega)=\widehat{V_{f}f}(\omega,-x).

(3.4)

This is confirmed by a direct computation.

\displaystyle\widehat{V_{f}f}(\omega,-x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}V_{f}f(\xi,\eta)e^{-2\pi i(\xi\cdot\omega-x\cdot\eta)}\,d\eta\right)\,d\xi

(3.5)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\overline{f(t-\xi)}e^{-2\pi i\eta\cdot t}\,dt\right)e^{-2\pi i(\xi\cdot\omega-x\cdot\eta)}\,d\eta\right)\,d\xi

(3.6)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\underbrace{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\mathcal{F}\left(f\,T_{\xi}\overline{f}\right)(\eta)\right)\,e^{2\pi ix\cdot\eta}\,d\eta\right)}_{=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}\left(f\,T_{\xi}\overline{f}\right)\right)(x)}e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot\omega}\,d\xi

(3.7)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\overline{f(x-\xi)}e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot\omega}\,d\xi=f(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\overline{f(\xi)}e^{-2\pi i(-\xi+x)\cdot\omega}\,d\xi

(3.8)

\displaystyle=f(x)\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\xi)e^{2\pi i(-\xi+x)\cdot\omega}\,d\xi}=f(x)\overline{\widehat{f}(\omega)}e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\omega}

(3.9)

\displaystyle=Rf(x,\omega).

(3.10)

We can also state (
3.4
) in a more general way.

R(f,g)(x,\omega)=f(x)\overline{\widehat{g}(\omega)}e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\omega}=\widehat{V_{g}f}(\omega,-x).

(3.11)

We call
R(f,g)
the cross-Rihaczek distribution of
f
and
g
. In this context, we should
Rf=R(f,f)
actually call the auto-Rihaczek distribution of
f
. We note that, up to the complex exponential, the STFT factors under the Fourier transform. We will mainly use
Rf
when presenting the classical uncertainty principle of Heisenberg, Pauli and Weyl.

3.3
The Ambiguity Function

Another time-frequency representation, which is up to a complex exponential the same as the STFT, is the cross-ambiguity function of two functions
f
and
g
.

Definition 3.2
.

For
f
and
g
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, their cross-ambiguity function is defined as

A(f,g)(x,\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t+\tfrac{x}{2})\overline{g(t-\tfrac{x}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g}f(x,\omega).

(3.12)

For the case
f=g
, we write
Af=A(f,f)
and call it the (auto-)ambiguity function of
f
.

By definition, it follows immediately that

\overline{Af(-x,-\omega)}=Af(x,\omega).

(3.13)

Particularly,
Af(0,0)
is a real value. Of course, most properties of the STFT carry over to the cross-ambiguity function. In particular the orthogonality relations in Theorem
2.7
imply

\lVert Af\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}=\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}.

(3.14)

However, the ambiguity function
Af
is a quadratic representation of a function
f
, whereas the STFT with window
g
, i.e.,
V_{g}f
, is a linear transformation of
f
. Therefore,
f
can only be recovered up to a phase factor by
Af
. In particular we have
A(cf)=Af
for any
|c|=1
. Such a
c
is called a phase factor. If
f(0)\neq 0
, we have the following inversion formula

f(x)=\frac{1}{\,\overline{f(0)}\,}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Af(x,\omega)e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}\,d\omega.

(3.15)

This can be obtained as follows. First, we note that for fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
Af(x,.)
is the Fourier transform of
t\mapsto f(t+\tfrac{x}{2})\overline{f(t-\tfrac{x}{2})}
. Hence, by the Fourier inversion formula we have

f(t+\tfrac{x}{2})\overline{f(t-\tfrac{x}{2})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Af(x,\omega)e^{2\pi it\cdot\omega}\,d\omega.

(3.16)

By setting
t=\tfrac{x}{2}
, we obtain the desired result. Furthermore, it is easy to check that for a phase factor
|c|=1
, equation (
3.15
) holds for
cf
as well.

Of course, it is not quite “fair” to compare the inversion formula for the auto-ambiguity function with the inversion formula for the STFT. If we introduce an “auto-STFT”, i.e.,
V_{f}f
, then we can also only recover
f
from
V_{f}f
up to a phase factor. The key difference is that for the STFT, we completely know the window function
g
. Therefore, for the cross-ambiguity function, we may completely recover
f
from
A(f,g)
by knowing
g
.

The ambiguity function occurs naturally in radar applications and, therefore, is often called the radar ambiguity function. We will briefly discuss a simplified model as described in
[
19
, Chap. 4.2]
. Suppose that the distance and speed of an unidentified flying object (UFO) are to be determined. For this purpose a transmitter/receiver station sends a test signal
f
towards the UFO. The signal
f
is reflected and a part of the reflected signal is received as an echo
e
. The test signal might be a “pulse” of short duration of the form
f(x)=\sigma(x)e^{2\pi i\omega_{0}\cdot x}
., where
\sigma
is a nice envelope signal of slow variation. This means that
\supp(\widehat{\sigma})\subset[-W,W]
with
W
small compared to the carrier frequency
\omega_{0}
, so
\supp(\widehat{f})\subset[\omega_{0}-W,\omega_{0}+W]
. Let
r
be the distance between the transmitter/receiver station and the UFO,
v
the relative speed between the station and th UFO and
c
the speed of light. Then, the echo
e
is received with a time lag
\Delta t=\frac{2r}{c}
. Moreover, each frequency
\omega
in the band
[\omega_{0}-W,\omega_{0}+W]
of
f
undergoes a Doppler shift
\Delta\omega=-\frac{2\omega v}{c}
. Since the bandwidth
2W
is small compared to
\omega_{0}
, the frequency shifts can be approximated by
\Delta\omega\approx-\frac{2\omega_{0}v}{c}
, independently of the exact form of
f
. We ignore any further distortion of the signal
f
in this example, therefore the echo
e
has the form

M_{\Delta\omega}T_{\Delta t}f.

(3.17)

At the receiver, the echo is then compared to time-frequency shifts of the original signal
f
by taking the correlation

|\langle e,M_{\omega}T_{x}f\rangle|=|V_{f}f(x-\Delta t,\omega-\Delta\omega)|=|Af(x-\Delta t,\omega-\Delta\omega)|.

(3.18)

The values of
\Delta t
and
\Delta\omega
, and hence the distance
r
and the velocity
v
of the UFO, can be determined by means of the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
f\neq 0
, then

|Af(x,\omega)|<Af(0,0)=\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2},

(3.19)

for all
(x,\omega)\neq(0,0)
.

Proof.

The inequality

|Af(x,\omega)|=|\langle f,M_{\omega}T_{x}f\rangle|\leq\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}

(3.20)

is just the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Equality holds if and only if
M_{\omega}T_{x}f=cf
(i.e., we have linear dependence) for some
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and
|c|=1
. Now, assume
x\neq 0
and further assume that
T_{x}|f|=|cM_{\omega}T_{x}f|=|cf|=|f|
, then
|f|
would be periodic with period
x
. However, as
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it cannot be periodic unless
f=0
. If
x=0
and
\omega\neq 0
, then, analogously,
T_{\omega}|\widehat{f}|=|\widehat{f}|
would be
\omega
-periodic. Therefore,
|Af(x,\omega)|
is maximal if and only if
(x,\omega)=(0,0)
.

The value of
Af(0,0)
yields the energy of
f
, i.e.,

Af(0,0)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\overline{f(t)}\,dt=\langle f,f\rangle=\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(3.21)

∎

Now, to find the lag
(\Delta t,\Delta\omega)
, one determines experimentally the values
(x_{0},\omega_{0})
where the correlation function
|\langle e,M_{\omega}T_{x}f\rangle|
takes its maximum. According to the lemma above, the position of this maximum is the desired lag
(\Delta t,\Delta\omega)
.

We give now two examples of auto-ambiguity functions, which are used to measure time-frequency concentrations.

Example 3.4
.

(a)

The ambiguity function of the box function is given by

Ab_{0}(x,\omega)=\begin{cases}\frac{\sin(\pi\omega(1-|x|))}{\pi\omega},&|x|\leq 1\\
0,&\text{else}\end{cases}

(3.22)

Figure 7:
Illustration of the function
b_{0}(t+\tfrac{x}{2})b_{0}(t-\tfrac{x}{2})
for some
x
with
|x|\leq 1
.

We show this by a directly computing

Ab_{0}(x,\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}b_{0}(t+\tfrac{x}{2})b_{0}(t-\tfrac{x}{2})e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt.

(3.23)

We distinct the cases
x\in[0,1]
and
x\in[-1,0]
. We start with the case
x\in[0,1]
.

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}b_{0}(t+\tfrac{x}{2})b_{0}(t-\tfrac{x}{2})e^{-2\pi i\omega t}\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{\tfrac{-1+x}{2}}^{\tfrac{1-x}{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega t}\,dt

(3.24)

\displaystyle=\int_{\tfrac{-1+x}{2}}^{\tfrac{1-x}{2}}\cos(2\pi\omega t)\,dt=\frac{\sin(2\pi\omega t)}{2\pi\omega}\Bigg|_{t=\tfrac{-1+x}{2}}^{\tfrac{1-x}{2}}

(3.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(2\pi\omega\frac{1-x}{2})}{2\pi\omega}-\frac{\sin(2\pi\omega\frac{-1+x}{2})}{2\pi\omega}

(3.26)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\pi\omega(1-x))}{\pi\omega}.

(3.27)

The case
x\in[-1,0]
follows easily by the symmetry
Ab_{0}(x,\omega)=Ab_{0}(-x,\omega)
and the result follows.

(b)

Next, we compute the ambiguity function of the 1-dimensional standard Gaussian
g_{0}(t)=2^{1/4}e^{-\pi t^{2}}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
(the case for of the
d
-dimensional standard Gaussian follows just as easily.) As an in-between step, we will use the dilation operator
D_{a}g(t)=a^{-1/2}g(a^{-1}t)
and its behavior under the Fourier transform and Theorem
0.8
, which shows that
\mathcal{F}g_{0}=g_{0}
.

\displaystyle Ag_{0}(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=2^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\pi(t+\tfrac{x}{2})^{2}}e^{-\pi(t-\tfrac{x}{2})^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(3.28)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi\tfrac{x^{2}}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\underbrace{2^{1/2}e^{-2\pi t^{2}}}_{D_{1/\sqrt{2}}g_{0}}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(3.29)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi\tfrac{x^{2}}{2}}\mathcal{F}\left(D_{1/\sqrt{2}}g_{0}\right)(\omega)=e^{-\pi\tfrac{x^{2}}{2}}D_{\sqrt{2}}\mathcal{F}g_{0}(\omega)=e^{-\pi\tfrac{x^{2}}{2}}D_{\sqrt{2}}g_{0}(\omega)

(3.30)

\displaystyle=e^{-\tfrac{\pi}{2}(x^{2}+\omega^{2})}.

(3.31)

Note, that we also have the relation
A(f,g)(x,\omega)=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g}f(x,\omega)
. Hence, using the result from Example
2.3
that
V_{g_{0}}g_{0}(x,\omega)=e^{-\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(x^{2}+\omega^{2})}
we would have obtained the result without any further computations.

(a)

Density-plots of the absolute values of the auto-ambiguity functions of the box function
b_{0}
and the standard Gaussian
g_{0}
.

(b)

3d-plots of the absolute values of the auto-ambiguity functions of the box function
b_{0}
and the standard Gaussian
g_{0}
.

Figure 8:
Time-frequency concentration of the box-function
b_{0}
and the standard Gaussian
g_{0}
.

\diamond

Remark
.

We see that, for
x=0
, we obtain the Fourier transform of the box function

Ab_{0}(0,\omega)=\mathcal{F}b_{0}(\omega)=\sinc(\omega),

(3.32)

which demonstrates, again, that the frequency resolution of the box function is not suitable for measuring time-frequency content. On the time-side, the ambiguity function measures the auto-correlation of a signal. Since the box function is an even function (i.e.,
g^{\vee}=g
), for
\omega=0
, we obtain the convolution of the box function with itself by using l’Hospital’s rule

Ab_{0}(x,0)=\lim_{\omega\to 0}\frac{\sin(\pi\omega)(1-|x|)}{\pi\omega}=1-|x|.

(3.33)

For the second example, we see that for
x=0
, we obtain the auto-correlation of the Gaussian spectrum

Ag_{0}(0,\omega)=e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\omega^{2}},

(3.34)

which is a dilated Gaussian, and not the standard Gaussian. The same is true in the case
\omega=0
;

Ag_{0}(x,0)=e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}x^{2}}.

(3.35)

This is, as in the case of the box function, the convolution of the Gaussian with itself.

What causes the difference in the spectral behavior of the box function compared to the Gaussian, is the fact that
Ab_{0}(0,\omega)
does actually not give the Fourier transform of
b_{0}
, but the auto-correlation of the spectrum
\mathcal{F}b_{0}
. But, the auto-correlation corresponds to a convolution in this case. Now, the
\sinc
-function is invariant under convolution, as can easily be seen by the following computation;

(\sinc*\sinc)(\omega)=(\mathcal{F}b_{0}*\mathcal{F}b_{0})(\omega)=\mathcal{F}(\underbrace{b_{0}\,b_{0}}_{=b_{0}})(\omega)=\mathcal{F}b_{0}(\omega)=\sinc(\omega).

(3.36)

Note that, in general, we have

Af(0,\omega)=\mathcal{F}(f\,\overline{f})(\omega)=(\mathcal{F}f*\mathcal{F}\overline{f})(\omega)\quad\text{ and }\quad Af(x,0)=(f*\overline{f}^{\vee})(x).

(3.37)

\diamond

Since, we have the relation
A(f,g)(x,\omega)=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g}f(x,\omega)
, it is clear that the ambiguity functions enjoys similar properties as the STFT. In particular, the orthogonality relations and the Parseval-like identity hold;

\displaystyle\langle A(f_{1},g_{1}),A(f_{2},g_{2})\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle=\langle e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g_{1}}f_{1},e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g_{2}}f_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

(3.38)

\displaystyle=\langle V_{g_{1}}f_{1},V_{g_{2}}f_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}=\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle\overline{\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle}.

(3.39)

Therefore, we also have

\lVert A(f,g)\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}=\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}.

(3.40)

As the ambiguity function has a symmetry in the time-frequency shifts,
A(f,g)(x,\omega)=\langle\pi(-\frac{\lambda}{2})f,\pi(\frac{\lambda}{2})g\rangle
, the analogue to Proposition
2.5
(Fundamental identity of TFA) becomes more symmetric as well;

\displaystyle A(f,g)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g}f(x,\omega)=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{\widehat{g}}\widehat{f}(\omega,-x)

(3.41)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{\widehat{g}}\widehat{f}(\omega,-x)=A(\widehat{f},\widehat{g})(\omega,-x).

(3.42)

Lastly, we discuss a covariance result for the ambiguity function. For
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
x,\omega,t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\,A(M_{\eta_{1}}T_{\xi_{1}}f,M_{\eta_{2}}T_{\xi_{2}}g)(x,\omega)

(3.43)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(M_{\eta_{1}}T_{\xi_{1}}f)(t+\tfrac{x}{2})(M_{\eta_{2}}T_{\xi_{2}}f)(t-\tfrac{x}{2})e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(3.44)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t+\tfrac{x}{2}-\xi_{1})\overline{g(t-\tfrac{x}{2}-\xi_{2})}\,e^{2\pi i\eta_{1}\cdot(t+\frac{x}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\eta_{2}\cdot(t-\frac{x}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt\quad\begin{subarray}{c}\phantom{=}\end{subarray}_{(t\mapsto t+\tfrac{\xi_{1}}{2}+\tfrac{\xi_{2}}{2})}

(3.45)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi ix\cdot(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t+\tfrac{x-\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2})\overline{g(t-\tfrac{x-\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2})}\,e^{-2\pi i(\omega-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})\cdot(t+\frac{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2})}\,dt

(3.46)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi ix\cdot(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})}e^{-\pi i\omega\cdot(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}e^{\pi i(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})\cdot(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t+\tfrac{x-\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2})\overline{g(t-\tfrac{x-\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2})}\,e^{-2\pi i(\omega-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})\cdot t}\,dt

(3.47)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi ix\cdot(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})}e^{-\pi i\omega\cdot(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}e^{\pi i(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})\cdot(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}A(f,g)(x-\xi_{1}+\xi_{2},\omega-\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}).

(3.48)

In particular, for
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=\xi
and
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}=\eta
we obtain

A(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f,M_{\eta}T_{\xi}g)(x,\omega)=e^{2\pi i(x\cdot\eta-\omega\cdot\xi)}A(f,g)(x,\omega).

(3.49)

This shows that shifting, both,
f
and
g
in the time-frequency plane, only yields a phase factor.

3.4
The Wigner Distribution

The Wigner distribution was “invented” by Eugene Wigner in 1932
[
53
]
in the context of quantum mechanics. We quote from the introduction in
[
17
]
:

In this paper
[
[
53
]
; author’s note]
, Wigner introduced a probability quasi-distribution that allowed him to express quantum mechanical expectation values in the same form as the averages of classical statistical mechanics. There have been many speculations about how Wigner got his idea; his eponymous transform seems to be pulled out of thin air.

We will now study some of the properties of this almost mysterious transform, including Hudson’s theorem, which will essentially take us all the way to very recent mathematical research. Besides the textbook of Gröchenig
[
19
]
, the book by de Gosson
[
17
]
may be consulted as a further reference and for more information on the Wigner transform.

Definition 3.5
.

The Wigner distribution of a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is given by

Wf(x,\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x+\tfrac{t}{2})\overline{f(x-\tfrac{t}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt.

(3.50)

The cross-Wigner transform of two functions
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is given by

W(f,g)(x,\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x+\tfrac{t}{2})\overline{g(x-\tfrac{t}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt.

(3.51)

We quote from the introduction in
[
17
]
:

Truly, Wigner’s definition, in modern notation did not remind of anything one had seen before; a rapid glance suggests it is something of a mixture of a Fourier transform and a convolution. And, yet it worked! For instance, under some mild assumptions on the function
\psi

[which may be a quantum state or wave function; author’s note]
we recover the probability amplitudes
|\psi(x)|^{2}
and
|\phi(p)|^{2}

[
\phi=\widehat{\psi}
, author’s note]
of quantum mechanics:

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}W\psi(x,p)\,dp=|\psi(x)|^{2},

(3.52)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}W\psi(x,p)\,dx=|\phi(p)|^{2};

(3.53)

assuming
\psi
normalized to unity and integrating any of these equalities, we get

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}W\psi(x,p)\,dp\,dx=1,

(3.54)

so that the Wigner transform
W\psi
can be used as a mock probability distribution.

The fact that we (can) only call the Wigner transform a quasi-distribution stems from the fact that it fails to be non-negative in general. The only exceptions come from Gaussian functions, which is Hudson’s Theorem. Before we prove that
Wf
is a quasi-distribution, we need to describe the connections to the other time-frequency representations.

We recall that the reflection of a function
g
is denoted by
g^{\vee}
;

g^{\vee}(t)=g(-t),\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(3.55)

The following lemma shows the algebraic connection between
V_{g}f
(and hence
A(f,g)
) and
W(f,g)
.

Lemma 3.6

(Algebraic Relations)
.

For
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

W(f,g)(x,\omega)=2^{d}e^{4\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g^{\vee}}f(2x,2\omega)=2^{d}A(f,g^{\vee})(2x,2\omega).

(3.56)

Proof.

We will use the substitution
u=x+\tfrac{t}{2}
in the definition of
W(f,g)
and compute

\displaystyle W(f,g)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x+\tfrac{t}{2})\overline{g(x-\tfrac{t}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(3.57)

\displaystyle=2^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(u)\overline{g(-(u-2x)}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot(2u-2x)}\,du

(3.58)

\displaystyle=2^{d}e^{4\pi ix\cdot\omega}\langle f,M_{2\omega}T_{2x}g^{\vee}\rangle

(3.59)

\displaystyle=2^{d}e^{4\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g^{\vee}}f(2x,2\omega).

(3.60)

∎

We will now collect properties of the Wigner distribution from the STFT. Some of these properties readily follow by using Lemma
3.6
.

Proposition 3.7
.

For
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the cross-Wigner distribution has the following properties.

(i)

W(f,g)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\cap C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, in particular
W(f,g)
is uniformly continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and is bounded by

\lVert W(f,g)\rVert_{\infty}\leq 2^{d}\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}.

(3.61)

(ii)

W(f,g)=\overline{W(g,f)}
. In particular,
Wf
is real-valued.

(iii)

For
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\,W(M_{\eta_{1}}T_{\xi_{1}}f,\,M_{\eta_{2}}T_{\xi_{2}}g)

(3.62)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,e^{\pi i(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})\cdot(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})}e^{2\pi ix\cdot(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})}\,W(f,g)(x-\tfrac{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2},\omega-\tfrac{\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}}{2}).

(3.63)

In particular,
Wf
is covariant, i.e.,

W(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f)(x,\omega)=Wf(x-\xi,\omega-\eta).

(3.64)

(iv)

W(\widehat{f},\widehat{g})(x,\omega)=W(f,g)(-\omega,x)

(v)

Also, we have the Parseval-like identity, which goes under the name Moyal’s formula;

\langle W(f_{1},g_{1}),W(f_{2},g_{2})\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}=\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle\overline{\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle}.

(3.65)

Proof.

The proofs follow mainly by direct computation.

(i)

The assertion that
W(f,g)
is uniformly continuous and decaying to 0 follows from the respective properties of
V_{g}f
by using Lemma
3.6
. To show the
L^{\infty}
-bound we compute

\displaystyle|W(g,f)(x,\omega)|

\displaystyle=2^{d}|V_{g^{\vee}}f(2x,2\omega)|=2^{d}|\langle f,M_{2\omega}T_{2x}g^{\vee}\rangle|

(3.66)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{d}\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert M_{2\omega}T_{2x}g^{\vee}\rVert_{2}=2^{d}\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}.

(3.67)

In the above calculations we used, again, Lemma
3.6
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Also we used that
M_{\omega}
and
T_{x}
are unitary and that flipping
g
, i.e.,
g\mapsto g^{\vee}
, is unitary as well.

(ii)

By using the substitution
t\mapsto-t
, we compute

\displaystyle W(f,g)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x+\tfrac{t}{2})\overline{g(x-\tfrac{t}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(3.68)

\displaystyle=\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\overline{f(x-\tfrac{t}{2})}g(x+\tfrac{t}{2})e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt}

(3.69)

\displaystyle=\overline{W(g,f)(x,\omega)}.

(3.70)

(iii)

We compute

\displaystyle\,W(M_{\eta_{1}}T_{\xi_{1}}f,M_{\eta_{2}}T_{\xi_{2}}g)(x,\omega)

(3.71)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(M_{\eta_{1}}T_{\xi_{1}}f)(x+\tfrac{t}{2})\overline{(M_{\eta_{2}}T_{\xi_{2}}g)(x-\tfrac{t}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(3.72)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x-\xi_{1}+\tfrac{t}{2})\overline{g(x-\xi_{2}-\tfrac{t}{2})}e^{2\pi i\eta_{1}\cdot(x+\frac{t}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\eta_{2}\cdot(x-\frac{t}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt\quad\begin{subarray}{c}\phantom{=}\end{subarray}_{(t\mapsto t+\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})}

(3.73)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi ix\cdot(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x-\tfrac{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2}+\tfrac{t}{2})\overline{g(x-\tfrac{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2}-\tfrac{t}{2})}e^{-2\pi i(\omega-\frac{\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}}{2})\cdot(t+\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})}\,dt

(3.74)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi ix\cdot(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x-\tfrac{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2}+\tfrac{t}{2})\overline{g(x-\tfrac{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2}-\tfrac{t}{2})}e^{-2\pi i(\omega-\frac{\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}}{2})\cdot t}\,dt

(3.75)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi ix\cdot(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})}e^{\pi i(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})}W(f,g)(x-\tfrac{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2},\omega-\tfrac{\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}}{2}).

(3.76)

(iv)

We note that
\widehat{g^{\vee}}=\widehat{g}^{\vee}
. We compute

\displaystyle W(\widehat{f},\widehat{g})(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=2^{d}e^{4\pi ix\cdot\omega}\langle\widehat{f},M_{2\omega}T_{2x}\widehat{g}^{\vee}\rangle

(3.77)

\displaystyle=2^{d}e^{4\pi ix\cdot\omega}\langle f,T_{-2\omega}M_{2x}g^{\vee}\rangle

(3.78)

\displaystyle=2^{d}e^{-4\pi ix\cdot\omega}\langle f,M_{2x}T_{-2\omega}g^{\vee}\rangle

(3.79)

\displaystyle=W(f,g)(-\omega,x).

(3.80)

In the above computations we used Lemma
3.6
, Parseval’s formula (
0.21
) and the commutation relations (
1.3
).

(v)

We compute

\displaystyle\,\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}W(f_{1},g_{1})(x,\omega)\overline{W(f_{2},g_{2})(x,\omega)}\,dxd\omega

(3.81)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,2^{2d}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g_{1}^{\vee}}f_{1}(2x,2\omega)\overline{V_{g_{2}^{\vee}}f_{2}(2x,2\omega)}\,dxd\omega

(3.82)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle\overline{\langle g^{\vee}_{1},g^{\vee}_{2}\rangle}

(3.83)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle\overline{\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle}

(3.84)

When changing from the integral formulas to the inner products, we made the substitution
(2x,2\omega)\mapsto(x,\omega)
, which is why the factor
2^{2d}
disappeared. Also, we used the orthogonality relations (
2.27
) of the STFT.

∎

Before we proceed, we introduce some more notation. By
\mathcal{T}_{s}
we denote the symmetric coordinate change defined by

\mathcal{T}_{s}F(x,t)=F(x+\tfrac{t}{2},x-\tfrac{t}{2}).

(3.85)

We also note that its inverse is given by

\mathcal{T}_{s}^{-1}F(x,t)=F(\tfrac{x+t}{2},x-t).

(3.86)

Furthermore, we re-call the notion of the partial Fourier transforms
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, which transform a function of two arguments only in the first and the second argument, respectively.

\mathcal{F}_{1}F(x,\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(t,\omega)e^{-2\pi ix\cdot t}\,dt.\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathcal{F}_{2}F(x,\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(x,t)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt.

(3.87)
