Using these (unitary) operators, the cross-Wigner distribution can be written as

W(f,g)(x,\omega)=\mathcal{F}_{2}\left(\mathcal{T}_{s}(f\otimes\overline{g})(x,t)\right)(x,\omega),

(3.88)

where
(f\otimes g)(x,\omega)=f(x)g(\omega)
is the already familiar tensor product. From this factorization, we easily obtain an inversion formula for the (auto-)Wigner distribution.

f(x)\overline{f(y)}=\left(\mathcal{T}_{s}^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{2}^{-1}Wf\right)(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Wf\left(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\omega\right)e^{2\pi i(x-y)\cdot\omega}\,d\omega.

(3.89)

Now, if
f(0)\neq 0
, then

f(x)=\frac{1}{\overline{f(0)}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Wf\left(\tfrac{x}{2},\omega\right)e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}\,d\omega.

(3.90)

Of course, this inversion formula is true only up to a global phase factor
|c|=1
.

The next lemma is highly important and reveals a deep connection between the ambiguity function and the Wigner distribution via the Fourier transform.

Lemma 3.8
.

For
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

W(f,g)(x,\omega)=\mathcal{F}A(f,g)(\omega,-x)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad A(f,g)(x,\omega)=\mathcal{F}W(f,g)(\omega,-x)

(3.91)

Proof.

We note that the Fourier transform on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
factors into the partial Fourier transforms on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{1}\mathcal{F}_{2}=\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}
. By using (
3.88
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}W(f,g)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{T}_{s}(f\otimes\overline{g})(x,\omega)

(3.92)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{T}_{s}(f\otimes\overline{g})(t,\omega)e^{2\pi it\cdot x}\,dt

(3.93)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t+\tfrac{\omega}{2})\overline{g(t-\tfrac{\omega}{2})}e^{2\pi ix\cdot t}\,dt

(3.94)

\displaystyle=A(f,g)(\omega,-x).

(3.95)

This proves the first assertion. The second one follows analogously.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}W(f,g)(\omega,-x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{1}\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{s}(f\otimes\overline{g})(\omega,x)

(3.96)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{T}_{s}(f\otimes\overline{g})(t,x)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(3.97)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t+\tfrac{x}{2})\overline{f(t-\tfrac{x}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(3.98)

\displaystyle=A(f,g)(x,\omega).

(3.99)

The computations certainly hold for Schwartz functions (or on the dense subspace
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
) and the formulas extend to all
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by continuity of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{T}_{s}
.
∎

After this discussion on the connection with other time-frequency representations, we will discuss the support properties of the Wigner distribution. To simplify notation, we are only going to discuss this in the case
d=1
(hence, for intervals and not for
d
-dimensional hyper cubes).

Lemma 3.9
.

For
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, if
\supp(f)\subset[a,b]=\mathcal{Q}_{[a,b]}
, then
Wf(x,\omega)=0
for
x\notin\mathcal{Q}_{[a,b]}
. Likewise, if
\supp(\widehat{f})\subset\mathcal{Q}_{[c,d]}
, then
Wf(x,\omega)=0
for
\omega\notin\mathcal{Q}_{[c,d]}
.

Proof.

Wf(x,\omega)\neq 0
is possible only if
x+\frac{t}{2}\in\supp(f)
and
x-\frac{t}{2}\in\supp(f)
. Then
x=\frac{1}{2}(x+\frac{t}{2})+\frac{1}{2}(x-\frac{t}{2})\in\mathcal{Q}_{[a,b]}
. Thus
Wf(x,\omega)=0
for
x\notin\mathcal{Q}_{[a,b]}
.

The second statement follows from the relation
Wf(x,\omega)=W\widehat{f}(\omega,-x)
.
∎

So, in contrast to the ambiguity function (and the STFT), the Wigner transform preserves the support properties of
f
and
\widehat{f}
exactly (so does the Rihaczek distribution). Also, note that the auto-Wigner distribution is taken as a joint (quasi-) probability distribution in phase space. The interpretation of the STFT usually depends on the auxiliary window and the “sliding” of the window
g
spreads out the support of
f
.

The next result is at the origin of interest in quantum mechanics and shows that the Wigner distribution yields the correct marginal densities, which then allow to interpret the Wigner distribution as a joint (quasi-) probability density function.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
f,\widehat{f}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Wf(x,\omega)\,d\omega=|f(x)|^{2},

(3.100)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Wf(x,\omega)\,dx=|\widehat{f}(\omega)|^{2}.

(3.101)

In particular, if
\lVert f\rVert_{2}=\lVert\widehat{f}\rVert_{2}=1
, then

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}Wf(x,\omega)\,d(x,\omega)=1.

(3.102)

Proof.

We use the algebraic relation
3.6
between the Wigner transform and the STFT and write the STFT as a convolution of Fourier transforms (see (
2.11
));

|Wf(x,\omega)|=2^{d}|V_{f^{\vee}}f(2x,2\omega)|=2^{d}|\widehat{f}*M_{-2x}\widehat{\overline{f}}^{\vee}(2\omega)|.

(3.103)

By Young’s Theorem
0.7
we have the convolution inequality (
0.29
) which tells us that the convolution of two
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-functions is again an
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-function. Hence, under the Assumption that
\widehat{f}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and (
3.103
) we see that, for any fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
Wf(x,\omega)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},d\omega)
. Therefore, the Fourier inversion formula for the partial Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{2}
is applicable and yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Wf(x,\omega)\,d\omega

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{F}_{2}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{T}_{s}(f\otimes\overline{f})\right)\right)(x,0)=\mathcal{T}_{s}(f\otimes\widehat{f})(x,0)=|f(x)|^{2}.

(3.104)

Now, using Proposition
3.7
(iv) we compute

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Wf(x,\omega)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}W\widehat{f}(\omega,-x)\,dx=|\widehat{f}(\omega)|^{2}.

(3.105)

∎

In the language of quantum mechanics, this means that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Wf(x,\omega)\,d\omega
yields the probability density
|f(x)|^{2}
for the position variable and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}WF(x,\omega)\,dx
yields the density
|\widehat{f}|^{2}
for the momentum variable.

Also, we remark that, for a suitable subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
Wf\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
(which allows us to use Fubini’s theorem), we have

\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Wf(x,\omega)\,dx\,d\omega=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Wf(x,\omega)\,d\omega\,dx=\lVert\widehat{f}\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(3.106)

Thus, Lemma
3.10
implies Plancherel’s theorem (on this subspace, which is actually dense).

We will now discuss the positivity of the Wigner distribution and, hence, whether it can serve as an energy density or joint probability density. We mention right away that, in general the Wigner distribution fails to be positive. For example, let
f
be an odd function, i.e.,
f(t)=-f^{\vee}(t)=-f(-t)
, then

Wf(0,0)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\tfrac{t}{2})\overline{f(-\tfrac{t}{2})}\,dt=-2^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\overline{f^{\vee}(-t)}\,dt=-2^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(t)|^{2}\,dt=-2^{d}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}<0.

(3.107)

Negative values cannot be interpreted as a probability or as an energy density. However, a single point in the time-frequency plane or phase space also has no physical meaning. Now, the hope is that, maybe there is a “nice” subspace of “physically realizable” functions for which the Wigner distribution is positive? The answer to this question is given by Hudson’s theorem
[
29
]
.

Theorem 3.11

(Hudson)
.

Assume that
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
Wf(x,\omega)>0
for all
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
if and only if
f
is a generalized Gaussian, i.e.,

f(t)=e^{-\pi t\cdot At+2\pi b\cdot t+c},

(3.108)

where
A\in GL(\mathbb{C},d)
is an invertible
d\times d
matrix with entries from
\mathbb{C}
and positive definite real part (
\Re(A)>0
) and with
\overline{A}^{T}=A^{*}=A
,
b\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
and
c\in\mathbb{C}
.

We will postpone the proof to a later point as we need more preparation for the proof.

A bit more generally, Theorem
3.11
can also be stated as follows
[
24
]
,
[
40
]
.

Theorem 3.12

(Hudson-Lieb)
.

Let
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(non-zero). Then
W(f,g)\geq 0
for all
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
if and only if
f=K\,g
,
K>0
and
g
is a generalized Gaussian of the form (
3.108
). In this case,
W(f,g)(x,\omega)>0
for all
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

We note that
W(f,g)
is not necessarily real-valued. However, if it is non-negative and real-valued, then
f
and
g
are the same generalized Gaussian up to a positive constant and the cross-Wigner distribution is essentially an auto-Wigner distribution and actually positive everywhere. Hudson’s original theorem dates back to 1974 and the extension of Lieb was established in 1990.

A related and hard problem, which still remains open in full generality, is to characterize pairs of functions
f
and
g
for which
W(f,g)(x,\omega)\neq 0
for all
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. Only very recently examples of pairs
(f,g)
, which are not Gaussians and yield zero-free Wigner distributions, or likewise zero-free STFTs and ambiguity functions, have been found and described by Gröchenig, Jaming and Malinnikova in their article
[
24
]
published in 2020. The prototype example they give is obtained from the one-sided exponential.

Example 3.13
.

Let
f
be a one-sided exponential, i.e.,

\displaystyle f(t)=\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(t)e^{-\pi t}=\begin{cases}e^{-\pi t},&t>0\\
0,&else\end{cases}.

(3.109)

Figure 9:
The one-sided exponential function.

Assume
x\geq 0
, then the ambiguity function is given by

\displaystyle Af(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(t-\tfrac{x}{2})\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(t+\tfrac{x}{2})e^{-\pi(-\frac{x}{2})}e^{-\pi(t+\frac{x}{2})}e^{-2\pi i\omega t}\,dt

(3.110)

\displaystyle=\int_{\frac{x}{2}}^{\infty}e^{-2\pi t}e^{-2\pi i\omega t}\,dt=\int_{\frac{x}{2}}^{\infty}e^{-2\pi i(\omega-i)t}\,dt=\frac{e^{-2\pi i(\omega-i)t}}{-2\pi i(\omega-i)}\Bigg|_{t=\frac{x}{2}}^{\infty}

(3.111)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\pi i(\omega-i)x}}{2\pi i(\omega-i)}.

(3.112)

The part for
x<0
follows analogously and we get

Af(x,\omega)=\frac{e^{-\pi i(\omega-i)|x|}}{2\pi i(\omega-i)}.

(3.113)

In particular,
Af(x,\omega)\neq 0
for all
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. It follows immediately that
W(f,f^{\vee})\neq 0
by the algebraic relation in Lemma
3.6
.

Figure 10:
3d plot and density plot of the absolute values of the ambiguity function of the 1-sided exponential. While it is well-concentrated in the time domain, the localization in the frequency domain is not satisfying, as the decay is only
\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\omega})
. This is caused by the discontinuity of the complex exponential (as smoothness on the time-side corresponds to fast decay in the frequency domain).

On the other hand, it follows from the Hudson-Lieb Theorem
3.12
that if
f=\pm f^{\vee}
, i.e.,
f
is either an odd or even function, then
A(f,f^{\vee})=\pm Af
can only be positive if
f
is a generalized Gaussian.

•

If
f=f^{\vee}
, i.e.,
f
is even, then
W(f,f^{\vee})=Wf
. Also,
Wf
is real-valued and
Wf(0,0)=2^{d}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}
. By Hudson’s theorem the Wigner transform is only positive for Gaussians. Therefore, in this case the Wigner distribution must possess zeros if
f
is not a generalized Gaussian. Now, by the algebraic relation given by Lemma
3.6
the same holds for the ambiguity function
Af
.

•

If
f=-f^{\vee}
, i.e.,
f
is odd, then
W(f,f^{\vee})=-Wf
and,
W(f,f^{\vee})(0,0)=2^{d}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}
. Therefore, as
Wf
is real-valued, the Wigner distribution
W(f,f^{\vee})
is real-valued and by the Hudson-Lieb theorem either possesses zeros or
f
is a generalized Gaussian. Again, by Lemma
3.6
the same is true for the ambiguity function
Af
.

This shows that if the ambiguity function
Af
and cross Wigner distribution
W(f,f^{\vee})
are zero-free, then
f
does naturally not possess “simple” symmetries, meaning that it cannot be an even or an odd function, unless it is a Gaussian.

\diamond

Curiously, this prototype example was (almost) already given by Augustus Janssen more than 20 years earlier in 1996. In
[
33
]
, Janssen already computed the ambiguity function of the one-sided exponential (evaluated only at integer points, i.e.,
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
). However, it was not noticed that a zero-free ambiguity function was already given in
[
33
]
until the appearance of
[
24
]
. The authors re-discovered this example independently as the one-sided exponential is also a prototype example of so-called totally positive functions of finite type. These functions have attracted considerable attraction in recent years in Gabor analysis and we will encounter them later on as well.

Example 3.14
.

Next, we will compute the Wigner distribution of the point measure or delta distribution
\delta_{0}
, where
\langle\delta_{x},f\rangle=\overline{f(x)}
. For
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, by using (
3.88
), we have

\displaystyle\langle W(\delta_{0},\delta_{0}),f\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{T}_{s}(\delta_{0}\otimes\overline{\delta_{0}}),f\rangle

(3.114)

\displaystyle=\langle\delta_{0}\otimes\overline{\delta_{0}},\mathcal{T}_{s}^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{2}^{-1}f\rangle

(3.115)

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{T}_{s}^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{2}^{-1}f(0,0)}

(3.116)

Using the formula (
3.86
) for
\mathcal{T}_{s}^{-1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{T}_{s}^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{2}^{-1}f(0,0)}

\displaystyle=\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\left(\tfrac{x+y}{2},\omega\right)e^{2\pi i(x-y)\cdot\omega}\,d\omega}\ \Bigg|_{x=y=0}

(3.117)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\overline{f(0,\omega)}\,d\omega

(3.118)

\displaystyle=\langle\delta_{0}\otimes\mathbf{1},f\rangle,

(3.119)

where
\mathbf{1}
is the constant 1-function. Therefore,
W\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}\otimes\mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

\diamond

4
The Poisson Summation Formula

The Poisson summation formula is a highly useful tool in time-frequency analysis, but also in many other branches of mathematics. It relates the periodization of a function
f
to a Fourier series on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with Fourier coefficients obtained from the Fourier transform on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Definition 4.1
.

Given a function
f
, its
\alpha
-periodization is defined by

\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}f(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(t+\alpha k),\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\alpha>0.

(4.1)

We note that if
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. If
\alpha=1
, we will omit the index and write

\mathcal{P}f(t)=\mathcal{P}_{1}f(t).

(4.2)

Also, we have the following property.

Lemma 4.2
.

If
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then for all
\alpha>0
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\,dt=\int_{[0,\alpha]^{d}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}f(t)\right)\,dt.

(4.3)

Proof.

We note that the cubes
\alpha k+[0,\alpha]^{d}
form a (disjoint) partition of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(up to an overlap of measure 0).
We compute

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{\alpha k+[0,\alpha]^{d}}f(t)\,dt

(4.4)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,\alpha]^{d}}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(t+\alpha k)\right)\,dt.

(4.5)

As
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the exchange of summation and integration is justified by Fubini’s theorem.
∎

We are now ready to prove the Poisson summation formula in its standard version.

Proposition 4.3

(Poisson Summation Formula)
.

Let
f
be continuous and
\varepsilon>0
,
C>0
. Assume that
|f(t)|\leq C(1+|t|)^{-d-\varepsilon}
and
|\widehat{f}(\omega)|\leq C(1+|\omega|)^{-d-\varepsilon}
. Then

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(t+k)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\widehat{f}(l)e^{2\pi il\cdot t}.

(4.6)

The above identity holds point-wise for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and both sums converge absolutely for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

The decay conditions imply that
f
as well as
\widehat{f}
are in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and by assumption (and the Lemma of Riemann-Lebesgue) both are continuous. As
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(t+k)=\mathcal{P}f(t)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. We want to check the Fourier coefficients of
\mathcal{P}f
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(\mathcal{P}f)(l)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{P}f(t)e^{-2\pi il\cdot t}\,dt

(4.7)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(t+k)e^{-2\pi il\cdot(t+k)}\right)\,dt

(4.8)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)e^{-2\pi il\cdot t}\,dt=\mathcal{F}f(l)=\widehat{f}(l).

(4.9)

By assumption
\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\widehat{f}(l)|<\infty
, we see that
\mathcal{P}f
has an absolutely converging Fourier series

\mathcal{P}f(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(t+k)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\widehat{f}(l)e^{2\pi il\cdot t}.

(4.10)

∎

Remark
.

(i)

The conditions in Proposition
4.3
are stronger than needed for the point-wise equality to hold, but they ensure absolute convergence of both series as well as the point-wise equality. The conditions on
f
and
\widehat{f}
under which the formula holds point-wise have been worked out, e.g., by Gröchenig in
[
18
]
.

(ii)

Note that we assume that
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which, by the Lemma of Riemann-Lebesgue, implies that
\widehat{f}
is continuous. On the Fourier side, we also assume that
\widehat{f}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which also implies that
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\widehat{f}
is continuous. Many authors hence drop the condition on
f
being continuous as they implicitly assume that
f(t)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\widehat{f}(t)
.

(iii)

If we replace the absolute convergence in Proposition
4.3
by convergence in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and pointwise equality by equality almost everywhere, we obtain a weaker version of the Poisson summation formula.

If
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(t+k)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\widehat{f}(k)|^{2}<\infty
, then (
4.6
) holds almost everywhere.

(iv)

The Poisson summation formula can also be written for arbitrary lattices
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and their duals. For a lattice
\Lambda=A\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
A\in GL(\mathbb{R},d)
, the dual lattice is given by
\Lambda^{\perp}=A^{-T}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and the Poisson summation formula reads

\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}f(t+\lambda)=\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\lambda^{\perp}\in\Lambda^{\perp}}\widehat{f}(\lambda^{\perp})e^{2\pi i\lambda^{\perp}\cdot t}

(4.11)

(v)

In analytic number theory the Poisson summation formula yields the following functional equation for the Jacobi theta function
\theta(\tau)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\pi i\tau k^{2}}
,
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
;

\theta(\tau)=\sqrt{\tfrac{i}{\tau}}\ \theta(-\tfrac{1}{\tau})

(4.12)

This functional equation was already used by Bernhard Riemann to come up with the functional equation for the Riemann zeta function
\zeta(s)
.

Using Proposition
4.3
for the function
M_{\omega}T_{x}f(t)
at
t=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(k-x)e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}M_{\omega}T_{x}f(k)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathcal{F}(M_{\omega}T_{x}f)(l)

(4.13)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot x}M_{-x}T_{\omega}\mathcal{F}f(l)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\widehat{f}(l-\omega)e^{2\pi ix\cdot(\omega-l)},

(4.14)

or, likewise, by exchanging the order of translation and modulation we obtain

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(k-x)e^{2\pi i\omega(k-x)}=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\widehat{f}(l-\omega)e^{-2\pi ix\cdot l}.

(4.15)

4.1
The Whittaker-Nyquist-Kotelnikov-Shannon Sampling Theorem

We are now going to state and prove a sampling theorem for band-limited functions. The information theoretical problem is as follows. Assuming that a continuous signal is band-limited, how many discrete measurements does one need in order to be able to completely determine the signal by its samples?

The solution to this question is often (only) referred to as the Nyquist-Shannon Sampling Theorem. However, it is also known as the Whittaker-Nyquist-Kotelnikov-Shannon (WNKS) Sampling Theorem
4
4

4

Often, only the contributions of the information theorists H. Nyquist (1928) and C. Shannon (1949) are mentioned, but the mathematical foundations were known already decades before to E. Whittaker (around 1915). Probably the problem had been treated mathematically already even earlier. For a long time the work of V. Kotelnikov from 1933 was not known outside Russia and it became more prominent only in the 1950s, after Shannon’s work. Kotelinkov’s work was probably the first work which treated the problem of sampling a continuous function in an information theoretical context
[
39
]
(see also
[
55
, Chap. 2]
)
.

Definition 4.4
.

A function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is called band-limited with band limit
\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2}
and band width
\mathsf{B}
if its Fourier transform
\widehat{f}
is supported on the interval
[-\tfrac{\mathsf{B}}{2},\tfrac{\mathsf{B}}{2}]
, i.e.,
\widehat{f}(\omega)=0
for all
|\omega|>\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2}
.

We will now state and prove the sampling theorem for the case
\mathsf{B}=1
, but the general case follows in the same manner.

Theorem 4.5

(WNKS Sampling Theorem)
.

Let
f
be a band-limited function with band limit
\frac{1}{2}
. In particular

f(t)=\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}\widehat{f}(\omega)e^{2\pi it\cdot\omega}\,d\omega,

(4.16)

with
\widehat{f}\in L^{2}([-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}])\left(\subset L^{1}([-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]\right)
and, hence,
f
is also continuous. Then
f
can be reconstructed from its samples on
\mathbb{Z}
by the formula

f(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f(k)\frac{\sin(\pi(t-k))}{\pi(t-k)},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

(4.17)

The series converges absolutely and uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Note that
\widehat{f}
coincides with its periodization on
[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
, i.e.,

\widehat{f}(\omega)=(\mathcal{P}\widehat{f}(\omega))\,\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(\omega)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{f}(\omega+l)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(\omega).

(4.18)

We apply the Poisson summation formula (in reverse) to obtain

\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{f}(\omega+l)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f(k)e^{-2\pi ik\omega}.

(4.19)

Next, we use the fact that
f
is the inverse Fourier transform of
\widehat{f}
to apply the Poisson summation formula;

\displaystyle f(t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widehat{f})(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{f}(\omega)e^{2\pi i\omega t}\,d\omega=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{f}(\omega+l)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(\omega)e^{2\pi i\omega t}\,dt

(4.20)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f(k)e^{-2\pi ik\omega}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(\omega)e^{2\pi it\omega}\,d\omega=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f(k)\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(\omega)e^{2\pi i(t-k)\omega}\,d\omega}_{=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}\right)(t-k)}

(4.21)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f(k)\frac{\sin(\pi(t-k))}{\pi(t-k)}

(4.22)

Our arguments all work in the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-sense, however, by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (for the space
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
) to our statement, we actually see that the convergence is absolutely and uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.
∎

The value
\frac{1}{2}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{B}=1
was of course chosen on purpose to obtain a statement and a proof in a simple form. More generally, if
\widehat{f}
is supported on the interval
[-\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2},\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2}]
, then

\displaystyle f(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f(\tfrac{k}{\mathsf{B}})\sinc(\mathsf{B}(t-\tfrac{k}{\mathsf{B}}))

(4.23)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f(\mathsf{T}k)\sinc\left(\frac{t-\mathsf{T}k}{\mathsf{T}}\right),

(4.24)

where we set
\mathsf{T}=\frac{1}{\mathsf{B}}
. The value of
\mathsf{T}
then gives the time between two successive samples. The rate
\frac{1}{\mathsf{T}}
is the sampling rate and in the previous case is exactly
\mathsf{B}
, which is known as the Nyquist rate. This is the minimum rate at which we need to sample in order to reconstruct
f
completely (see, e.g.,
[
55
, Chap. 2]
) by means of a sampling function, in this case the
\sinc
function. In general, assuming a band-limited signal with band limit
\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2}
, we distinguish 3 cases:

(i)

\mathsf{T}>\frac{1}{\mathsf{B}}
: undersampling, no guaranteed reconstruction

(ii)

\mathsf{T}=\frac{1}{\mathsf{B}}
: critical sampling, reconstruction is possible with the WKNS sampling formula, but losing 1 sample leads to undersampling

(iii)

\mathsf{T}<\frac{1}{\mathsf{B}}
: oversampling, reconstruction is possible, but the samples are linearly dependent. Note that the reconstruction formula
f(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f(\mathsf{T}k)\sinc\left(\frac{t-\mathsf{T}k}{\mathsf{T}}\right)
still holds, because we extended the frequency-band
[-\frac{1}{2\mathsf{T}},\frac{1}{2\mathsf{T}}]\supset[-\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2},\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2}]
. Due to the linear dependence of the samples it is still possible to reconstruct if (some) samples are lost.

(a)

Undersampling of
\sinc(t)^{2}
. The sampling rate is only half the Nyquist rate, hence we can only recover
\sinc(t)
, but not
\sinc(t)^{2}
. Note that
\sinc(t)
and
\sinc(t)^{2}
coincide at the sampling points
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(b)

Reconstruction of
\sinc(t)^{2}
using the WKNS sampling theorem.

(c)

Reconstruction of
\sinc(t)^{2}
at the Nyquist rate with the lost sample
f(0)
.

(d)

Reconstruction of
\sinc(t)^{2}
at the Nyquist rate with the lost sample
f(\tfrac{1}{2})
.

Figure 11:
The function
\sinc(t)^{2}
(gray, dashed) and some un-/successful reconstructions (black, solid) using the WKNS sampling theorem. Note that
\mathcal{F}(\sinc(t)^{2})(\omega)
is supported on
[-1,1]
and therefore the Nyquist rate is
\mathsf{B}=2
.

A further generalization of the WKNS sampling theorem is obtained by shifting the spectrum of the band-limited function
f
by
\omega_{0}
, i.e.,

\supp(T_{\omega_{0}}\widehat{f})\subset[\omega_{0}-\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2},\omega_{0}+\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2}].

(4.25)

So, if
\supp(\widehat{f})\subset[-\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2},\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2}]
, then
T_{\omega_{0}}\widehat{f}
is supported on
[\omega_{0}-\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2},\omega_{0}+\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2}]
. Since, by (
1.6
) we have

M_{\omega}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}T_{\omega}\mathcal{F},

(4.26)

it follows that
\mathcal{F}^{-1}T_{\omega_{0}}\widehat{f}=M_{\omega_{0}}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\widehat{f}=M_{\omega_{0}}f
.

Now, define the function
f_{1}(t)=M_{\omega_{0}}f(t)
, which has Fourier transform
\widehat{f_{1}}(\omega)=T_{\omega_{0}}\widehat{f}(\omega)
. We may apply the Poisson summation formula to
M_{-\omega_{0}}f_{1}(t)=f(t)
;

f(t)=e^{-2\pi i\omega_{0}t}f_{1}(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-2\pi i\omega_{0}\mathsf{T}k}f_{1}(\mathsf{T}k)\sinc\left(\tfrac{t-\mathsf{T}k}{\mathsf{T}}\right)

(4.27)

Hence, the function
f_{1}
which is band-limited within the interval
[\omega_{0}-\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2},\omega_{0}+\frac{\mathsf{B}}{2}]
can be written as

f_{1}(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{1}(\mathsf{T}k)\sinc\left(\tfrac{t-\mathsf{T}k}{\mathsf{T}}\right)e^{2\pi i\omega_{0}(t-\mathsf{T}k)}

(4.28)

Now, assume that
\supp(\widehat{f})\subset\bigcup_{l=1}^{N}I_{l}
, where
I_{l}=\omega_{l}+[-\frac{B_{l}}{2},\frac{B_{l}}{2}]
,
\omega_{l}\in\mathbb{R}
and

|I_{m}\cap I_{n}|=0,

(4.29)

for
l\neq m
. Then we can only apply the WKNS sampling theorem directly to a single frequency band
\omega_{m}+I_{m}
. To apply it to the whole signal we need to cut the spectrum into single frequency bands;

\widehat{f}(\omega)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{f}(\omega)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{I_{l}}(\omega)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{f_{l}}(\omega).

(4.30)

By setting
f_{l}(t)=\int_{I_{l}}\widehat{f_{l}}(\omega)e^{2\pi it\omega}\,d\omega
we have a band-limited function with
\supp(\widehat{f_{l}})\subset I_{l}
which can be recovered by the sampling theorem.

f_{l}(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{l}(\mathsf{T}_{l}k)\sinc\left(\frac{t-\mathsf{T}_{l}k}{\mathsf{T}_{l}}\right)e^{2\pi i\omega_{l}(t-\mathsf{T}_{l}k)}.

(4.31)

To recover the original signal, we need to “glue” together the band-limited functions
f_{l}
. If

f(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{f}(\omega)e^{2\pi it\omega}\,d\omega,

(4.32)

then we also have
f(t)=\sum_{l=1}^{N}\int_{I_{l}}\widehat{f_{l}}(\omega)e^{2\pi it\omega}\,d\omega=\sum_{l=1}^{N}f_{l}(t)
and we get

f(t)=\sum_{l=1}^{N}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{l}(\mathsf{T}_{l}k)\sinc\left(\frac{t-\mathsf{T}_{l}k}{\mathsf{T}_{l}}\right)e^{2\pi i\omega_{l}(t-\mathsf{T}_{l}k)}.

(4.33)

Lastly, assume
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap C(\mathbb{R})
is given as the Fourier transform of an
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})(\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
function
\widehat{f}
(so
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as well), so it need not be band-limited. Then, we may slice the Fourier domain into frequency bands
I_{l}=l+[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
of band-width 1, so
\bigcup_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}I_{l}=\mathbb{R}
(and
|I_{m}\cap I_{n}|=0
). As

\widehat{f}(\omega)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{f}(\omega)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{I_{l}}(\omega)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{f_{l}}(\omega),

(4.34)

we may apply the WKNS sampling theorem to every frequency band
I_{l}
and obtain

\displaystyle f(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{l}(k)\sinc(t-k)e^{2\pi il(t-k)}

(4.35)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{l}(k)\sinc(t-k)e^{2\pi ilt}\underbrace{e^{-2\pi ilk}}_{=1},

(4.36)

where
f_{l}(\omega)=\int_{I_{l}}\widehat{f_{l}}(\omega)e^{2\pi it\omega}\,d\omega
. So, we need to perform an inverse Fourier transform on every frequency band to obtain a part of the signal
f
which is then band-limited on
I_{l}
. We note that a band-limited function actually has infinite support in the time domain, as we will see in the next section. So, even if the signal
f
has a finite time duration, none of the pieces
f_{l}
will have a finite time duration as they are all band-limited.

In the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-sense we can actually write

f=\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{l}(k)M_{l}T_{k}\sinc=\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle f,M_{l}T_{k}\sinc\rangle M_{l}T_{k}\sinc=\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\sinc}f(k,l)\,\pi(k,l)\sinc.

(4.37)

The equality of the coefficients
f_{l}(k)=\langle f,M_{l}T_{k}\sinc\rangle
follows because the system
\{M_{l}T_{k}\sinc\mid k,l\in\mathbb{Z}\}
is actually orthonormal with dense span in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Thus, the coefficients
f_{l}(k)
in the WKNS expansion are therefore uniquely given by the inner products of
f
with the (integer) time-frequency shifted
\sinc
-function, or likewise, by sampling the STFT
V_{\sinc}f
on (the integer lattice)
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. However, this can also be confirmed by a direct computation.

\displaystyle f_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{f}(\omega)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{l+[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(\omega)e^{2\pi it\omega}\,d\omega=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widehat{f}\,T_{l}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]})(t)=f(t)*(M_{l}\sinc)(t)

(4.38)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_STFT_notation}}}{{=}}e^{2\pi itl}V_{\sinc}f(t,l),

(4.39)

as
\overline{\sinc}^{\vee}(t)=\sinc(t)
. Hence, sampling
f_{l}
on
\mathbb{Z}
yields

f_{l}(k)=\underbrace{e^{2\pi ikl}}_{=1}V_{\sinc}f(k,l),\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.40)

Remark
.

Beating the Nyquist rate and still obtaining perfect reconstruction is possible, if further restrictions of the signal are known. One such restriction might be that the (digitized) signal is “sparse”, i.e., having few non-zero entries, in some domain (e.g., with respect to a certain basis). The concept of sparsity is excessively used in the area of compressed sensing. However, this would lead too far away from the intended topics of this course and will hence not be discussed any further.

5
Uncertainty Principles

Classical uncertainty principles are statements about the pair
(f,\widehat{f})
, including inequalities on their supports or a vanishing theorem. We will introduce uncertainty principles in a non-rigorous and rather descriptive way as stated in
[
20
]
. They can be summed up in the following metatheorems.

Metatheorem A
.

A function
f
and its Fourier transform
\widehat{f}
cannot both be simultaneously small.

Metatheorem B
.

A function
f
occupies an area of at least 1 in the time-frequency plane (or phase space).

Metatheorem C
.

Every time-frequency representation comes with its own uncertainty principle.

The above metatheorems should be understood in a heuristic sense rather than in a precise mathematical way. At this point, we remark that the Nyquist sampling rate might be understood as a reverse uncertainty principle. We called the case
\mathsf{T}>\frac{1}{\mathsf{B}}
undersampling. Formulated differently, we might put it as

\mathsf{T}\mathsf{B}>1.

(5.1)

Hence, on average we have less than 1 sample per unit square in the time-frequency plane, which is too little information to recover a (band-limited) signal.

In order to obtain rigorous mathematical statements, we need precise definitions of the terms “small” and “to occupy an area”. Metatheorem
C
stresses the fact that we cannot beat the “classical” uncertainty principles by choosing a different “time-frequency representation”. To be more precise, the idea is to take an uncertainty principle of type
A
or
B
for the pair
(f,\widehat{f})
and replace it by a statement about the STFT or the Wigner distribution. The statement might be a (support) inequality or a vanishing result.

The size of a function is usually measured by some
L^{p}
-norm. In the case that
p=2
, we also speak of the energy. Also, decay conditions might be used. In order to describe how much area a function occupies, we need the concept of the essential support of a function and, already established, the notion of the time-frequency plane.

We start with the very classical Heisenberg-Pauli-Weyl uncertainty principle.

Theorem 5.1

(Heisenberg-Pauli-Weyl Uncertainty Principle)
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x-a)^{2}|f(x)|^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\omega-b)^{2}|\widehat{f}(\omega)|^{2}\,d\omega\right)^{1/2}\geq\frac{1}{4\pi}\lVert f\rVert^{2}_{2}\,.

(5.2)

Proof.

We note that by looking at the function

f_{a,b}(x)=e^{-2\pi ib\cdot x}f(x+a)=M_{-b}T_{-a}f(x),

(5.3)

we may assume that
a=b=0
. Further, we need some necessary technical assumptions. For the following calculations we actually need to assume that
xf(x)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\omega\widehat{f}(\omega)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, so
f^{\prime},\widehat{f}^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as well. However, on the other hand, if either of the assumptions is not met, then the inequality is trivially true. An (unmotivated) integration by parts shows that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(xf(x)\right)\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx=x|f(x)|^{2}\Bigg|_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(|f(x)|^{2}+xf^{\prime}(x)\overline{f(x)}\right)\,dx.

(5.4)

Assuming
\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}x|f(x)|^{2}=0
, which holds for any Schwarz function, the above equation can be re-arranged in the following way

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|^{2}\,dx=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}xf(x)\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}}x\overline{f(x)}f^{\prime}(x)\,dx=-2\Re\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}xf(x)\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx\right),

(5.5)

as
x\in\mathbb{R}
. We note the simple estimate

\left|-2\Re\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}xf(x)\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx\right)\right|\leq 2\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}xf(x)\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx\right|.

(5.6)

Now, we use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to obtain

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}xf(x)\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx\right|=|\langle xf,f^{\prime}\rangle|\leq\lVert xf\rVert_{2}\lVert f^{\prime}\rVert_{2}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{2}|f(x)|^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f^{\prime}(x)|^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}.

(5.7)

From the last three calculations it follows that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|^{2}\,dx\leq 2\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{2}|f(x)|^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f^{\prime}(x)|^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}.

(5.8)

By combining Plancherel’s theorem and the action of the Fourier transform on derivatives (
1.16
) we get

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f^{\prime}(x)|^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|(2\pi i\omega)\widehat{f}(\omega)|^{2}\,d\omega\right)^{1/2}.

(5.9)

Combining the last lines, we obtain

\frac{\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}}{4\pi}\leq\lVert xf\rVert_{2}\lVert\omega\widehat{f}\rVert_{2}

(5.10)

∎

We note that in the statement of Theorem
5.1
we have a factor of
2\pi
due to our choice of the normalization of the Fourier transform. Often, the uncertainty principle is stated with a factor
\frac{1}{2}
due to a different convention of normalizing the Fourier transform. In quantum mechanics, the statement usually also involves Planck’s reduced constant
\hbar
. We will make a little excursion into the field of quantum mechanics now.

Excursion
. The following paragraphs are the author’s interpretation of quantum mechanical results and it should be emphasized that the author does not possess a solid background on the subject.

In quantum mechanics, the (energy – or density – of the) Fourier transform is something which can be measured and, hence, depends on measure(able) units. One way to write it is the following.

\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}f(p)=\left(\frac{1}{2\pi\hbar}\right)^{d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}p\cdot x}\,dx

(5.11)

Now, assuming
\lVert f\rVert_{2}=1
, the expression
|f(x)|^{2}
is the probability density of the chance to find a particle at a certain position, or rather in an interval. The measure unit is meter [m]. The expression
\hbar=\frac{h}{2\pi}
is Planck’s constant
h
divided by
2\pi
. It is named after physicist Max Planck
5
5

5

Max Planck was awarded the Nobel Prize in Physics “in recognition of the services he rendered to the advancement of Physics by his discovery of energy quanta” in 1919 for the year 1918. During the selection process in 1918, the Nobel Committee for Physics decided that none of the year’s nominations met the criteria as outlined in the will of Alfred Nobel. According to the Nobel Foundation’s statutes, the Nobel Prize can in such a case be reserved until the following year, and this statute was then applied. Max Planck therefore received his Nobel Prize for 1918 one year later, in 1919.

https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/physics/1918/summary/
in recognition of his discovery that the energy of harmonically oscillating systems can only change in integer multiples of some smallest portion proportional to the oscillating frequency. Planck’s constant describes this proportionality factor;
\Delta E=\hbar\omega
(
\Delta E
being the change of energy,
\omega
being the circle frequency). Hence,
h
measure energy times time, [J s], which can also be expressed as [m
2
kg/s]. Lastly, we note that
p
, hence, has measure unit [m kg/s] which is velocity times mass, also known as momentum. Hence,
|\widehat{f}|^{2}
(with the normalization induced by
\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}
) is the probability density that the particle has a certain momentum. For
d=1
, Theorem
5.1
is usually stated as

\Delta_{f}x\Delta_{f}p\geq\frac{\hbar}{2}.

(5.12)

The interpretation is that a particle’s position and momentum cannot be measured to arbitrary precision at the same time. It is highly important to understand that this is not a restriction due to measurement errors, it is imposed by a mathematical theorem! Admittedly, expressed as a mathematical theorem the uncertainty principle is much more prosaic and loses some of its mystery. It is a simple inequality for standard deviations.

A model where it finds applications is in a simple model of the hydrogen atom. The hydrogen atom consists of a core with one proton and it has an electron which oscillates around it. In the simplest model, the electron only moves within one direction (
\mathbb{R}
) and satisfies the Schrödinger equation of the harmonic oscillator. Then
a=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x|f(x)|^{2}\,dx
is the expected position of the electron and
b=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p|\widehat{f}(p)|^{2}\,dp
is its expected momentum. Finally,
\Delta_{f}x
and
\Delta_{f}p
are the standard deviations of the probability densities
|f(x)|^{2}
and
|\widehat{f}(p)|^{2}
.

If
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for general
d\in\mathbb{N}
(the physically most relevant case being
d=3
or
d=3N
for
N
particles), then the uncertainty principle holds for conjugate coordinates, i.e.,

\Delta_{f}x_{k}\Delta_{f}p_{k}\geq\frac{\hbar}{2},\qquad k=1,\ldots,d.

(5.13)

We note that in time-frequency analysis the pair
(x,p)
of position and momentum is simply exchanged by the pair
(x,\omega)
. In quantum mechanics, the point
(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
is said to be a point in phase-space, which is the same concept as the time-frequency plane. Finally, by setting
\hbar=\frac{1}{2\pi}
we obtain the Fourier transform as used in time-frequency analysis. Mathematically, there is hence no big difference between the fields of quantum mechanics and time-frequency analysis.

\diamond

The Heisenberg-Pauli-Weyl uncertainty principle can more generally be derived from an inequality about (non-commuting) self-adjoint operators on a Hilbert space. For this, let
A
and
B
be two linear operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle A:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle B:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}

(5.14)

\displaystyle f\mapsto Af

\displaystyle f\mapsto Bf

(5.15)

We denote the commutator of these operators by

[A,B]=AB-BA.

(5.16)

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
A
and
B
be (possibly unbounded) self-adjoint operators on
\mathcal{H}
. Then

\lVert(A-a)f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\lVert(B-b)f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}|\langle[A,B]f,f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}|,

(5.17)

for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and for all
f
in the domain of
AB
and
BA
. Equality holds if and only if
(A-a)f=ic(B-b)f
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We rewrite the commutator and use the fact that
A
and
B
are self-adjoint to obtain

\displaystyle\langle[A,B]f,f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\langle\left((A-a)(B-b)-(B-b)(A-a)\right)f,f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

(5.18)

\displaystyle=\langle(B-b)f,(A-a)f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\langle(A-a)f,(B-b)f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

(5.19)

\displaystyle=\langle(B-b)f,(A-a)f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\overline{\langle(B-b)f,(A-a)f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}

(5.20)

\displaystyle=2\Im\left(\langle(B-b)f,(A-a)f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\right).

(5.21)

Now, we use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and get

\displaystyle|\langle[A,B]f,f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}|

\displaystyle\leq 2|\langle(B-b)f,(A-a)f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}|

(5.22)

\displaystyle\leq 2\lVert(A-a)f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\lVert(B-b)f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}

(5.23)

Equality in (
5.22
) holds if and only if
\langle(B-b)f,(A-a)f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is purely imaginary, and equality in (
5.23
) (Cauchy-Schwarz inequality) holds if and only if
(A-a)f=\lambda(B-b)f
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
. Together, this implies that for equality to hold
\lambda=ic
with
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

To deduce Theorem
5.1
from Lemma
5.2
we need to introduce the position and momentum operators
6
6

6

The factor
\frac{1}{2\pi i}
for the momentum operator
P
clearly comes from the normalization of the Fourier transform. In quantum mechanics, it is often stated as
P=-i\hbar\partial_{x}
.
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, given by

Xf(x)=xf(x)\quad\text{ and }\quad Pf(x)=\tfrac{1}{2\pi i}f^{\prime}(x).

(5.24)

The position operator
X
gives back the expectation value of the position of a particle in the quantum state
f
;

\langle Xf,f\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}Xf(x)\overline{f(x)}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x|f(x)|^{2}\,dx.

(5.25)

The momentum operator gives back the expectation value of the momentum of the particle in the quantum state
f
;

\langle Pf,f\rangle=\langle\mathcal{F}Pf,\mathcal{F}f\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_FT_derivative}}}{{=}}\langle X\mathcal{F}f,\mathcal{F}f\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega|\widehat{f}(\omega)|^{2}\,d\omega.

(5.26)

Having settled the notation, we note that the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
is a common domain for
X,P,XP,PX
. The largest common domain for these operators is the subspace

\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\mid xf(x),f^{\prime},xf^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\}.

(5.27)

Also, we note that these operators are the infinitesimal generators of the modulation and translation groups, i.e.,

\dfrac{d}{d\omega}M_{\omega}f\Big|_{\omega=0}=2\pi iXf\quad\text{ and }\quad\dfrac{d}{dx}T_{x}f\Big|_{x=0}=-2\pi iPf.

(5.28)

To apply Lemma
5.2
we need to check that
X
and
P
are indeed self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. We start with the position operator and compute

\langle Xf,f\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}xf(x)\overline{f(x)}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\overline{(xf(x))}\,dx=\langle f,Xf\rangle,

(5.29)

as
x=\overline{x}
because
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Now, we compute for the momentum operator

\langle Pf,f\rangle=\langle\mathcal{F}Pf,\mathcal{F}f\rangle=\langle X\mathcal{F}f,\mathcal{F}f\rangle=\langle\mathcal{F}f,X\mathcal{F}f\rangle=\langle f,\mathcal{F}^{-1}X\mathcal{F}f\rangle=\langle f,Pf\rangle,

(5.30)

where we used Parseval’s identity and (
1.16
)
7
7

7

Alternatively, one may write the inner product as an integral and use integration by parts to establish the result for a suitable subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, e.g.,
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
.
.

Second proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Assume that
f
is in the domain of
X,P,XP,PX
. Then the commutator of
X
and
P
, applied to
f
, is given by

[X,P]f(x)=\tfrac{1}{2\pi i}(xf^{\prime}(x)-(xf)^{\prime}(x))=\tfrac{1}{2\pi i}\left(xf^{\prime}(x)-(f(x)+xf^{\prime}(x))\right)=-\tfrac{1}{2\pi i}f(x).

(5.31)

Thus, by Lemma
5.2
we have

\tfrac{1}{4\pi}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}|\langle[X,P]f,f\rangle|\leq\lVert(X-a)f\rVert_{2}^{2}\lVert(P-b)f\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(5.32)

The first factor is

\lVert(X-a)f\rVert_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(x-a)^{2}|f(x)|^{2}\,dx

(5.33)

and by Plancherl’s theorem and (
1.16
) the second factor is

\lVert(P-b)f\rVert_{2}^{2}=\lVert\mathcal{F}((P-b)f)\rVert_{2}^{2}=\lVert(X-b)\mathcal{F}f\rVert_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\omega-b)^{2}|\widehat{f}(\omega)|^{2}\,d\omega.

(5.34)

∎

Equality in (
5.32
) holds if and only if
(P-b)f=ic(X-a)f
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. This is the differential equation

f^{\prime}-2\pi ibf=-2\pi c(x-a)f.

(5.35)

The solutions to this differential equation are exactly scalar multiples of the time-frequency shifted, dilated standard Gaussian
g_{0}
;

M_{b}T_{a}e^{-\pi ct^{2}}=e^{-\pi c(t-a)^{2}}e^{2\pi ibt}.

(5.36)

As we assume
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we need
c>0
and can indeed write
M_{b}T_{a}D_{1/\sqrt{c}}g_{0}
(up to a scalar).

A mathematical consequence which can be drawn from the uncertainty principle is the following result.

Corollary 5.3
.

For
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
we have

\lVert Xf\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert Pf\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2},

(5.37)

with equality if and only if
f(t)=ce^{-\pi t^{2}}
.

Proof.

We apply the inequality

2\alpha\beta\leq\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}

(5.38)

to the uncertainty principle with
a=b=0
and
\alpha=\lVert Xf\rVert_{2}
and
\beta=\lVert Pf\rVert_{2}
. For equality we need
\alpha=\beta
and equality in the uncertainty principle. This means we need

Pf=\pm iXf\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad f^{\prime}(x)=\pm 2\pi xf(x)

(5.39)

and the only
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
solutions are the Gaussians
c\,e^{-\pi x^{2}}
,
c\in\mathbb{C}
.
∎

Theorem
5.1
can also be formulated by means of the Rihaczek distribution.

\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|(x-a)(\omega-b)Rf(x,\omega)|^{2}\,d(x,\omega)\right)^{1/2}\geq\frac{1}{4\pi}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(5.40)

We already identified the Rihaczek distribution as a very crude time-frequency representation. This is due to the fact that it still represents the temporal and spectral behavior separately, i.e., it is (up to a phase factor) a simple tensor product of
f
and
\widehat{f}
. Nonetheless, Metatheorem
C
tells us that the inability to exactly measure the instantaneous time-frequency behavior of a function
f
is not due to the crude representation of
Rf
.

The next results treats
f
and
\widehat{f}
separately as well. In this case, we measure the size of a function by its support. This leads to the qualitative statement that a function cannot simultaneously be time- and band-limited.

Theorem 5.4

(Benedicks)
.

Assume
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If

|\supp(f)|\ |\supp(\widehat{f})|<\infty,

(5.41)

then
f\equiv 0
.

Proof.

Let
A=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid f(x)\neq 0\}
and
B=\{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid\widehat{f}(\omega)\neq 0\}
. By using dilations of
f
, we may assume that
|A|<1
. Then, by using Lemma
4.2
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\mid\exists k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\colon f(k+x)\neq 0\}|

\displaystyle=|\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\mid\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{A}(k+x)\geq 1\}|

(5.42)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{A}(k+x)\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{A}(x)\,dx=|A|<1=|\mathbb{T}^{d}|.

(5.43)

Therefore, the set
N_{x}=\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\mid f(k+x)=0,\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
has positive measure.

Next, we observe that

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{B}(l+\omega)\,d\omega=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{B}(\omega)\,d\omega=|B|.

(5.44)

Assume
|B|<\infty
. This implies that
\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{B}(l+\omega)
is finite (almost everywhere) on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Hence, for (almost all)
\omega\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, the set
\widehat{N_{\omega}}=\{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mid\widehat{f}(l+\omega)\neq 0\}
is finite.

For fixed
\omega\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
consider the periodization

\mathcal{P}(M_{-\omega}f)(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(k+x)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot(k+x)}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}T_{-x}M_{-\omega}f(k),

(5.45)

which is a well defined Fourier series for
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By the Poisson summation formula we have

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(T_{-x}M_{-\omega}f)(k)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathcal{F}(T_{-x}M_{-\omega}f)(l)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}M_{x}T_{-\omega}\widehat{f}(l).

(5.46)

Hence, the
l
-th Fourier coefficient of the above periodization equals

\mathcal{F}(M_{-\omega}f)(l)=\widehat{f}(l+\omega),\qquad l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

(5.47)

Since the set
\widehat{N_{\omega}}
is finite, this expression is non-zero for only finitely many
l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, which means that
\mathcal{P}(M_{-\omega}f)
equals a trigonometric polynomial
Q
. However, as
\mathcal{P}(M_{-\omega}f)
vanishes for all
x\in N_{x}
, the trigonometric polynomial
Q
vanishes on
N_{x}
as well. But a trigonometric polynomial that vanishes on a set of positive measure must be identically zero, i.e.,
Q\equiv 0
. Therefore, the Fourier coefficients
\widehat{f}(\omega+l)
are 0 for all
l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and (almost all)
\omega\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Hence,
\widehat{f}\equiv 0
and
f\equiv 0
.
∎

Benedicks’ uncertainty principle holds more generally for
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. It also leads to a qualitative uncertainty principle for the Rihaczek distribution:

If
|\supp(Rf)|<\infty
, then
f\equiv 0
.

This motivates the question, whether such uncertainty principles can be obtained for
V_{g}f
,
A(f,g)
and
W(f,g)
as well. The statement in Metatheorem
C
already suggests that we will obtain similar uncertainty principles in these cases.

We will need the following results to prove qualitative uncertainty principles for the STFT, the ambiguity function and the Wigner distribution.

Lemma 5.5
.

Assume
f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

\mathcal{F}\left(V_{g_{1}}f_{1}\,\overline{V_{g_{2}}f_{2}}\right)(x,\omega)=\left(V_{f_{2}}f_{1}\,\overline{V_{g_{2}}g_{1}}\right)(-\omega,x).

(5.48)

Proof.

Recall that the Fourier transform of the STFT is given by the (cross-)Rihaczek distribution, i.e.,

\widehat{V_{g}f}(\xi,\eta)=e^{2\pi i\xi\cdot\eta}f(-\eta)\overline{\widehat{g}(\xi)}.

(5.49)

Furthermore, by using Parseval’s identity we obtain

\mathcal{F}(f\,\overline{g})(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\overline{g(t)}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt=\langle f,M_{\omega}g\rangle=\langle\widehat{f},T_{\omega}\widehat{g}\rangle.

(5.50)

Since
V_{g_{1}}f_{1}
and
V_{g_{2}}f_{2}
are in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
(because
f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
), their product is in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\left(V_{g_{1}}f_{1}\overline{V_{g_{2}}f_{2}}\right)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{V_{g_{1}}f_{1}},\,T_{(x,\omega)}\widehat{V_{g_{2}}f_{2}}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

(5.51)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\widehat{V_{g_{1}}f_{1}}(\xi,\eta)\overline{\widehat{V_{g_{2}}f_{2}}(\xi-x,\eta-\omega)}\,d(\xi,\omega)

(5.52)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(e^{2\pi i\xi\cdot\eta}f_{1}(-\eta)\overline{\widehat{g_{1}}(\xi)}\right)

(5.53)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\overline{\left(e^{2\pi i(\xi-x)\cdot(\eta-\omega)}f_{2}(-(\eta-\omega))\overline{\widehat{g_{2}}(\xi-x)}\right)}\,d(\xi,\eta)

(5.54)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{1}(\eta)\overline{f_{2}(\eta+\omega)}e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\eta}\,d\eta\,\overline{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widehat{g_{1}}(\xi)\overline{\widehat{g_{2}}(\xi-x)}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot(\xi-x))}\,d\xi}

(5.55)

The first integral is already the desired expression
V_{f_{2}}f_{1}(-\omega,x)
. The second integral can also be expressed by

\langle\widehat{g_{1}},T_{x}M_{\omega}\widehat{g_{2}}\rangle=\langle g_{1},M_{x}T_{-\omega}g_{2}\rangle=V_{g_{2}}f_{2}(-\omega,x).

(5.56)

∎

We obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 5.6
.

For
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the function

F(x,\omega)=e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g}f(x,\omega)\,V_{g}f(-x,-\omega)

(5.57)

satisfies

\widehat{F}(x,\omega)=F(-\omega,x).

(5.58)

Furthermore, the function

F_{(\xi,\eta)}(x,\omega)=e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g}\left(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f\right)(x,\omega)\,V_{g}\left(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f\right)(-x,-\omega)

(5.59)

satisfies

\widehat{F_{(\xi,\eta)}}(x,\omega)=F_{(\xi,\eta)}(-\omega,x),\qquad\forall(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}.

(5.60)

Proof.

It suffices to prove the first assertion, the second follows by replacing
f
by
M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f
. We note that

V_{g}f(-x,-\omega)e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\overline{g(t+x)}e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot(t+x)}\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t-x)\overline{g(t)}e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt=\overline{V_{f}g(x,\omega)}.

(5.61)

Therefore, we can write

F(x,\omega)=\left(V_{g}f\,\overline{V_{f}g}\right)(x,\omega).

(5.62)

By using Lemma
5.5
we see that

\widehat{F}(x,\omega)=\mathcal{F}\left(V_{g}f\,\overline{V_{f}g}\right)(x,\omega)=\left(V_{g}f\,\overline{V_{f}g}\right)(-\omega,x)=F(-\omega,x).

(5.63)

∎

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then the following are equivalent:

(i)

|\supp(V_{g}f)|<\infty

(ii)

|\supp(A(f,g))|<\infty

(iii)

|\supp(W(f,g))|<\infty

(iv)

Either
f\equiv 0
or
g\equiv 0
(or both).

Proof.

The equivalence of (
i
), (
ii
) and (
iii
) follows from the algebraic relations in Lemma
3.6
. Hence, we only need to prove (
i
)
\Rightarrow
(
iv
) as the other direction is trivially true.
We use the function from Corollary
5.6

F_{(\xi,\eta)}(x,\omega)=e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}\,V_{g}(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f)(x,\omega)\,V_{g}(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f)(-x,-\omega).

(5.64)

By the covariance principle, given by formula (
2.22
) in Proposition
2.6
, we see that

|V_{g}\left(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f\right)(x,\omega)|=|V_{g}f(x-\xi,\omega-\eta)|.

(5.65)

By assumption, the support of
V_{g}f
has finite measure, which now implies that
F_{(\xi,\eta)}
has finite measure. By Corollary
5.6
,
\widehat{F_{(\xi,\eta)}}
must have finite measure as well and we have a pair
(F,\widehat{F})
where, both,
F
and
\widehat{F}
have finite measure. By Benedicks’ theorem, this implies that
F_{(\xi,\eta)}\equiv 0
for all
(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. Using the covariance principle again and evaluating
F_{(\xi,\eta)}
at
(x,\omega)=(0,0)
, we see that

|F_{(-\xi,-\eta)}(0,0)|=|V_{g}\left(M_{-\eta}T_{-\xi}f\right)(0,0)|^{2}=|V_{g}f(\xi,\eta)|^{2},\qquad\forall(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}.

(5.66)

Particularly, this shows that
V_{g}f\equiv 0
and by the isometry property
\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{2}=\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}
, obtained from the orthogonality relations (
2.27
), we conclude that either
f\equiv 0
or
g\equiv 0
(or both).
∎

Remark
.

This result was, e.g., established by Janssen
[
34
]
, who also proved the following result. Let
H
be any half-space of
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, then

|\{(x,\omega)\in H\mid Wf(x,\omega)\neq 0\}|\in\{0,\infty\}.

(5.67)

We will now continue with essential support conditions. Hence, we need to define the essential support of a function.

Definition 5.8
.

A function is said to be
\varepsilon
-concentrated on a (measurable) set
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if

\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash T}|f(t)|^{2}\,dt\right)^{1/2}\leq\varepsilon\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(5.68)

For small
\varepsilon
, the definition above tells us that most of the energy is concentrated in
T
, which may than be considered as the essential support of
f
. The first idea to modify Benedicks’ theorem is to replace the support condition by an essential support condition using the
\varepsilon
-concentration. This leads to the uncertainty principle of Donoho-Stark

Theorem 5.9

(Donoho-Stark)
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(not the zero-function) be
\varepsilon_{T}
-concentrated on
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\widehat{f}
being
\varepsilon_{\Omega}
-concentrated on
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then

|T||\Omega|\geq(1-\varepsilon_{T}-\varepsilon_{\Omega})^{2}.

(5.69)

Proof.

Without loss of generality we may assume that
T
and
\Omega
have finite measure. We introduce the time-limiting and band-limiting operators

P_{T}f=\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}f\quad\text{ and }\quad Q_{\Omega}f(t)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\Omega}\widehat{f})(x)=\int_{\Omega}\widehat{f}(\omega)e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}\,d\omega.

(5.70)

Both operators are orthogonal projections on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The range of
P_{T}
is
L^{2}(T,dx)
and the range of
Q_{\Omega}
consists of all
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
functions with spectrum in
\Omega
, i.e.,
\supp(\widehat{f})\subset\Omega
. With this notation,
f
is
\varepsilon_{T}
-concentrated if and only if

\lVert f-P_{T}f\rVert_{2}\leq\varepsilon_{T}\lVert f\rVert_{2}.

(5.71)

Similarly,
\widehat{f}
is
\varepsilon_{\Omega}
-concentrated if and only if

\lVert f-Q_{\Omega}f\rVert_{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\Omega}\lVert f\rVert_{2}.

(5.72)

Since
\lVert Q_{\Omega}\rVert_{op}\leq 1
, we obtain

\lVert f-Q_{\Omega}P_{T}f\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert f-Q_{\Omega}f\rVert_{2}+\lVert Q_{\Omega}(f-P_{T}f)\rVert_{2}\leq(\varepsilon_{\Omega}+\varepsilon_{T})\lVert f\rVert_{2},

(5.73)

and consequently

\lVert Q_{\Omega}P_{T}f\rVert_{2}\geq\lVert f\rVert_{2}-\lVert f-Q_{\Omega}P_{T}f\rVert_{2}\geq(1-\varepsilon_{\Omega}-\varepsilon_{T})\lVert f\rVert_{2}.

(5.74)

Next, we compute the integral kernel and then the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of
Q_{\Omega}P_{T}
. We have

\displaystyle Q_{\Omega}P_{T}f(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\Omega}\mathcal{F}(P_{T}f))(x)

(5.75)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_{T}f(t)e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt\right)e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}\,d\omega

(5.76)

Since
T
and
\Omega
have finite support and
f\in L^{2}(T)\subset L^{1}(T)
, this double integral converges absolutely and by Fubini we may exchange the order of integration. This yields

Q_{\Omega}P_{T}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}k(x,t)f(t)\,dt,

(5.77)

with integral kernel

k(x,t)=\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(t)\int_{\Omega}e^{2\pi i(x-t)\cdot\omega}\,d\omega=\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(t)T_{t}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\Omega})(x).

(5.78)

The Hilbert-Schmidt norm of
Q_{\Omega}P_{T}
is given by

\lVert Q_{\Omega}P_{T}\rVert_{H.S.}^{2}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|k(x,t)|^{2}\,d(x,t).

(5.79)

Since the translation operator
T_{t}
and the (inverse) Fourier transform are unitary, we have for fixed
t
that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|k(x,t)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(t)\lVert T_{t}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\Omega}\rVert_{2}^{2}

(5.80)

\displaystyle=\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(t)\lVert\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\Omega}\rVert_{2}^{2}

(5.81)

\displaystyle=|\Omega|\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(t),

(5.82)

and, therefore,

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|k(x,t)|^{2}\,d(x,t)=|\Omega||T|.

(5.83)

By combining (
5.74
) and (
5.83
) and the fact that the operator norm is dominated by the Hilbert-Schmidt norm, we obtain

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon_{T}-\varepsilon_{\Omega})^{2}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert Q_{\Omega}P_{T}f\rVert_{2}^{2}

(5.84)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert Q_{\Omega}P_{T}\rVert_{op}^{2}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}

(5.85)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert Q_{\Omega}P_{T}\rVert_{H.S.}^{2}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}

(5.86)

\displaystyle\leq|T||\Omega|\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(5.87)

∎

By letting
\varepsilon
tend to 0, we obtain a precise statement for Metatheorem
B
. The essential support conditions can be transferred to the STFT, ambiguity function and Wigner distribution as well. The next result is called the “Weak Uncertainty Principle for the STFT”.

Theorem 5.10

(Weak Uncertainty Principle for the STFT)
.

Assume
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\lVert f\rVert_{2}=\lVert g\rVert_{2}=1
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and
\varepsilon\geq 0
such that

\iint_{U}|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|^{2}\,d(x,\omega)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

(5.88)

Then,
|U|\geq 1-\varepsilon
.

Proof.

The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality implies

|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|=|\langle f,M_{\omega}T_{x}g\rangle|\leq\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}=1.

(5.89)

Hence,

1-\varepsilon\leq\iint_{U}|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|^{2}\,d(x,\omega)\leq\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{\infty}^{2}|U|\leq|U|.

(5.90)

∎

It is obvious that the same inequality holds if we replace
V_{g}f
by
A(f,g)
. By the relation in Lemma
3.8
the inequality holds for the Wigner distribution
W(f,g)
as well.

We note that there are many more uncertainty principles and for an overview, the reader is referred to
[
20
]
.

6
Gabor Systems and Frames

The following section follows the textbooks of Gröchenig
[
19
]
and Christensen
[
5
]
.

After our (extensive) preliminary work, we will now study Gabor systems and Gabor frames. The field might be called Gabor analysis and is considered to be a sub-field of (applied) harmonic analysis. It has been a fruitful domain for engineers for decades and has particular applications in wireless communications and is fundamental in the new 5G transmission system. Gabor systems are named after Dennis Gabor who studied such systems in his article
Theory of Communication

[
14
]
in 1946. However, he was not the first person to study these systems, they have been considered already by John von Neumann in the context of (reformulating) quantum mechanics
[
44
]
in the 1930s.

From a sampling theoretic point of view, we have seen the WKNS sampling theorem for band-limited functions and an extension to non-band-limited function, which involved sampling the STFT with a
\sinc
-window. For general windows
g
, we have only dealt with continuous time-frequency representations, which is fine for many theoretical purposes. However, this is not always satisfactory for practical purposes and we would like to have something similar to the extension of the WKNS sampling theorem. For this purpose, we consider the inversion formula (
2.60
) for the STFT

f=\frac{1}{\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle}\iint_{R^{2d}}V_{g}f(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g}\,d(x,\omega).

(6.1)

This is a continuous expansion of
f
with respect to the uncountable system of functions

\{M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g}\mid(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\}.

(6.2)

The coefficients in this continuous expansions are given by the evaluations of
V_{g}f
at
(x,\omega)
. We might call
g
the analysis window and
\widetilde{g}
the synthesis window.

However,
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a separable Hilbert space and we should be able to find a series expansion of
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with respect to a countable set of time-frequency shifts of
\widetilde{g}
. Assume that, in the time-frequency plane,
\widetilde{g}
has its essential time-frequency support on a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. Let
(x_{1},\omega_{1})
and
(x_{2},\omega_{2})
be two neighboring points in the time-frequency plane, then, for
i=1,2
,
M_{\omega_{i}}T_{x_{i}}\widetilde{g}
has essential time-frequency support
E+(x_{i},\omega_{i})
. The two supports will have a “large” overlap for neighboring points, and also, with similar reasoning, the information carried by the coefficients
V_{g}f(x_{i},\omega_{i})
is roughly the same for
i=1,2
. The representation of
f
by the inversion formula is thus highly redundant and also the interpretation of
V_{g}f
as the time-frequency content becomes rather vague.

The goal is, therefore, to obtain a discrete representation of
f
by a countable set of time-frequency shifts of the synthesis window
\widetilde{g}
, such that, in the time-frequency plane, the essential supports of
M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g}
have minimal overlap. A first attempt is to replace the integral by a Riemann sum over a sufficiently dense discrete set, representing
f
as

f=\mathop{\sum\sum}_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\langle f,M_{\beta l}T_{\alpha k}\,g\rangle M_{\beta l}T_{\alpha k}\,\widetilde{g},

(6.3)

\alpha
and
\beta
sufficiently small.

More generally (or modestly, as one prefers), we could also try to find an expansion of the form

f=\mathop{\sum\sum}_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}c_{k,l}M_{\beta l}T_{\alpha k}g,

(6.4)

with coefficients
c_{k,l}=c_{k,l}(f)
to be determined. For
\alpha,\beta
chosen such that the essential time-frequency supports
(\alpha k,\beta l)+E
of
M_{\beta l}T_{\alpha k}g
are almost disjoint, the coefficients
c_{k,l}
obtain more meaning as a measure of the time-frequency content of
f
in the region
(\alpha k,\beta l)+E
. We note that (
6.4
) can be seen as a generalized Fourier series. Consider the function space
L^{2}([0,1]^{d})
, then any element
f\in L^{2}([0,1]^{d})
has an expansion of the form

f(t)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}c_{l}\,e^{2\pi il\cdot t}=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}c_{l}\,M_{l}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[0,1]^{d}}(t),

(6.5)

where the
c_{l}
are the Fourier coefficients. The set
\{M_{l}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[0,1]^{d}}\}
yields an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}([0,1]^{d})
. Shifting the set by
T_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we obtain an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and have an expansion of the form

\mathop{\sum\sum}_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}c_{k,l}M_{l}T_{k}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[0,1]^{d}}.

(6.6)

As discussed in the previous sections, the properties of
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[0,1]^{d}}
are not really satisfactory for time-frequency analysis. Gabor (as well as already earlier von Neumann) considered the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and exchanged the indicator function by the Gaussian function and used time-frequency shifts
M_{l}T_{k}
with respect to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, i.e., (k,l)
\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Expansions of type (
6.4
) are now usually called Gabor expansions and the coefficients are called Gabor coefficients.

Yet, a third way of discretization is obtained from the interpretation of the spectrogram
|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|^{2}
as an energy density of
f
in a time-frequency cell centered at
(x,\omega)
. We then need to sample
V_{g}f
densely enough so that the energy is preserved under the discretization. This means that, for positive constants
0<A\leq B<\infty
, the following inequality needs to be fulfilled

A\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\mathop{\sum\sum}_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|V_{g}f(\alpha k,\beta l)|^{2}\leq B\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2},\qquad\forall f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(6.7)

Determining pairs
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
such that the above inequality holds, is in general a very challenging topic. The meaning of the right-hand side of inequality (
6.7
) is that the sampling operation is continuous on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, while the left-hand side of (
6.7
) expresses that
f
is uniquely determined by the samples of the STFT. We remark that
f
also depends continuously on the samples
V_{g}f(\alpha k,\beta l)
. From this point of view, the discretization problem for the STFT is reminiscent of related problems and questions in the theory of band-limited functions, such as the Nyquist-Shannon sampling theorem.

We will see that the three approaches of discretization are equivalent and yield the same answers. However, expansions of type (
6.3
) and (
6.4
) raise new mathematical questions.

(a)

In general, the time-frequency atoms
M_{\beta l}T_{\alpha k}g
are not orthogonal. Therefore, the question pops up in which way the series (
6.3
) and (
6.4
) converge.

(b)

Given a window
g
, how can we determine a dual window
\widetilde{g}
in (
6.3
)? More generally, how can we determine the Gabor coefficients in (
6.4
)?

(c)

What are suitable lattice parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
such that
f
is uniquely determined by samples of
V_{g}f
on
\alpha\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\beta\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

(d)

How does the uncertainty principle manifest itself in the context of discrete time-frequency representations?

(e)

Can we construct orthonormal bases of the form
\{M_{\beta l}T_{\alpha k}g\mid k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}
, where
g
has “nice” properties in the time-frequency plane?

Even after these questions may have been answered, the applied signal analysit will still not be happy. The Gabor expansion (
6.4
) still lives within the infinite-dimensional space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. From an engineering point of view, we therefore require another discretization step, which yields a finite-dimensional model of time-frequency analysis and gives numerical algorithms. We will not deal with these questions in the scope of the course. We refer the interested reader to
[
12
, Chap. 8]
as a starting point for further reading.

6.1
Frames

We start with the general theory of frames. Frames extend the concept of bases, which leads to, in general, non-orthogonal, overcomplete systems, which means that the elements will also not be linearly independent. Motivated by (
6.7
), we start with the following definition.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a (separable) Hilbert space. A set
\{e_{\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}
in
\mathcal{H}
is called a frame if there exist positive constants
0<A\leq B<\infty
such that for all
f\in\mathcal{H}
the following inequality holds

A\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}|\langle f,e_{\gamma}\rangle|^{2}\leq B\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

(6.8)

Any two constants
A,B
satisfying (
6.8
) are called frame bounds (or frame constants). If
A=B
the set
\{e_{\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}
is said to constitute a tight frame.

Example 6.2
.

(i)

Any orthonormal basis is a (tight) frame with optimal lower and upper frame bound
A=B=1
. Any positive
A<1
and any finite
B>1
gives a lower or upper frame bound, respectively.

(ii)

The union of any two orthonormal basis is a tight frame with optimal frame bounds
A=B=2
.

(iii)

The union of any orthonormal basis with
L
arbitrary unit vectors is a frame with bounds
A=1
and
B=L+1
.

We give now examples in the finite dimensional Hilbert space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(iv)

Let
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let

\mathbf{F}=\{v_{k}=(v_{k,1},\ldots,v_{k,d})\mid k=1,\ldots,K\},

(6.9)

of
K
column-vectors,
K\geq d
. Then, the so-called frame operator is given by

S_{\mathbf{F}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\qquad f\mapsto S_{\mathbf{F}}f=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\langle f,v_{k}\rangle v_{k}=D_{\mathbf{F}}C_{\mathbf{F}}f,

(6.10)

where
C_{\mathbf{F}}
and
D_{\mathbf{F}}
are the matrices (operators) given by

C_{\mathbf{F}}=\begin{pmatrix}v_{1,1}&\ldots&v_{1,d}\\
&\ddots&\\
v_{K,1}&\ldots&v_{K,d}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{ and }\quad D_{\mathbf{F}}=C_{\mathbf{F}}^{T}.

(6.11)

The matrix
C_{\mathbf{F}}
is the coefficient matrix and its transpose (adjoint) is the synthesis matrix. Note that
S_{\mathbf{F}}
is its own transpose (it is self-adjoint) and that the elements
f
and
S_{\mathbf{F}}f
may in general differ from one another.

Furthermore, let
\sigma_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\sigma_{d}
denote the singular values of
C_{\mathbf{F}}
. Then, for any
f\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\sigma_{1}^{2}\lVert f\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert C_{\mathbf{F}}f\rVert^{2}=\langle S_{\mathbf{F}}f,f\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}|\langle f,v_{k}\rangle|^{2}\leq\sigma_{d}^{2}\lVert f\rVert^{2}.

(6.12)

(v)

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the following collection of vectors

\mathbf{F}_{N}=\{\left(\cos(2\pi k/N),\sin(2\pi k/N)\right)\mid k=0,\ldots N-1\}

(6.13)

Let
N=1
. Then,
\mathbf{F}_{1}
does not span
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and cannot be a frame. The upper frame bound is
B=1
, whereas the lower frame bound vanishes, i.e.,
A=0
. For
N=2
, we obtain a unit vector and its negative. Hence, the upper bound is
B=2
but
\mathbf{F}_{2}
does not span
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and so the lower bound is
A=0
.

Hence, let
N\geq 3
. Then, the coefficient operator is given by the matrix

C_{\mathbf{F}_{N}}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(2\pi 0/N)&\sin(2\pi 0/N)\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
\cos(2\pi(N-1)/N)&\sin(2\pi(N-1)/N)\end{pmatrix}.

(6.14)

We compute the Gram matrix

C_{\mathbf{F}_{N}}^{T}C_{\mathbf{F}_{N}}=\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\cos(2\pi k/N)^{2}&\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\cos(2\pi k/N)\sin(2\pi k/N)\\
\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\cos(2\pi k/N)\sin(2\pi k/N)&\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\sin(2\pi k/N)^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(6.15)

It is easily seen that

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\cos(2\pi k/N)\sin(2\pi k/N)=0,

(6.16)

as the parity of the sine terms makes everything cancel out. Next, we observe that

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\cos(2\pi k/N)^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\sin(2\pi k/N)^{2}=N.

(6.17)

Hence, we only need to compute the value of the sum
\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\cos(2\pi k/N)^{2}
. We use the trigonometric identity
\cos(x)^{2}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\cos(2x)}{2}
and obtain

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\cos(2\pi k/N)^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\left(1+\cos(2\pi 2k/N)\right)=\frac{N}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\cos(2\pi 2k/N).

(6.18)

Now, we write

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\cos(2\pi 2k/N)

\displaystyle=\Re\left(\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}e^{2\pi i2k/N}\right)

(6.19)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\left(e^{4\pi i/N}\right)^{N}}{1-e^{4\pi i/N}}=0,

(6.20)

for
N\geq 3
, by the formula
\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}q^{k}=\frac{1-q^{N}}{1-q}
,
q\neq 1
.
Therefore,

\sum_{k=0}^{N_{1}}\cos(2\pi k/N)^{2}=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\sin(2\pi k/N)^{2}=\frac{N}{2}.

(6.21)

It follows that

C_{\mathbf{F}_{N}}^{T}C_{\mathbf{F}_{N}}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{N}{2}&0\\
0&\frac{N}{2}\end{pmatrix},

(6.22)

and, so the squared singular values are
\sigma_{1}^{2}=\sigma_{2}^{2}=\frac{N}{2}
. It readily follows that the collections of functions
\mathbf{F}_{N}
,
N\geq 3
yield tight frames with frame bounds
A=B=\frac{N}{2}
.

\diamond

After these first, simple examples, we will now introduce the concept of unconditional convergence and some important operators.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
\{e_{\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}
be a countable set in a Banach space
\mathcal{B}
. The series
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}e_{\gamma}
is said to converge unconditionally to the element
f\in\mathcal{B}
if for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists a finite set
F_{0}\subset\Gamma
such that

\lVert f-\sum_{\gamma\in F}e_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}<\varepsilon\qquad\text{ for all finite set }F\supset F_{0}.

(6.23)

Since the index set
\Gamma
is countable, there exists a bijective map
\pi:\mathbb{N}\to\Gamma
such that
\Gamma
can be enumerated by
\mathbb{N}
. Then, the convergence of
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}e_{\gamma}
can be defined by the convergence of the partial sums
\sum_{k=1}^{N}e_{\pi(k)}
, i.e.,

f=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N}e_{\pi(k)}.

(6.24)

This approach encounters the following problems. First, if
\Gamma
is an unstructured index set, then there is no canonical enumeration
\pi
and, thus, no natural sequence of partial sums. Second, in general it is not clear whether the limit in (
6.24
) is independent of the enumeration
\pi
. However, if the series converges unconditionally, then these problems cannot arise. This is shown by the following result.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\{e_{\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}
be a countable set in the Banach space
\mathcal{B}
. Then, the following are equivalent.

(i)

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}e_{\gamma}
converges unconditionally to
f\in\mathcal{B}
.

(ii)

For every enumeration
\pi:\mathbb{N}\to\Gamma
the sequence of partial sums
\sum_{k=1}^{N}e_{\pi(k)}
converges to
f\in\mathcal{B}
, i.e.,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\lVert f-\sum_{k=1}^{N}e_{\pi(k)}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}=0.

(6.25)

In particular, the limit does not depend on the specific enumeration
\pi
.

Proof.

(i)\Rightarrow(ii)
: Let
\pi:\mathbb{N}\to\Gamma
be an enumeration of
\Gamma
and let
\varepsilon>0
. Since
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}e_{\gamma}
converges unconditionally, there is a finite set
F_{0}\subset\Gamma
such that

\lVert f-\sum_{\gamma\in F}e_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}<\varepsilon,\qquad\text{ for all finite }F\supset F_{0}.

(6.26)

Now, choose
N_{0}
large enough such that
F_{0}\subset\{\pi(1),\ldots,\pi(N_{0})\}
. Then

\lVert f-\sum_{k=1}^{N}e_{\pi(k)}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}<\varepsilon,\qquad\text{ for }N\geq N_{0}.

(6.27)

(ii)\Rightarrow(i)
: Assume that every rearrangement of
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}e_{\gamma}
converges to
f\in\mathcal{B}
, but not unconditionally. Then, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for every finite set
F\subset\Gamma
there is
F^{\prime}\supset F
with

\lVert f-\sum_{\gamma\in F^{\prime}}e_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}\geq\varepsilon.

(6.28)

Now, fix an enumeration
\pi:\mathbb{N}\to\Gamma
. Since
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e_{\pi(k)}
converges, there is an index
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\lVert f-\sum_{k=1}^{N}e_{\pi(k)}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\qquad\forall N\geq N_{0}.

(6.29)

By induction, we can therefore construct a sequence of finite sets
F_{n}\subset\Gamma
of cardinality
N_{n}
with the following properties;

(a)

F_{n}\subset F_{n+1}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}

(b)

\lVert f-\sum_{\gamma\in F_{2n}}e_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}>\varepsilon
for the sets with even index

(c)

F_{2n+1}
is of the form
\{\pi(1),\ldots,\pi(N_{2n+1})\}
where
N_{2n+1}(\geq N_{0})
is chosen large enough so that
F_{2n+1}\supset F_{2n}
. Then

\lVert f-\sum_{\gamma\in F_{2n+1}}e_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}=\lVert f-\sum_{k=1}^{N_{2n+1}}e_{\pi(k)}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

(6.30)

Now, we define a new rearrangement
\sigma:\mathbb{N}\to\Gamma
by enumerating the elements in the finite sets
F_{1},F_{2}\backslash F_{1},\ldots,F_{n+1}\backslash F_{n},\ldots
consecutively. Then, we have

\displaystyle\lVert\sum_{k=N_{2n}+1}^{N_{2n+1}}e_{\sigma(k)}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\lVert\sum_{\gamma\in F_{2n+1}}e_{\gamma}-\sum_{\gamma\in F_{2n}}e_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}

(6.31)

\displaystyle\geq\lVert f-\sum_{\gamma\in F_{2n}}e_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}-\lVert f-\sum_{\gamma\in F_{2n+1}}e_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}>\varepsilon-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

(6.32)

Therefore,
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e_{\sigma(k)}
does not converge, which contradicts the assumption.
∎

If
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, then
F_{0}
can be taken to be the cube
\{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mid|k_{j}|\leq N,j=1,\ldots,d\}
or the ball
\{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mid|k|\leq N\}
. Thus, unconditional convergence implies convergence of the rectangular and radial partial sums, but the convergence of the rectangular and radial partial sums does in general not imply unconditional convergence.

Often, it is necessary to interchange the action of a linear operator with summation. If a series converges unconditionally, then this interchange is always justified.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
A
be a bounded operator between two Banach spaces
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
. If
f=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}e_{\gamma}
converges unconditionally in
\mathcal{B}_{1}
, then
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}Ae_{\gamma}
converges unconditionally in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
and
