F(x,\omega)\langle\pi(x,\omega)f,g\rangle=0,\quad\text{for almost all }(x,\omega)

(7.144)

and this holds for all
f,g\in\mathcal{H}
. Thus,
F(x,\omega)=0
(almost everywhere) and we have proved that the extended representation is one-to-one on
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.
∎

One also says that
\pi
extends to a faithful representation of the Banach algebra
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
under twisted convolution.

7.3.1
The Stone-von Neumann Theorem

There is a classification of all irreducible unitary representations of the Heisenberg group, given by the Stone-von Neumann theorem. Its motivation lies in the commutation relations of the quantum mechanical operators. We will follow Gröchenig
[
19
]
, giving von Neumann’s original proof, which (once again) highlights the fundamental role of Gaussians.

Theorem 7.32

(Stone-von Neumann)
.

Every irreducible representation of
\mathbf{H}
is equivalent to exactly one of the following representations:

(i)

\chi_{a,b}(x,\omega,\tau)=e^{2\pi i(a\cdot x+b\cdot\omega)}
acting on
\mathbb{C}
, for some
(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, or

(ii)

\rho_{\kappa}
for some
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
, acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Since
\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}\subset\ker\chi_{a,b}
, the characters
\chi_{a,b}
are simply the characters of the quotient group
\mathbf{H}/(\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

The proof of the Stone-von Neumann theorem requires some preparation. We will first state and prove Schur’s lemma, which gives a criterion for the irreducibility of a representation. Then, we will study some statements on Gaussians in the language of representations and twisted convolutions.

Lemma 7.33

(Schur’s Lemma)
.

Let
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
be a unitary representation of
\mathbf{H}
. Then the following are equivalent.

(a)

\pi
is irreducible.

(b)

For every
g\in\mathcal{H}\backslash\{0\}
the subspace spanned by the finite linear combinations of
\pi(\mathbf{h})g
,
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbf{H}
, is dense in
\mathcal{H}
.

(c)

If a bounded operator
S:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
satisfies
\pi(\mathbf{h})S=S\pi(\mathbf{h})
for all
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbf{H}
, then
S=cI_{\mathcal{H}}
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

(a)\Longleftrightarrow(b)
: For
g\in\mathcal{H}
,
g\neq 0
, let
\mathcal{H}_{g}=\text{span}\{\pi(\mathbf{h})g\mid\mathbf{h}\in\mathbf{H}\}
. If

f=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}\pi(\mathbf{h_{k}})g\in\mathcal{H}_{g},

(7.145)

then
\pi(\mathbf{h})f=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}\pi(\mathbf{hh}_{k})g\in\mathcal{H}_{g}
as well. Thus,
\mathcal{H}_{g}
is invariant under
\pi
. Since all
\pi(\mathbf{h})
,
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbf{H}
, are unitary, hence bounded, operators, the closure
\overline{\mathcal{H}_{g}}
is also invariant. If
\pi
is irreducible, then
\overline{\mathcal{H}_{g}}=\mathcal{H}
, as claimed.

Conversely, for any
g\in\mathcal{H}\backslash\{0\}
we have that
\overline{\mathcal{H}_{g}}=\mathcal{H}
by assumption and these are the smallest (non-trivial) subspaces of
\mathcal{H}
invariant under
\pi
. Hence, only
\{0\}
and
\mathcal{H}
are invariant, so
\pi
is irreducible.

(c)\Longrightarrow(a)
: Suppose that
\mathcal{K}
is a closed invariant subspace for
\pi
and let
P
be the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{K}
. Since
\langle f,\pi(\mathbf{h})g\rangle=\langle\pi(\mathbf{h}^{-1})f,g\rangle=0
for all
f\in\mathcal{K}
,
g\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
, and
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbf{H}
, we conclude that
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
is also invariant under
\pi
. Therefore,
P\pi(\mathbf{h})(I_{\mathcal{H}}-P)f=0
for all
f\in\mathcal{H}
, and consequently

P\pi(\mathbf{h})f=P\pi(\mathbf{h})Pf=\pi(\mathbf{h})Pf,\quad\forall\mathbf{h}\in\mathbf{H}.

(7.146)

Therefore, by assumption
P=cI_{\mathcal{H}}
. But since
P=P^{2}
, we must have either
c=0
and
\mathcal{K}=\{0\}
or
c=1
and
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{H}
. Thus,
\pi
is irreducible.

(a)\Longrightarrow(c)
: Suppose that
\pi
is irreducible and that
S\pi(\mathbf{h})=\pi(\mathbf{h})S
for all
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbf{H}
. Since

S^{*}\pi(\mathbf{h})=\left(\pi(\mathbf{h}^{-1})S\right)^{*}=\left(S\pi(\mathbf{h}^{-1})\right)^{*}=\pi(\mathbf{h})S^{*},

(7.147)

S^{*}S
also commutes with all
\pi(\mathbf{h})
and we may assume, without loss of generality, that
S
is self-adjoint. If
S\neq cI_{\mathcal{H}}
, then the spectral theorem for (bounded) self-adjoint operators implies the existence of an orthogonal projection
P
,
P\neq 0
,
P\neq I_{\mathcal{H}}
, that commutes with
S
and with all
\pi(\mathbf{h})
,
\mathbf{h}\in\mathbf{H}
. Then
P\mathcal{H}
is invariant under
\pi
and
P\mathcal{H}
is not trivial. This contradicts the irreducibility of
\pi
.
∎

As announced, we are going to state more properties about Gaussians.

Lemma 7.34
.

Let
\varphi(t)=2^{d/4}e^{-\pi t^{2}}
be the normalized standard Gaussian in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We define

\Phi^{\xi,\eta}(x,\omega)=\langle\rho(\xi,\eta)\varphi,\rho(x,\omega)\varphi\rangle

(7.148)

and set
\Phi=\Phi^{0,0}
. Then

(a)

\Phi(x,\omega)=e^{-\tfrac{\pi}{2}(x^{2}+\omega^{2})}\quad\text{ and }\quad\Phi^{\xi,\eta}(x,\omega)=e^{\pi i(x\cdot\eta-\xi\cdot\omega)}\Phi(x-\xi,\omega-\eta)

(7.149)

(b)

\Phi\natural\Phi^{\xi,\eta}=e^{-\tfrac{\pi}{2}(\xi^{2}+\eta^{2})}\Phi.

(7.150)

(c)

If
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
is a unitary representation of the Heisenberg group such that
\pi(0,0,\tau)=e^{2\pi i\tau}I_{\mathcal{H}}
, then

\pi(\xi,\eta,0)\pi(\Phi)=\pi(\Phi^{\xi,\eta}).

(7.151)

Proof.

(a)

By a direct computation and using the fact that the Gaussian is invariant under the Fourier transform, we see (once again) that

\langle\varphi,\rho(x,\omega)\varphi\rangle=A\varphi=\Phi(x,\omega).

(7.152)

Then, a direct computation shows

\displaystyle\Phi^{\xi,\eta}(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\langle\rho(\xi,\eta)\varphi,\rho(x,\omega)\varphi\rangle=\langle M_{\frac{\eta}{2}}T_{\xi}M_{\frac{\eta}{2}}\varphi,M_{\frac{\omega}{2}}T_{x}M_{\frac{\omega}{2}}\varphi\rangle

(7.153)

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi,M_{-\frac{\eta}{2}}T_{\xi}M_{-\frac{\eta}{2}}M_{\frac{\omega}{2}}T_{x}M_{\frac{\omega}{2}}\varphi\rangle

(7.154)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i\eta\cdot\xi}e^{\pi i\eta\cdot x}\langle\varphi,T_{-\xi}M_{\frac{\omega-\eta}{2}}T_{x}M_{\frac{\omega-\eta}{2}}\varphi\rangle

(7.155)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i\eta\cdot\xi}e^{\pi i\eta\cdot x}e^{-\pi i\xi\cdot(\omega-\eta)}\langle\varphi,M_{\frac{\omega-\eta}{2}}T_{x-\xi}M_{\frac{\omega-\eta}{2}}\varphi\rangle

(7.156)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi i(x\cdot\eta-\xi\cdot\omega)}\Phi(x-\xi,\omega-\eta).

(7.157)

(b)

According to the definition of the twisted convolution (
7.94
), we have

\Phi\natural\Phi^{\xi,\eta}(x,\omega)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\langle\varphi,\rho(x^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})\varphi\rangle\langle\rho(\xi,\eta)\varphi,\rho(x-x^{\prime},\omega-\omega^{\prime})\varphi\rangle e^{\pi i(x\cdot\omega^{\prime}-x^{\prime}\cdot\omega)}\,d(x^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}).

(7.158)

By (
7.110
),
\langle\varphi,\rho(x^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})\varphi\rangle=e^{\pi ix^{\prime}\cdot\omega^{\prime}}V_{\varphi}\varphi(x^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
and, by a similar calculation

\displaystyle\langle\rho(\xi,\eta)\varphi,\rho(x-x^{\prime},\omega-\omega^{\prime})\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\rho(\xi,\eta)\varphi,e^{\pi i(x\cdot\omega^{\prime}-x^{\prime}\cdot\omega)}\rho(-x^{\prime},-\omega^{\prime})\rho(x,\omega)\varphi\rangle\quad\text{by }\eqref{eq_Hr_composition}

(7.159)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i(x\cdot\omega^{\prime}-x^{\prime}\cdot\omega)}\langle\rho(x^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})\rho(\xi,\eta)\varphi,\rho(x,\omega)\varphi\rangle

(7.160)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i(x\cdot\omega^{\prime}-x^{\prime}\cdot\omega)}e^{-\pi ix^{\prime}\cdot\omega^{\prime}}\overline{V_{\rho(\xi,\eta)\varphi}(\rho(x,\omega)\varphi)(x^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})}.

(7.161)

Substituting these expressions into (
7.158
), all the complex exponentials cancel and the twisted convolution becomes an inner product of two STFTs. We will use the orthogonality relations (
2.27
) to finish the proof.

\displaystyle\Phi\natural\Phi^{\xi,\eta}(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{\varphi}\varphi(x^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})\overline{V_{\rho(\xi,\eta)\varphi}(\rho(x,\omega)\varphi)(x^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})}\,d(x^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})

(7.162)

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi,\rho(x,\omega)\varphi\rangle\overline{\langle\varphi,\rho(\xi,\eta)\varphi\rangle}

(7.163)

\displaystyle=\Phi(\xi,\eta)\Phi(x,\omega)

(7.164)

\displaystyle=e^{-\tfrac{\pi}{2}(\xi^{2}+\eta^{2})}\Phi(x,\omega).

(7.165)

(c)

\displaystyle\pi(\xi,\eta,0)\pi(\Phi)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\Phi(x,\omega)\pi(\xi,\eta,0)\pi(x,\omega,0)\,d(x,\omega)

(7.166)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}e^{\pi i(x\cdot\eta-\xi\cdot\omega)}\Phi(x,\omega)\pi(x+\xi,\omega+\eta,0)\,d(x,\omega)

(7.167)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}e^{\pi i(x\cdot\eta-\xi\cdot\omega)}\Phi(x-\xi,\omega-\eta)\pi(x,\omega)\,d(x,\omega)

(7.168)

\displaystyle=\pi(\Phi^{\xi,\eta}),

(7.169)

by (a).

∎

Note in particular that the above lemma shows that
\Phi\natural\Phi=\Phi
. This means that the twisted convolution operator
F\mapsto F\natural\Phi
is a projection. For a given representation
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
of
\mathbf{H}
, this observation will help us to define a subspace of
\mathcal{H}
which corresponds to the Gaussians in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

We can now prove the Stone-von Neumann theorem.

Proof.

(of the Stone-von Neumann theorem
7.32
)
Let
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
be an irreducible unitary representation of
\mathbf{H}
. Since

\pi(0,0,\tau)\pi(x,\omega,\tau^{\prime})=\pi(x,\omega,\tau+\tau^{\prime})=\pi(x,\omega,\tau^{\prime})\pi(0,0,\tau),

(7.170)

Schur’s lemma implies that
\pi(0,0,\tau)=\chi(\tau)I_{\mathcal{H}}
, for some
\chi(\tau)\in\mathbb{C}
.

As
\tau\mapsto\pi(0,0,\tau)
is a homomorphism of
\mathbb{R}
into the group of unitary operators we must have
|\chi(\tau)|=1
and
\chi(\tau_{1}+\tau_{2})=\chi(\tau_{1})\chi(\tau_{2})
. Therefore,
\chi
is of the form
\chi(\tau)=e^{2\pi i\kappa\tau}
for some
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. We distinguish two cases.

Case I:
\kappa=0
. We see that
\pi(x,\omega,\tau)
no longer depends on
\tau
. Thus,
\pi
induces an irreducible representation
\widetilde{\pi}(x,\omega)=\pi(x,\omega,\tau)
of the Abelian group
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. Now, all operators
\widetilde{\pi}(x,\omega)
commute and by Schur’s lemma
7.33

\widetilde{\pi}(x,\omega)=\chi(x,\omega)I_{\mathcal{H}}
. Therefore,
\chi
must be a character of the form
\chi_{a,b}(x,\omega)=e^{2\pi i(a\cdot x+b\cdot\omega)}
. Since every subspace is invariant under the action of the identity operator, the irreducibility forces
\mathcal{H}
to be one-dimensional.

Case II:
\kappa\neq 0
. By considering
\pi(\delta_{\kappa}^{-1}(x,\omega,\tau))=\pi(\tfrac{x}{\kappa},\omega,\tfrac{\tau}{\kappa})
we may assume, without loss of generality, that
\kappa=1
. We note that this representation satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
7.31
and Proposition
7.34
(
c
).

We look at the integrated representation of
\pi
and its action on the Gaussians
\Phi(x,\omega)=\langle\varphi,\rho(x,\omega)\varphi\rangle
and
\Phi^{\xi,\eta}(x,\omega)=\langle\rho(\xi,\eta)\varphi,\rho(x,\omega)\varphi\rangle
, which are representation coefficients of the Schrödinger representation. Theorem
7.31
(
ii
) and Proposition
7.30
(
c
) imply that
\pi(\Phi)\neq 0
and that
\pi(\Phi)=\pi(\Phi)^{*}
. Now, Lemma
7.34
furnishes the fundamental identity

\pi(\Phi)\pi(\xi,\eta,0)\pi(\Phi)=\pi(\Phi)\pi(\Phi^{\xi,\eta})=\pi(\Phi\natural\Phi^{\xi,\eta})=e^{-\tfrac{\pi}{2}(\xi^{2}+\eta^{2})}\pi(\Phi).

(7.171)

Thus, a Gaussian appears outside of
\pi(\Phi)
almost by magic. Further, for
(\xi,\eta)=(0,0)
, we have
\pi(\Phi)^{2}=\pi(\Phi)
and so
\pi(\Phi)
is a non-zero, orthogonal projection.

Now, choose a normalized vector
g\in\pi(\Phi)\mathcal{H}
. Then
g=\pi(\Phi)g
and
\lVert g\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\langle g,\pi(\Phi)g\rangle
. Using Lemma
7.34
and (
7.171
) (several times), we obtain

\displaystyle\langle\pi(\xi,\eta,0)g,\pi(x,\omega,0)g\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\pi(\Phi)g,\pi(-\xi,-\eta,0)\pi(x,\omega,0)\pi(\Phi)g\rangle

(7.172)

\displaystyle=\langle\pi(\Phi)g,\underbrace{\pi(x-\xi,\omega-\eta,\tfrac{1}{2}(\xi\cdot\omega-x\cdot\eta))}_{\pi(0,0,\frac{1}{2}(\xi\cdot\omega-x\cdot\eta))\pi(x-\xi,\omega-\eta,0)}\pi(\Phi)g\rangle

(7.173)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi i(x\cdot\eta-\xi\cdot\omega)}\langle g,\pi(\Phi)\pi(x-\xi,\omega-\eta,0)\pi(\Phi)g\rangle

(7.174)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi i(x\cdot\eta-\xi\cdot\omega)}e^{-\tfrac{\pi}{2}\left((x-\xi)^{2}+(\omega-\eta)^{2}\right)}\langle g,\pi(\Phi)g\rangle

(7.175)

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\xi,\eta}(x,\omega)

(7.176)

\displaystyle=\langle\rho(\xi,\eta)\varphi,\rho(x,\omega)\varphi\rangle.

(7.177)

Now, we define an operator
U
in the following way.

U\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}\pi(x_{k},\omega_{k},0)g\Big)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}\rho(x_{k},\omega_{k})\varphi.

(7.178)

Then,
U
is defined on the subspace
\mathcal{H}_{g}\subset\mathcal{H}
spanned by finite linear combinations of
\pi(x,\omega,0)g
. Since
\pi
is irreducible by assumption,
\mathcal{H}_{g}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
by Schur’s lemma
7.33
. Also, by Theorem
7.27
the Schrödinger representation is irreducible, hence,
U
has dense range in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We will now show, using (
7.172
), that
U
is an isometry on
\mathcal{H}_{g}
, since

\displaystyle\lVert U\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}\pi(x_{k},\omega_{k},0)g\Big)\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}\rho(x_{k},\omega_{k})\varphi\rVert_{2}^{2}

(7.179)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{n}c_{k}\overline{c_{l}}\langle\rho(x_{k},\omega_{k})\varphi,\rho(x_{l},\omega_{l})\varphi\rangle

(7.180)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{n}c_{k}\overline{c_{l}}\langle\pi(x_{k},\omega_{k},0)g,\pi(x_{l},\omega_{l},0)g\rangle

(7.181)

\displaystyle=\lVert\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}\pi(x_{k},\omega_{k},0)g\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

(7.182)

Consequently,
U
extends to a unitary operator from
\mathcal{H}
onto
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Finally, we verify that
U
intertwines the representations
\pi
and
\rho
. Let

f=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}\pi(x_{k},\omega_{k},0)g\in\mathcal{H}_{g}.

(7.183)

Then

\displaystyle U\pi(x,\omega,\tau)f

\displaystyle=U\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}e^{2\pi i\tau}\pi(x,\omega,0)\pi(x_{k},\omega_{k},0)g\Big)

(7.184)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}e^{2\pi i\tau}\rho(x,\omega)\rho(x_{k},\omega_{k})\varphi

(7.185)

\displaystyle=\rho(x,\omega,\tau)Uf.

(7.186)

By continuity, the above calculations extend from
\mathcal{H}_{g}
to
\mathcal{H}
. Thus, we have shown that any irreducible representation
\pi
of
\mathbf{H}
with
\pi(x,\omega,\tau)=e^{2\pi i\tau}\pi(x,\omega,0)
is unitarily equivalent to the Schrödinger representation
\rho
.
∎

A slight variation in the above proof yields the following generalization of the Stone-von Neumann theorem.

Theorem 7.35
.

If
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
is a unitary (irreducible) representation of
\mathbf{H}
, such that
\pi(x,\omega,\tau)=e^{2\pi i\tau}\pi(x,\omega,0)
, then
\pi
is a (finite or infinite) direct sum of representations equivalent to
\rho
.

Proof.

Choose an orthonormal basis
\{g_{j}\mid j\in J\}
of the subspace
\pi(\Phi)\mathcal{H}
. Then, in (
7.172
) replacing
g
by
g_{j}
and
g_{k}
shows that
\mathcal{H}_{g_{j}}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{H}_{g_{k}}
for
j\neq k
. Furthermore, the same proof as above shows that
\mathcal{H}_{g_{j}}
is equivalent to
\rho
.

We need to show that
\mathcal{H}=\oplus_{j\in J}\mathcal{H}_{g_{j}}
. If
\mathcal{K}=\left(\oplus_{j\in J}\mathcal{H}_{g_{j}}\right)^{\perp}\neq\{0\}
, then
\pi
restricted to
\mathcal{K}
satisfies the hypotheses of the theorem , and
\pi(\Phi)\mathcal{K}\neq\{0\}
. But this contradicts the choice of the orthonormal basis
\{g_{j}\}
, thus
\mathcal{K}=\{0\}
.
∎

As a consequence of the Stone-von Neumann theorem we obtain the following result.

Corollary 7.36
.

Every irreducible unitary representation of
\mathbf{H}_{r}
is equivalent to exactly one of the following representations

(i)

\chi_{a,b}(x,\omega,e^{2\pi i\tau})=\chi_{a,b}(x,\omega)
acting on
\mathbb{C}
, for some
(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, or

(ii)

\rho_{k}(x,\omega,e^{2\pi i\tau})=e^{2\pi ik\tau}e^{\pi ikx\cdot\omega}T_{kx}M_{\omega}=e^{2\pi ik\tau}M_{\omega/2}T_{kx}M_{\omega_{2}}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
.

Proof.

Every unitary representation of
\mathbf{H}_{r}
extends to a unitary representation of
\mathbf{H}
. Therefore, the irreducible unitary representations of
\mathbf{H}_{r}
occur among the list of
\chi_{a,b}
and
\pi_{\kappa}
,
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
. Conversely, a representation
\pi
of
\mathbf{H}
yields a representation of
\mathbf{H}_{r}\cong\mathbf{H}/(\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z})
, if and only if
\ker(\pi)\supset\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}
. Among the irreducible representations, these are exactly
\chi_{a,b}
and
\rho_{k}
.
∎

Remark
.

We note that in the proof of the Stone-von Neumann theorem we have (implicitly) used Plancherel’s theorem at several points.

Now, let

\pi(x,\omega,\tau)=\rho(\omega,-x,\tau)=e^{2\pi i\tau}e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}M_{-x}T_{\omega}.

(7.187)

Then,
\pi
acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
yields an irreducible unitary representation of
\mathbf{H}
with
\pi(0,0,\tau)=e^{2\pi i\tau}I
. Therefore, it is unitarily equivalent to the Schrödinger representation
\rho
and there exists a unitary operator
U
(on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
), such that

U^{-1}\rho(x,\omega,\tau)U=\pi(x,\omega,\tau).

(7.188)

We compute the effect of the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}
on
\rho
;

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\rho(x,\omega,\tau)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi i\tau}e^{-\pi ix\cdot\omega}\mathcal{F}M_{\omega}T_{x}=e^{2\pi i\tau}e^{-\pi ix\cdot\omega}T_{\omega}M_{-x}\mathcal{F}

(7.189)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi i\tau}e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}M_{-x}T_{\omega}\mathcal{F}=\pi(x,\omega,\tau)\mathcal{F}.

(7.190)

Thus,
U\mathcal{F}\rho(\mathbf{h})=U\pi(\mathbf{h})\mathcal{F}=\rho(\mathbf{h})U\mathcal{F}
. By Schur’s Lemma
7.33
,
U\mathcal{F}=cI
, or
\mathcal{F}=cU^{-1}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}
is a multiple of a unitary operator. So we have to compute a concrete Fourier transform (any one) in order to determine
c
. We choose the example of the Gaussian
g(t)=e^{-\pi t^{2}}
(see again
0.8
) and get

\mathcal{F}g(t)=g(t).

(7.191)

Hence,
c=1
and we see that
\mathcal{F}
is indeed unitary, i.e., we obtain Plancherel’s theorem.

At this point, it should be mentioned that we used a slightly more general form of Schur’s lemma, as given in
[
28
, p. 823]
: If you have an intertwining operator between irreducible representations, which is densely defined and its adjoint is densely defined (the operator is said to be closable), it will be unitary up to a scalar.

In a similar fashion we can derive the inversion formula for the Fourier transform. Recall that
f^{\vee}(t)=f(-t)
. Then

\displaystyle\left(\rho(x,\omega,\tau)f\right)^{\vee}(t)=e^{2\pi i\tau}e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}T_{-x}M_{-\omega}f(-t)=\rho(-x,-\omega,\tau)f^{\vee}(t).

(7.192)

So
{}^{\vee}\rho(x,\omega,\tau)=\rho(-x-\omega,\tau)^{\vee}
, if we denote the reflection operator by
∨
as well. Composition with
\mathcal{F}^{2}
yields

{}^{\vee}\mathcal{F}^{2}\rho(x,\omega,\tau)=\,^{\vee}\mathcal{F}\rho(\omega,-x,\tau)\mathcal{F}=\,^{\vee}\rho(-x,-\omega,\tau)\mathcal{F}^{2}=\rho(x,\omega,\tau)^{\vee}\mathcal{F}^{2}.

(7.193)

Again, Schur’s Lemma
7.33
gives
{}^{\vee}\mathcal{F}^{2}=cI
and again
c=1
. Written out, the identity
{}^{\vee}\mathcal{F}^{2}=I
is

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{F}f(\omega)e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}\,d\omega=f(x).

(7.194)

\diamond

7.4
The Metaplectic Group

Now, we are going to present a group of unitary operators, the so-called metaplectic operators. We will see that these operators have generators which are closely related to the generators of the symplectic group. In fact, the metaplectic group
Mp(\mathbb{R},2d)
is a (reducible) representation of the (connected) two-fold cover of the symplectic group
Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
. This is the fastest way of introducing (and defining) the metaplectic group. Equivalently, one can define it by saying that the sequence of group homomorphisms

0\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to Mp(\mathbb{R},2d)\to Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)\to 0

(7.195)

is exact. This means that the image of each homomorphism is the kernel of the next one.

However, we are going to use a more constructive approach to the metaplectic group. Since every lattice in the time-frequency plane is of the form
\Lambda=M\mathbb{Z}^{2d}
for some
M\in GL(\mathbb{R},2d)
, it is first necessary to understand which of the automorphisms
z\mapsto Mz
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,
z=(x,\omega)
, extend to automorphisms
\mathbf{a}_{M}(z,\tau)=(Mz,\tau)
of
\mathbf{H}
. These are exactly the automorphisms where the matrix is symplectic, because

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{M}(z,\tau)\mathbf{a}_{M}(z^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})=(Mz,\tau)(Mz^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})=(M(z+z^{\prime}),\tau+\tau^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma(Mz,Mz^{\prime}))

(7.196)

and

\mathbf{a}_{M}((z,\tau)(z^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))=\mathbf{a}_{M}(z+z^{\prime},\tau+\tau^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma(z,z^{\prime}))=(M(z+z^{\prime}),\tau+\tau^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma(z,z^{\prime})).

(7.197)

By composing the Schrödinger representation with an automorphism
\mathbf{a}_{S}
,
S\in Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
, we obtain a new representation
\rho_{S}=\rho\circ\mathbf{a}_{S}
defined by

\rho_{S}(z,\tau)=\rho(Sz,\tau).

(7.198)

This representation has the following properties. First,
\rho_{S}
is irreducible because
\rho(Sz)
still runs through all time-frequency shifts and, second,
\rho_{S}(0,0,\tau)=\rho(0,0,\tau)=e^{2\pi i\tau}I_{L^{2}}
.

By the Stone-von Neumann theorem the representations
\rho
and
\rho_{S}
are equivalent and there exists a unitary operator
\mu(S)
such that

\rho(Sz,\tau)=\rho_{S}(z,\tau)=\mu(S)\rho(z,\tau)\mu(S)^{-1}.

(7.199)

We will start with computing how the Schrödinger representation interacts with 3 important unitary operators.

Example 7.37
.

(a)

We start with the Fourier transform operator
\mathcal{F}
and how it interacts with the symmetric time-frequency shifts
\rho(x,\omega)=\rho(x,\omega,0)
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\rho(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}\mathcal{F}T_{x}M_{\omega}=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}M_{-x}T_{\omega}\mathcal{F}

(7.200)

\displaystyle=M_{-x/2}T_{\omega}M_{-x/2}\mathcal{F}=\rho(J(x,\omega))\mathcal{F}

(7.201)

Thus, the representation
\rho(J(x,\omega),\tau)
is unitarily equivalent to the Schrödinger representation with the Fourier transform as intertwining operator.

(b)

Next, we consider the dilation operator
\mathcal{D}_{L}f(t)=|\det(L)|^{-1/2}f(L^{-1}t)
, with
\det(L)\neq 0
. This time, we use the standard time-frequency shifts
\pi(x,\omega)=M_{\omega}T_{x}
, instead. This simplifies the notation in the following computation

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{L}M_{\omega}T_{x}f(t)

\displaystyle=|\det(L)|^{-1/2}e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot(L^{-1}t)}f(L^{-1}t-x)

(7.202)

\displaystyle=|\det(L)|^{-1/2}e^{2\pi i(L^{-T}\omega)\cdot t}f(L^{-1}(t-Lx))

(7.203)

\displaystyle=M_{L^{-T}\omega}T_{Lx}\mathcal{D}_{L}f(t).

(7.204)

Thus, we obtain that
\rho(x,\omega,\tau)
and
\rho(D_{L}(x,\omega),\tau)
,
D_{L}=\begin{pmatrix}L&0\\
0&L^{-T}\end{pmatrix}
, are unitarily equivalent.

(c)

Finally, consider the linear chirp operator
\mathcal{V}_{Q}f(t)=e^{\pi iQt\cdot t}f(t)
,
Q^{T}=Q
. We compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{Q}\,\rho(x,\omega)\,\mathcal{V}_{Q}^{-1}f(t)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi iQt\cdot t}e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{2\pi i\omega\cdot(t-x)}e^{-\pi iQ(t-x)\cdot(t-x)}f(t-x)

(7.205)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi i(\omega+Qx)\cdot x}e^{2\pi i(\omega+Qx)\cdot(t-x)}f(t-x)

(7.206)

\displaystyle=\rho(x,Qx+\omega,\tau)f(t)=\rho(V_{Q}(x,\omega),\tau)f(t),

(7.207)

where
V_{Q}=\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
Q&I\end{pmatrix}
.

In the examples, we have identified the intertwining operators of the Schrödinger representation for the generator matrices
J
,
D_{L}
and
V_{Q}
of the symplectic group. These are, up to phase factors, the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}
, the dilation operator
\mathcal{D}_{L}
and the linear chirp
\mathcal{V}_{Q}
, respectively. We note that (
7.199
) actually defines a whole class of unitary operators
\{c\mu(S)\mid|c|=1\}
. The choice of the phase factor is not important as long as we consider a single operator
\mu(S_{1})
, but it becomes crucial for their composition. Indeed, for
S_{1},S_{2}\in Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
and a particular choice of
\mu(S_{1})
and
\mu(S_{2})
, and
\mu(S_{1}S_{2})
, the repeated application of (
7.199
) yields

\mu(S_{1}S_{2})^{-1}\mu(S_{1})\mu(S_{2})\,\rho(x,\omega)\,\mu(S_{2})^{-1}\mu(S_{1})^{-1}\mu(S_{1}S_{2})=\rho((S_{1}S_{2})^{-1}S_{1}S_{2}(x,\omega))=\rho(x,\omega).

(7.208)

Schur’s lemma, however, only implies that
\mu(S_{1}S_{2})=c\mu(S_{1})\mu(S_{2})
, for some
c\in\mathbb{C}
,
|c|=1
, but we cannot assert that
c=1
. We might hope to adjust the phase factor for each
\mu(S)
so that
S\mapsto\mu(S)
becomes a homomorphism of
Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
. In fact, the phase factors of the operators
\mu(S)
can be chosen such that either
\mu(S_{1}S_{2})=\mu(S_{1})\mu(S_{2})
or
\mu(S_{1}S_{2})=-\mu(S_{1})\mu(S_{2})
, but this is the only freedom we have. Assuming we made the correct choice for the phase factors (up to
\pm 1
),
\mu
is then called the metaplectic representation of
Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
, which is a double-valued unitary representation of
Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
.

The fastidious way (mentioned at the beginning of the section) to deal with the double-valuedness is to pass to the double covering group of
Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
, called the metaplectic group denoted by
Mp(\mathbb{R},2d)
.
15
15

15

Mp(\mathbb{R},2d)
might as well be denoted by
Mp(\mathbb{R},d)
or
Sp_{2}(\mathbb{R},2d)
or
Sp_{2}(\mathbb{R},d)
.

As promised, we will however choose a more constructive approach.

After these preliminaries, we will define a group of unitary operators, namely quadratic Fourier transforms, closely connected to the symplectic matrices. Let

S=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}\in Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)\quad\text{ and }\det(A)\neq 0.

(7.209)

Recall that, by Proposition
7.7
, a symplectic matrix with
\det(A)\neq 0
can be decomposed into the building blocks
V_{Q}
,
D_{L}
and
J
. In particular, the matrix

V_{P}D_{L}JV_{-Q}J^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}L&LQ\\
PL&PLQ+L^{-T}\end{pmatrix}

(7.210)

is symplectic,
P=P^{T}
,
Q=Q^{T}
and
\det(L)\neq 0
. Next, we note that

SJ^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}B&-A\\
D&-C\end{pmatrix},

(7.211)

We might as well consider the decomposition
S_{W}=V_{P}D_{L}JV_{Q}=\begin{pmatrix}LQ&L\\
PLQ-L^{-T}&PL\end{pmatrix}
. Then, this decomposes matrices of the form
S=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}
with
\det(B)\neq 0
. There is an associated quadratic form
W(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}Px^{2}-L^{-1}x\cdot x^{\prime}+Qx^{\prime 2}
, called the generating function
16
16

16

The term “generating function” comes from classical mechanics and need not concern us any further.
of the matrix
S_{W}
. With this notion we define the quadratic Fourier transform.

Definition 7.38
.

For
W(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}Px^{2}-L^{-1}x\cdot x^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}Qx^{\prime 2}
, the operator

\widehat{S}_{W,m}f(x)=(-i)^{d/2}i^{m}\sqrt{|\det(L^{-1})|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x^{\prime})e^{2\pi iW(x,x^{\prime})}\,dx^{\prime},

(7.212)

is called the quadratic Fourier transform. The integer
m\in\{0,1,2,3\}
is called the Maslov index
17
17

17

Of course, if
m
is an appropriate choice of the Maslov index, then
m+2
is an equally good choice, but
\widehat{S}_{W,m}=-\widehat{S}_{W,m+2}
. Thus (
7.212
) associates two operators to each quadratic form
W
.
and is chosen such that

m\pi\equiv\arg(\det(L^{-1}))\mod 2\pi.

(7.213)

We will see that
\mu(S_{W})=\widehat{S}_{W,m}
. First, we start by assigning the appropriate phase factors (up to
\pm 1
) to the representations of the generator matrices. We start with the standard symplectic matrix. In this case, the quadratic form becomes
W(x,x^{\prime})=-x\cdot x^{\prime}
and we have

\widehat{J}f(x)=\mu(J)f(x)=(-i)^{d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x^{\prime})e^{-2\pi ix\cdot x^{\prime}}\,dx^{\prime}=(-i)^{d/2}\mathcal{F}f(x).

(7.214)

We note that

\widehat{J}^{-1}=\mu(J^{-1})=i^{d/2}\mathcal{F}^{-1}.

(7.215)

The metaplectic operator of the matrix
D_{L}
is given by

\widehat{D}_{L,m}f(x)=\mu(D_{L})f(x)=i^{m}\sqrt{|\det(L^{-1})|}\,f(L^{-1}x)=i^{m}\mathcal{D}_{L}f(x),\quad\det(L)\neq 0.

(7.216)

Finally, the metaplectic operator of the matrix
V_{Q}
is given by

\widehat{V}_{Q}f(x)=\mu(V_{Q})f(x)=e^{\pi iQx^{2}}f(x),\quad Q=Q^{T}.

(7.217)

We have the following factorization result for the quadratic Fourier transforms, similar to the factorization of symplectic matrices with
\det(B)\neq 0
(or
\det(A)\neq 0
).

Proposition 7.39
.

Let
W(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}Px^{2}-L^{-1}x\cdot x^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}Qx^{\prime 2}
.

(i)

We have the factorization

\widehat{S}_{W,m}=\widehat{V}_{P}\widehat{D}_{L,m}\widehat{J}\widehat{V}_{Q}.

(7.218)

(ii)

The operators
\widehat{S}_{W,m}
are unitary (extend to unitary operators) on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The inverse operator of
\widehat{S}_{W,m}
is given by

\widehat{S}_{W,m}^{-1}=\widehat{S}_{W^{*},m^{*}}\quad\text{ and }\quad W^{*}(x,x^{\prime})=-W(x^{\prime},x),m^{*}=d-m.

(7.219)

Proof.

(i)

By definition we have

\widehat{J}f(x)=(-i)^{d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x^{\prime})e^{-2\pi ix\cdot x^{\prime}}\,dx^{\prime}.

(7.220)

We note that

\widehat{D}_{L,m}\widehat{J}f(x)=(-i)^{d/2}i^{m}\sqrt{|\det(L^{-1})|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x^{\prime})e^{-2\pi iL^{-1}x\cdot x^{\prime}}\,dx^{\prime}.

(7.221)

Applying the chirps yields

\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{W,m}f(x)

\displaystyle=\widehat{V}_{P}\widehat{D}_{L,m}\widehat{J}\widehat{V}_{Q}f(x)

(7.222)

\displaystyle=(-i)^{d/2}i^{m}\sqrt{|\det(L^{-1})|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x^{\prime})e^{2\pi i\left(\tfrac{1}{2}Px^{2}-L^{-1}x\cdot x^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{2}Qx^{\prime 2}\right)}\,dx^{\prime}.

(7.223)

(ii)

The operators
\widehat{V}_{Q}
and
\widehat{D}_{L,m}
are unitary and, like
\mathcal{F}
, the operator
\widehat{J}
extends to a unitary operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

It is easy to see that

\widehat{V}_{Q}^{-1}=\widehat{V}_{-Q}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widehat{D}_{L,m}^{-1}=\widehat{D}_{L^{-1},-m}.

(7.224)

We compute

\displaystyle\widehat{J}^{-1}\widehat{D}_{L^{-1},-m}

\displaystyle=i^{d/2}i^{-m}\sqrt{|\det(L)|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(Lx^{\prime})e^{2\pi ix\cdot x^{\prime}}\,dx^{\prime}

(7.225)

\displaystyle=(-i)^{d/2}i^{d-m}\sqrt{|\det(L^{-1})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x^{\prime})e^{2\pi iL^{-T}x\cdot x^{\prime}}\,dx^{\prime}

(7.226)

\displaystyle=\widehat{D}_{-L^{-T},d-m}\widehat{J}f(x).

(7.227)

From this we obtain the result by noting that

\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{W,m}^{-1}f(x)

\displaystyle=\widehat{V}_{Q}^{-1}\widehat{J}^{-1}\widehat{D}_{L,m}^{-1}\widehat{V}_{P}^{-1}f(x)=\widehat{V}_{-Q}\widehat{D}_{-L^{-T},d-m}\widehat{J}\widehat{V}_{-P}f(x)

(7.228)

\displaystyle=(-i)^{d/2}i^{d-m}\sqrt{|\det(L^{-1})|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x^{\prime})e^{2\pi i\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}Qx^{2}+x\cdot L^{-1}x^{\prime}-\tfrac{1}{2}Px^{\prime 2}\right)}\,dx^{\prime}.

(7.229)

∎

From the proposition above, it follows that the operators
\widehat{S}_{W,m}
are unitary and are thus a subset of
\mathcal{U}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. By adding the identity operator to this subset, we see by the Proposition that this subset is closed under inversion and, thus forms a subgroup. Adding the identity operators is necessary as it cannot be represented by an operator
\widehat{S}_{W,m}
because
\det(B)=0
for the identity matrix.

Definition 7.40
.

The group generated by the quadratic Fourier transforms
\widehat{S}_{W,m}
and the identity operators is called the metaplectic group, denoted by
Mp(\mathbb{R},2d)
. The elements of
Mp(\mathbb{R},2d)
are called metaplectic operators.

Every
\widehat{S}\in Mp(\mathbb{R},2d)
is thus, by definition a product
\widehat{S}_{W_{1},m_{1}}\ldots\widehat{S}_{W_{k},m_{k}}
of quadratic Fourier transforms associated to symplectic matrices with
\det(B)\neq 0
. Similarly to the result we had on symplectic matrices, we get a characterization of all metaplectic operators, which we state without proof and refer to
[
16
, Chap. 7]
.

Theorem 7.41
.

Every
\widehat{S}\in Mp(\mathbb{R},2d)
can be written as a product of two quadratic Fourier transforms
\widehat{S}_{W,m}
and
\widehat{S}_{W^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
.

We note that this factorization is not unique. As a corollary we obtain the following decomposition result.

Corollary 7.42
.

The metaplectic group is generated by the operators
\widehat{V}_{Q}
,
\widehat{D}_{L,m}
and
\widehat{J}
.

We end our study of the metaplectic group with the natural projection map.

Theorem 7.43
.

The mapping
\widehat{S}_{W,m}\mapsto S_{W}
, which to a quadratic Fourier transform

\widehat{S}_{W,m}f(x)=(-i)^{d/2}i^{m}\sqrt{|\det(L^{-1})|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)e^{2\pi iW(x,x^{\prime})}\,dx^{\prime}

(7.230)

associates a symplectic matrix with
\det(B)\neq 0
and generating function
W
, extends into a surjective group homomorphism

\pi^{Mp}:Mp(\mathbb{R},2d)\to Sp(\mathbb{R},2d),

(7.231)

i.e.,

\pi^{Mp}(\widehat{S}_{1}\widehat{S}_{2})=\pi^{Mp}(\widehat{S}_{1})\pi^{Mp}(\widehat{S}_{2}).

(7.232)

The kernel of
\pi^{Mp}
is

\ker(\pi^{Mp})=\{\pm I\}.

(7.233)

Hence,
\pi^{Mp}:Mp(\mathbb{R},2d)\to Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
is a twofold covering of the symplectic group.

To end this whole chapter, we will consider some consequences for the STFT as well as for Gabor systems.

Lemma 7.44
.

Let
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
S\in Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
. Set
(x^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})=S(x,\omega)
. Then

V_{g}f(S(x,\omega))=e^{\pi i(x\cdot\omega-x^{\prime}\cdot\omega^{\prime})}V_{\widehat{S}^{-1}g}(\widehat{S}^{-1}f)(x,\omega),

(7.234)

where
\widehat{S}=\mu(S)
.

Proof.

The proof is by direct computation.

\displaystyle V_{g}f(S(x,\omega))

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi ix^{\prime}\cdot\omega^{\prime}}\langle f,\rho(S(x,\omega))g\rangle

(7.235)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi ix^{\prime}\cdot\omega^{\prime}}\langle f,\widehat{S}\rho(x,\omega)\widehat{S}^{-1}g\rangle

(7.236)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi i(x\cdot\omega-x^{\prime}\cdot\omega^{\prime})}\langle\widehat{S}^{-1}f,\pi(x,\omega)\widehat{S}^{-1}g\rangle

(7.237)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi i(x\cdot\omega-x^{\prime}\cdot\omega^{\prime})}V_{\widehat{S}^{-1}g}(\widehat{S}^{-1}f)(x,\omega).

(7.238)

∎

This leads to the following theorem.

Theorem 7.45
.

The Gabor system
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
is a frame if and only if
\mathcal{G}(\widehat{S}g,S\Lambda)
is a frame. In this case, both systems possess the same frame bounds.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in S\Lambda}|\langle f,\rho(\lambda)\widehat{S}g\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|\langle f,\rho(S\lambda)\widehat{S}g\rangle|^{2}

(7.239)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|\langle f,\widehat{S}\rho(\lambda)\widehat{S}^{-1}\widehat{S}g\rangle|^{2}

(7.240)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|\langle\widehat{S}^{-1}f,\rho(\lambda)g\rangle|^{2}.

(7.241)

Now,
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
is a frame, i.e.,

A\lVert\widehat{S}^{-1}f\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|\langle\widehat{S}^{-1}f,\rho(\lambda)g\rangle|^{2}\leq B\lVert\widehat{S}^{-1}f\rVert_{2}^{2},\qquad\forall f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(7.242)

But, we may exchange the middle expression in the frame inequality to get

A\lVert\widehat{S}^{-1}f\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\sum_{\lambda\in S\Lambda}|\langle f,\rho(\lambda)\widehat{S}g\rangle|^{2}\leq B\lVert\widehat{S}^{-1}f\rVert_{2}^{2},\qquad\forall f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(7.243)

Since
\lVert\widehat{S}^{-1}f\rVert_{2}^{2}=\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}
the result follows.
∎

We also note the following property for the Gabor frame operator.

Proposition 7.46
.

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,
S\in Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
and
\widehat{S}=\mu(S)
. Let
S_{g,\Lambda}
be the frame operator of the Gabor frame
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
. Then

S_{\widehat{S}g,S\Lambda}=\widehat{S}S_{g,\Lambda}\widehat{S}^{-1}

(7.244)

Proof.

We expand the Gabor frame operator

\displaystyle S_{\widehat{S}g,S\Lambda}f

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in S\Lambda}\langle f,\rho(\lambda)\widehat{S}g\rangle\rho(\lambda)\widehat{S}g

(7.245)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\langle f,\rho(S\lambda)\widehat{S}g\rangle\rho(S\lambda)\widehat{S}g

(7.246)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\langle f,\widehat{S}\rho(\lambda)\widehat{S}^{-1}\widehat{S}g\rangle\widehat{S}\rho(\lambda)\widehat{S}^{-1}\widehat{S}g

(7.247)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\langle\widehat{S}^{-1}f,\rho(\lambda)g\rangle\widehat{S}\rho(\lambda)g

(7.248)

\displaystyle=\widehat{S}S_{g,\Lambda}\widehat{S}^{-1}f

(7.249)

As we assumed that
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
is a frame, the interchange of summation and the action of the operator
\widehat{S}
from the second to last line to the last line is justified by Lemma
6.5
, as the sum converges unconditionally.
∎

8
Function Spaces

We will now introduce function spaces commonly used in time-frequency analysis. For this purpose, we first introduce spaces of smooth functions and some related notation before passing to a crash course on distribution theory. Besides
[
19
]
, the book by Bényi and Okoudjou
[
1
]
serves as a resource for this chapter.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
we write
|\alpha|=\sum_{{}_{k}=1}^{d}\alpha_{k}
,
\alpha!=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\alpha_{k}!
,
x^{\alpha}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}x_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}
and
\partial^{\alpha}
for the higher order partial derivative operator
\prod_{k=1}^{d}\partial_{x_{k}}^{\alpha_{k}}
, where
\partial_{x_{k}}^{\alpha_{k}}=\frac{\partial^{\alpha_{k}}}{\partial_{x_{k}}^{\alpha_{k}}}
. Furthermore, we write
\alpha\leq\beta
if
\alpha_{k}\leq\beta_{k}
for all
k=1,\ldots,d
.

Let
\Omega
be an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Definition 8.1
.

The collection of all functions
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\partial^{\alpha}f
is continuous for all multi-indices
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\leq k
is denoted by
C^{k}(\Omega)
and

C^{\infty}(\Omega)=\bigcap_{k=0}^{\infty}C^{k}(\Omega).

(8.1)

C^{\infty}_{c}
denotes the collection of all functions
f\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
with compact support in
\Omega
.

We note that for two functions
f,g\in C^{|\alpha|}(\Omega)
the product rule holds;

\partial^{\alpha}(fg)=\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!(\alpha-\beta)!}(\partial^{\beta}f)(\partial^{\alpha-\beta}g).

(8.2)

We note that these spaces are examples of Fréchet spaces, which are complete metric spaces, that are not normable. For example, the topology on
C^{\infty}(\Omega)
is that of uniform convergence of a function and all its derivatives on compact sets; more precisely, the topology of
C^{\infty}(\Omega)
is given by seminorms

p_{l,m}(f)=\sup\{|\partial^{\alpha}f(x)|\mid x\in\mathbf{K}_{l},|\alpha|\leq m\},

(8.3)

where
\Omega=\cup_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{K}_{l}
,
\mathbf{K}_{l}
compact and
\mathbf{K}_{l}\subset\mathbf{K}_{l+1}^{\circ}
. The notation
\mathbf{K}^{\circ}
stands for the interior of the set
\mathbf{K}
.

Note now that
C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
is a non-trivial subset of
C^{\infty}(\Omega)
, e.g., a properly scaled and translated version of the function
\psi(x)=e^{\frac{1}{|x|^{2}-1}}
,
|x|\leq 1
and
\psi(x)=0
for
|x|>1
is in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
. Inspired by the seminorms that determine the topology of
C^{\infty}(\Omega)
it is tempting to impose on
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the topology given by the family of norms

\lVert f\rVert_{m}=\sup\{|\partial^{\alpha}f(x)|\mid x\in\Omega,|\alpha|\leq m\}.

(8.4)

However, with this topology
C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
is incomplete.

Thus, one prefers to give this space a topology that makes it complete, although not metrizable; more precisely, the convergence
f_{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
means that there exists a compact subset
\mathbf{K}\subset\Omega
such that for all
k
the supports of
f_{k}
are contained in
\mathbf{K}
and for each multi-index
\alpha
,
\partial^{\alpha}f_{k}
converges uniformly to 0.

The properties of the convolution imply that if
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
1\leq p<\infty
and
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
f*\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which ultimately yields that
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is dense in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

We will now recall the definition of the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of rapidly decreasing functions.

Definition 8.2
.

A function
f
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\lVert f\rVert_{N,\alpha}=\sup\{(1+|x|)^{N}|\partial^{\alpha}f(x)|\mid x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}<\infty,

(8.5)

for all non-negative integers
N
and multi-indices
\alpha
.

Equivalently, the complete topology of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be given through the family of seminorms

p_{\alpha,\beta}(f)=\sup\{|x^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}f(x)|\mid x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}.

(8.6)

We note that for
f,g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we also have
fg\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
f*g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Furthermore we have the (strict) inclusions

C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(8.7)

In distribution theory, the space
C^{\infty}_{c}
is called the space of test functions and usually denoted by
\mathcal{D}
. Its dual space is denoted by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and is called the space of distributions. The dual space of
\mathcal{S}
is denoted by
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is called the space of tempered distributions.

A (conjugate-)linear functional
\ell
belongs to the space of tempered distributions
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
if and only if there exist
M,N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

|\ell(f)|\lesssim\sum_{|\alpha|\leq M}\sum_{|\beta|\leq N}p_{\alpha,\beta}(f),\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{S}.

(8.8)

It is common to write
\langle\ell,f\rangle
instead of
\ell(f)

18
18

18

Note that we have complex conjugation in the second argument of the bracket
\langle\,.\,,\,.\,\rangle
, so our functionals are actually anti-linear.
.

The prototype example of a tempered distribution is the Dirac delta
\delta_{0}
. For
f\in\mathcal{S}
, we defined
\delta_{0}(f)=\langle\delta_{0},f\rangle=\overline{f(0)}
. We note that

|\langle\delta_{x},f\rangle|\leq\lVert f\rVert_{0,0},

(8.9)

which shows that
\delta_{x}
is indeed an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Translations and modulations of a (tempered) distribution
\ell
are given by

\langle T_{x}\ell,f\rangle=\langle\ell,T_{-x}f\rangle\quad\text{ and }\quad\langle M_{\omega}\ell,f\rangle=\langle\ell,M_{-\omega}f\rangle.

(8.10)

The derivative of a (tempered) distribution is given by

\langle\partial^{\alpha}\ell,f\rangle=(-1)^{\alpha}\langle\ell,\partial^{\alpha}f\rangle.

(8.11)

Example 8.3
.

(i)

The “principle value distribution”
\text{p.v.}\frac{1}{x}
is defined by

\langle\text{p.v.}\tfrac{1}{x},f\rangle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|x|>\varepsilon}\frac{\overline{f(x)}}{x}\,dx.

(8.12)

Splitting the integral over the intervals
(-\infty,-\varepsilon)
and
(\varepsilon,\infty)
and using integration by parts we get

\langle\text{p.v.}\tfrac{1}{x},f\rangle=-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|x|>\varepsilon}\log|x|\,\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx+(\overline{f(-\varepsilon)}-\overline{f(\varepsilon)})\log(\varepsilon).

(8.13)

But
|(\overline{f(-\varepsilon)}-\overline{f(\varepsilon)})\log(\varepsilon)|\leq 2\lVert f^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\varepsilon|\log(\varepsilon)|\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Hence, we are left with

-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|x|>\varepsilon}\log|x|\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log|x|\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx,

(8.14)

since
\log|x|
is indeed integrable on
[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
. Therefore, as

-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log|x|\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx=\langle\log(|x|),-f^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\log(|x|)^{\prime},f\rangle,

(8.15)

we see that, in the distributional sense,

(\log(|x|))^{\prime}=\text{p.v.}\tfrac{1}{x}.

(8.16)

(ii)

For a locally integrable function
g
, we can construct a linear functional
\ell_{g}
on
\mathcal{D}
by

\langle\ell_{g},f\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(x)\overline{f(x)}\,dx.

(8.17)

If
\supp(f)\subset\mathbf{K}
compact, we have
|\langle\ell_{g},f\rangle|\leq\lVert f\rVert_{0}\,\lVert g\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathbf{K}}\rVert_{L^{1}}
.

Consider the sign function
\text{sign}(x)
, then
\text{sign}^{\prime}(x)=2\delta_{0}
. For
\supp(f)\subset(-a,a)
, we compute

\langle\ell_{\text{sign}}^{\prime},f\rangle=-\langle\ell_{\text{sign}},f^{\prime}\rangle=\int_{-a}^{0}\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx-\int_{0}^{a}\overline{f^{\prime}(x)}\,dx=2\overline{f(0)}.

(8.18)

\diamond

8.1
Modulation Spaces

In 1979, Hans Feichtinger presented the function space
S_{0}
at a workshop at the University of Vienna, which now also goes under the name
M^{1}
or
M^{1,1}
(see also the original article of Feichtinger
[
10
]
and for a modern treatise see the article by Jakobsen
[
31
]
). Mainly during the 1980s, a whole new family of function spaces was then discovered and studied by Feichtinger, which we now call (weighted) modulation spaces. While we will only consider them on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, Feichtinger introduced them for locally compact Abelian groups. At the center of the definition stands the idea to impose a natural norm condition on the convolution
M_{\omega}g*f
of a modulated window function
g\in\mathcal{S}
and a tempered distribution
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. By observing that this convolution is (basically) just the STFT of
f
with window
g
, it becomes convenient to impose appropriate decay and integrability conditions on the STFT. This is how we will approach (unweighted) modulation space theory.

First, we introduce the following mixed-norm space.

Definition 8.4
.

A function
F
is said to belong to the function space
L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
if and only if

\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p,q}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|F(x,\omega)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{q/p}\,d\omega\right)^{1/q}.

(8.19)

Now, we give the (classical) definition of a modulation space.

Definition 8.5
.

Let
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and fix
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(non-zero). The modulation space
M^{p,q}
is the set of all tempered distributions
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for which the
L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
mixed norm of the STFT is finite, i.e.,

\lVert f\rVert_{M^{p,q}}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{q/p}\,d\omega\right)^{1/q}<\infty

(8.20)

For
p=\infty
and/or
q=\infty
, the usual adjustment of using the essential supremum is made. If
p=q
, we simply write
M^{p}
instead of
M^{p,p}
.

We note that this definition clearly depends on the choice of
g
. It will be one goal to show that the modulation spaces are actually independent of the particular choice of the window
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. However, until this result has been derived, we will pick the standard Gaussian
g_{0}(t)=2^{d/4}e^{-\pi t^{2}}
as the particular window to define modulation spaces. Then, we want to find their basic properties: determine whether they are Banach spaces, characterize equivalent norms, find dual spaces and inclusion properties.

8.1.1
Time-Frequency Analysis of Distributions

In order to answer the above questions on modulation spaces, we start with an auxiliary lemma, which we state without proof (since it follows from direct computation and the fact that
M_{\omega}
and
t^{\beta}
as well as
T_{x}
and
\partial^{\alpha}
commute). It will allow us to interchange the differential and multiplication operators with time-frequency shifts.

Lemma 8.6
.

If
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

\partial^{\alpha}t^{\beta}(M_{\omega}T_{x}g(t))=\sum_{\gamma_{1}\leq\alpha}\sum_{\gamma_{2}\leq\beta}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\gamma_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\gamma_{2}\end{pmatrix}x^{\gamma_{2}}(2\pi i\omega)^{\gamma_{1}}M_{\omega}T_{x}(\partial^{\alpha-\gamma_{1}}t^{\beta-\gamma_{2}}g(t)),

(8.21)

for all
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and multi-indices
\alpha,\beta
.

The following result shows the continuity properties of the STFT for the Schwartz space and the space of tempered distributions. We recall that continuity of
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
means that there exists a constants
C>0
and integers
M,N>0
such that
19
19

19

Recall that
X
is the position operator, which multiplies a function with its argument and
D^{\alpha}
is the differentiation operator
D^{\alpha}:f(t)\mapsto\partial^{\alpha}f(t)
.

|\langle f,g\rangle|\leq C\sum_{|\alpha|\leq M}\sum_{|\beta|\leq N}\lVert D^{\alpha}X^{\beta}g\rVert_{\infty},

(8.22)

for all
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Corollary 8.7
.

The operator-valued map
(x,\omega)\mapsto M_{\omega}T_{x}
is strongly continuous on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and weak
∗
-continuous on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

For
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have to show that

\lim_{|x|,|\omega|\to 0}\lVert D^{\alpha}X^{\beta}(M_{\omega}T_{x}g-g)\rVert_{\infty}=0,

(8.23)

for all
\alpha
and
\beta
. According to Lemma
8.6
we have

\displaystyle\lVert D^{\alpha}X^{\beta}(M_{\omega}T_{x}g-g)\rVert_{\infty}\leq

\displaystyle\,\lVert M_{\omega}T_{x}(D^{\alpha}X^{\beta}g)-D^{\alpha}X^{\beta}g\rVert_{\infty}

(8.24)

\displaystyle+\mathop{\sum_{\gamma_{1}\leq\alpha}\sum_{\gamma_{2}\leq\beta}}_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\neq 0}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\gamma_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\gamma_{2}\end{pmatrix}|x^{\gamma_{2}}(2\pi i\omega)^{\gamma_{1}}|\lVert D^{\alpha-\gamma_{1}}X^{\beta-\gamma_{2}}g\rVert_{\infty}

(8.25)

The convergence of the first term, as
|x|,|\omega|\to 0
, follows immediately if
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is dense in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The general result follows by a density argument (3-
\varepsilon
argument). The terms in
\mathop{\sum\sum}_{\gamma\neq 0}
converge to 0 because
\gamma_{1}\neq 0
or
\gamma_{2}\neq 0
in all terms.

If
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

\lim_{|x|,|\omega|\to 0}\langle M_{\omega}T_{x}f,g\rangle=\lim_{|x|,|\omega|\to 0}\langle f,T_{-x}M_{-\omega}g\rangle=\langle f,g\rangle,

(8.26)

which shows the weak
∗
-continuity of
M_{\omega}T_{x}
on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
∎

Note that the last statement implies that the STFT of a tempered distribution is a continuous function on the time-frequency plane.

Theorem 8.8
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(non-zero) and
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the STFT
V_{g}f
is continuous and there are constants
C>0
,
N\geq 0
, such that

|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|\leq C(1+|x|+|\omega|)^{N},

(8.27)

for all
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

Proof.

The continuity estimate (
8.22
) yields

|\langle f,M_{\omega}T_{x}g\rangle|\leq C\sum_{|\alpha|\leq M_{1}}\sum_{|\beta|\leq M_{2}}\lVert D^{\alpha}X^{\beta}(M_{\omega}T_{x}g)\rVert_{\infty}.

(8.28)

Using Lemma
8.6
we obtain

\displaystyle|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|\alpha|\leq M_{1}}}{{|\beta|\leq M_{2}}}}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma_{1}\leq\alpha}}{{\gamma_{2}\leq\beta}}}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\gamma_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\gamma_{2}\end{pmatrix}|x^{\gamma_{2}}|\,|(2\pi i\omega)^{\gamma_{1}}|\,\lVert D^{\alpha-\gamma_{1}}X^{\beta-\gamma_{2}}g\rVert_{\infty}

(8.29)

\displaystyle\leq C\max_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|\alpha|\leq M_{1}}}{{|\beta|\leq M_{2}}}}\lVert D^{\alpha}X^{\beta}g\rVert_{\infty}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|\alpha|\leq M_{1}}}{{|\beta|\leq M_{2}}}}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma_{1}\leq\alpha}}{{\gamma_{2}\leq\beta}}}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\gamma_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\gamma_{2}\end{pmatrix}|x^{\gamma_{2}}|\,|(2\pi i\omega)^{\gamma_{1}}|.

(8.30)

Since
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the right-hand side is a polynomial of degree
M_{1}
in the variables
|x_{k}|
and of degree
M_{2}
in the variables
|\omega_{k}|
. By choosing
N=\max(M_{1},M_{2})
any such polynomial is bounded by
C(1+|x|+|\omega|)^{N}
for a suitable constant
C
. The continuity was already shown in the previous result.
∎

In contrast, consider a linear functional on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which comes from a (measurable) function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and defines a tempered distribution by
\langle\ell_{f},g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)\overline{g(t)}\,dt
. Then
f
does not necessarily possess polynomial growth. As an example consider the function

f(t)=e^{t}\cos(e^{t}).

(8.31)

Since
f
is the derivative of the bounded function
\sin(e^{t})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, we have
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
.

For a characterization of the Schwartz class we need a new technique to create Schwartz functions. This will be provided by the following result, which then leads to a characterization of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proposition 8.9
.

Fix
g\in\mathcal{S}
(non-zero) and assume
F(x,\omega)
has rapid decay on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, that is, for all
n\geq 0
there is a constant
C_{n}>0
such that

|F(x,\omega)|\leq C_{n}(1+|x|+|\omega|)^{-n}.

(8.32)

Then, the integral

f(t)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g(t)\,d(x,\omega)

(8.33)

defines a function in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

The integral under consideration is absolutely convergent in the variable
x
and
\omega
. Differentiating under the integral with respect to the parameter
t
is permitted as long as the resulting integral is absolutely convergent. By our assumptions, this is true and stays true even if we replace
F
by
F\cdot P
, where
P
is an arbitrary polynomial. By Lemma
8.6
we have

\partial^{\alpha}t^{\beta}f(t)=\sum_{\gamma_{1}\leq\alpha}\sum_{\gamma_{2}\leq\beta}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\gamma_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\gamma_{2}\end{pmatrix}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)\,x^{\gamma_{2}}(2\pi i\omega)^{\gamma_{1}}M_{\omega}T_{x}(\partial^{\alpha-\gamma_{1}}t^{\beta-\gamma_{2}}g(t))\,d(x,\omega).

(8.34)

Set
C=\max\{\lVert M_{\omega}T_{x}\partial^{\gamma_{1}}t^{\gamma_{2}}g\rVert_{\infty}\mid\gamma_{1}\leq\alpha,\gamma_{2}\leq\beta\}
, which is finite since
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and take the supremum in the above integral with respect to
t
. Then

\lVert D^{\alpha}X^{\beta}f\rVert_{\infty}\leq C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|F(x,\omega)|P(x,\omega)\,d(x,\omega)<\infty,

(8.35)

where
P
is a polynomial in
|x_{k}|
and
|\omega_{k}|
depending only on
\alpha
and
\beta
. In fact,
P
is explicitly given by

\displaystyle P(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma_{1}\leq\alpha}\sum_{\gamma_{2}\leq\beta}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\\
\gamma_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\gamma_{2}\end{pmatrix}|x^{\gamma_{2}}|\,|(2\pi i\omega)^{\gamma_{1}}|

(8.36)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{d}(1+|x_{k}|)^{\beta_{k}}(1+2\pi|\omega_{k}|)^{\alpha_{k}}.

(8.37)

Thus,
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
because
F
is rapidly decreasing.
∎

After these preparations we can now establish a characterization of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
via its STFT.

Theorem 8.10
.

Fix
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(non-zero). Then, for
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the following are equivalent.

(i)

f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(ii)

V_{g}f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.

(iii)

For all
n\geq 0
, there is
C_{n}>0
such that

|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|\leq C_{n}(1+|x|+|\omega|)^{-n},\quad\forall(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}.

(8.38)

Proof.

(i)\Rightarrow(ii)
: We use the factorization
V_{g}f=\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{T}_{a}(f\otimes\overline{g})
of the STFT, where
\mathcal{F}_{2}
is the Fourier transform with respect to the second variable and
\mathcal{T}_{a}F(x,t)=F(t,t-x)
is the asymmetric coordinate change. If
f,g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
f\otimes\overline{g}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
. Since
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
is invariant under both operators
\mathcal{T}_{a}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, it follows that
V_{g}f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.

(ii)\Rightarrow(iii)
is obvious.

(iii)\Rightarrow(i)
: Set

\widetilde{f}=\lVert g\rVert_{2}^{-2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g\,d(x,\omega).

(8.39)

Proposition
8.9
implies that
\widetilde{f}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. At the same time the inversion formula for the STFT shows that
\widetilde{f}=f
. Thus,
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
∎

Corollary 8.11
.

If
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then the collection of seminorms

\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{(1+|z|)^{s}}}=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(1+|z|)^{s}|V_{g}f(z)|,\quad s\geq 0,

(8.40)

forms an equivalent collection of seminorms for
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

Set

\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(1+|z|)^{s}|V_{g}f(z)|<\infty,\;\forall s\geq 0\}.

(8.41)

Theorem
8.10
implies that
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as an equality between two sets. It remains to show that they have the same topology. To verify this, we apply (
8.35
) to the inversion formula

f=\lVert g\rVert_{2}^{-2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g\,d(x,\omega).

(8.42)

Then, we majorize the appearing polynomial
P
by
(1+|z|)^{n}
for sufficiently large
n
. So

\displaystyle\lVert D^{\alpha}X^{\beta}f\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|V_{g}f(z)|\,P(z)\,dz

(8.43)

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{C}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|V_{g}f(z)|\,(1+|z|)^{n}\,dz

(8.44)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(|V_{g}f(z)|\,(1+|z|)^{n+2d+1}\right)\,\widetilde{C}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(1+|z|)^{-2d-1}\,dz<\infty.

(8.45)

This estimate shows that the identity operator
I:\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is continuous. By the open mapping theorem it is therefore an isomorphism, and thus the two topologies coincide on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
∎

Corollary 8.12
.

Assume that
g,\widetilde{g}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(both non-zero).

(a)

If
|F(x,\omega)|\leq C(1+|x|+|\omega|)^{N}
for some constants
C>0
,
N\geq 0
, then the integral

f=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g\,d(x,\omega),

(8.46)

defines a tempered distribution f
\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in the sense that for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\langle f,\varphi\rangle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)\langle M_{\omega}T_{x}g,\varphi\rangle\,d(x,\omega).

(8.47)

(b)

In particular, if
F=V_{g}f
for some
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

f=\frac{1}{\overline{\langle g,\widetilde{g}\rangle}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g}\,d(x,\omega).

(8.48)

Proof.

(a)

The integral converges absolutely because
|F(z)|=\mathcal{O}(|z|^{N})
with
N
fixed and
|V_{g}\varphi(z)|=\mathcal{O}(|z|^{-n})
for all
n\geq 0
, whenever
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Therefore, we find that

|\langle f,\varphi\rangle|\leq C\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(|V_{g}\varphi(z)|(1+|z|)^{N+2d+1}\right)\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(1+|z|)^{-2d-1}\,dz.

(8.49)

By Corollary
8.11
we have that
f
defines a continuous linear functional on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e.,
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(b)

By (a), the integral in (
8.48
) defines a tempered distribution
\widetilde{f}
by

\langle\widetilde{f},\varphi\rangle=\frac{1}{\overline{\langle g,\widetilde{g}\rangle}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(x,\omega)\langle M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g},\varphi\rangle\,d(x,\omega).

(8.50)

However, since

\varphi=\frac{1}{\langle g,\widetilde{g}\rangle}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{\widetilde{g}}\varphi(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g\,d(x,\omega)

(8.51)

holds in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we also have

\langle f,\varphi\rangle=\frac{1}{\overline{\langle g,\widetilde{g}\rangle}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\langle f,M_{\omega}T_{x}g\rangle\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}\varphi(x,\omega)}\,d(x,\omega)=\langle\widetilde{f},\varphi\rangle,

(8.52)

and so
\widetilde{f}=f
. This proves the inversion formula on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

∎

8.1.2
Properties of Modulation Spaces

After the excursion into time-frequency analysis on the Schwartz space and for tempered distributions, we return to our original object of study, the modulation spaces. We re-call that for a tempered distribution
f
belonging to a modulation space
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is equivalent to the fact that the
L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
mixed-norm of the STFT with a fixed Schwartz window
g
is finite, i.e.,

\lVert f\rVert_{M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}<\infty.

(8.53)

We will start working with the standard Gaussian
g_{0}(t)=2^{d/4}e^{-\pi t^{2}}
as our window of choice. The first result that we collect on modulation spaces is the following.

Proposition 8.13
.

The modulation space
M^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e.,

M^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(8.54)

Proof.

By assumption, the norm

\lVert f\rVert_{M^{2}}^{2}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|V_{g_{0}}f(x,\omega)|^{2}\,d(x,\omega)

(8.55)

is finite. It follows that
V_{g_{0}}f(x,\omega)=\mathcal{F}(fT_{x}\overline{g_{0}})(\omega)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},d\omega)
for almost all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By Plancherel’s theorem we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\mathcal{F}(fT_{x}\overline{g_{0}})(\omega)|^{2}\,d\omega=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(t)|^{2}|g_{0}(t-x)|^{2}\,dt.

(8.56)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{M^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(t)|^{2}|g_{0}(t-x)|^{2}\,dt\right)\,dx

(8.57)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|f(t)|^{2}|g_{0}(s)|^{2}\,d(t,s)

(8.58)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(t)|^{2}\,dt\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g_{0}(s)|^{2}\,ds\right)

(8.59)

\displaystyle=\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}\lVert g_{0}\rVert_{2}^{2}=\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(8.60)

∎

Since we worked in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
throughout most of the course anyways, we are now of course interested in modulation spaces different from
M^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

We will now (informally) define the adjoint operator of
V_{g}
. This will then lead to an inversion formula for the STFT for modulation spaces.

Definition 8.14
.

Given a non-zero window
g
and a function
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, let

V^{*}_{g}F=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g\,d(x,\omega).

(8.61)

The integral is to be interpreted in the weak sense by

\displaystyle\langle V^{*}_{g}F,f\rangle

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)\langle M_{\omega}T_{x}g,f\rangle\,d(x,\omega)

(8.62)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)\overline{V_{g}f(x,\omega)}\,d(x,\omega)

(8.63)

\displaystyle=\langle F,V_{g}f\rangle.

(8.64)

The following proposition states under which conditions
V^{*}_{g}
is a well-defined operator.

Proposition 8.15
.

Let
g,\widetilde{g}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

(a)

V_{g}^{*}
maps
L^{p,q}
into
M^{p,q}
and satisfies

\lVert V_{g}^{*}F\rVert_{M^{p,q}}\leq C\lVert V_{g_{0}}g\rVert_{L^{1}}\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p,q}}.

(8.65)

(b)

In particular, if
F=V_{\widetilde{g}}f
, then the inversion formula

f=\frac{1}{\langle g,\widetilde{g}\rangle}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{\widetilde{g}}f(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g\,d(x,\omega)

(8.66)

holds on
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In short,
I_{M^{p,q}}=\langle g,\widetilde{g}\rangle^{-1}V_{g}^{*}V_{\widetilde{g}}
.

(c)

The definition of
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is independent of the choice of the window
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Different windows yield equivalent norms.

Proof.

(a)

We first note
20
20

20

We refer to the textbook of Gröchenig
[
19
, Lemma 11.1.2]
and remark that the proof is the same as for
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
spaces.
that the Hölder inequality holds for mixed-norm spaces in the following form. If
F\in L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and
G\in L^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
and
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1
, then
FG\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and

\left|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(z)\overline{G(z)}\,dz\right|\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\lVert G\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}}.

(8.67)

We will now show that
V_{g}^{*}F\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
F\in L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, then we estimate

\displaystyle|\langle V_{g}^{*}F,\varphi\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle F,V_{g}\varphi\rangle|

(8.68)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\lVert V_{g}\varphi\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}}

(8.69)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\lVert(1+|z|)^{n}V_{g}\varphi\rVert_{\infty}\lVert(1+|z|)^{-n}\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}}.

(8.70)

This expression is finite for sufficiently large
n
. Using the equivalence of seminorms (Corollary
8.11
), it follows that the expression

V^{*}_{g}F=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g\,d(x,\omega)

(8.71)

is well-defined as a tempered distribution. This has as a consequence that
V_{g}^{*}F
has a continuous STFT, which is explicitly given by
21
21

21

From the second to the third line we use the covariance principle
2.6
for the function

\overline{V_{g}(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}\widetilde{g})(x,\omega)}=\overline{\langle M_{\eta}T_{\xi}\widetilde{g},M_{\omega}T_{x}g\rangle}=\langle M_{\omega}T_{x}g,M_{\eta}T_{\xi}\widetilde{g}\rangle=V_{\widetilde{g}}(M_{\omega}T_{x}g)(\xi,\eta).

\displaystyle V_{\widetilde{g}}(V_{g}^{*}F)(\xi,\eta)

\displaystyle=\langle V_{g}^{*}F,M_{\eta}T_{\xi}\widetilde{g}\rangle

(8.72)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)\overline{V_{g}(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}\widetilde{g})(x,\omega)}\,d(x,\omega)

(8.73)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)V_{\widetilde{g}}g(\xi-x,\eta-\omega)e^{-2\pi ix\cdot(\eta-\omega)}\,d(x,\omega).

(8.74)

By taking absolute values in this identity, we obtain the pointwise estimate

|V_{\widetilde{g}}(V_{g}^{*}F)(\xi,\eta)|\leq(|F|*|V_{\widetilde{g}}g|)(\xi,\eta)

(8.75)

The convolution relation
L^{1}*L^{p}\subset L^{p}
extends to mixed-norm spaces, i.e.,
L^{1}*L^{p,q}\subset L^{p,q}
and for
F\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and
G\in L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
we have
\lVert F*G\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\leq\lVert F\rVert_{L^{1}}\lVert G\rVert_{L^{p,q}}
. Applied to the above estimate, this yields

\lVert V_{\widetilde{g}}(V_{g}^{*}F)\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\leq C\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\lVert V_{\widetilde{g}}g\rVert_{L^{1}}.

(8.76)

Since,
g,\widetilde{g}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
V_{\widetilde{g}}g
decays rapidly and the right-hand side is finite.

To measure the modulation space norm of
V_{g}^{*}F
, we take the fixed (canonical) window
g_{0}
and obtain

\lVert V_{g}^{*}F\rVert_{M^{p,q}}=\lVert V_{g_{0}}(V_{g}^{*}F)\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\leq C\lVert F\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\lVert V_{g_{0}}g\rVert_{L^{1}}<\infty.

(8.77)

(b)

If
V_{\widetilde{g}}f\in L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, then

\widetilde{f}=\frac{1}{\langle g,\widetilde{g}\rangle}V_{g}^{*}V_{\widetilde{g}}f\in M^{p,q}

(8.78)

by the above proof. The equality
\widetilde{f}=f
follows from the inversion formula for
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(c)

The proof of (a) also implies the equivalence of norms. Using (
8.76
) with
g=\widetilde{g}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(we may also assume
\lVert g\rVert_{2}=1
), we obtain

\lVert f\rVert_{M^{p,q}}=\lVert V_{g_{0}}f\rVert_{L^{p,q}}=\lVert V_{g_{0}}(V_{g}^{*}V_{g}f)\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\leq C\lVert V_{g_{0}}g\rVert_{L^{1}}\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{L^{p,q}}.

(8.79)

Interchanging the roles of
g
and
g_{0}
, we have

\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\leq C\lVert V_{g}g_{0}\rVert_{L^{1}}\lVert V_{g_{0}}f\rVert_{L^{p,q}}=C_{1}\lVert f\rVert_{M^{p,q}}.

(8.80)

Thus,
f\in M^{p,q}
if and only if
V_{g}f\in L^{p,q}
for some and hence all
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the norms
\lVert V_{g_{0}}f\rVert_{L^{p,q}}
and
\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{L^{p,q}}
are equivalent on
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

∎

Proposition 8.16
.

If
1\leq p,q<\infty
, then
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a dense subspace of
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

We estimate the
M^{p,q}
-norm of
f
by

\lVert f\rVert_{M^{p,q}}=\lVert V_{g_{0}}f\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\leq\lVert(1+|z|)^{n}\,V_{g_{0}}f\rVert_{\infty}\lVert(1+|z|)^{-n}\rVert_{L^{p,q}}.

(8.81)

If
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then the expression is finite, hence
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Next, we choose an exhausting sequence of compact sets
K_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, e.g.,
K_{n}=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\mid|z|\leq n\}
, and a window
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\lVert g\rVert_{2}=1
. We set
F_{n}=V_{g}f\,\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{K_{n}}
and

f_{n}=V^{*}_{g}F_{n}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F_{n}(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g\,d(x,\omega)=\iint_{K_{n}}V_{g}f(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g\,d(x,\omega).

(8.82)

Since
F_{n}
decays rapidly, we see that
f_{n}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by Proposition
8.9
. Furthermore, by Proposition
8.15
we obtain that

\lVert f-f_{n}\rVert_{M^{p,q}}=\lVert V_{g}^{*}(V_{g}f-F_{n})\rVert_{M^{p,q}}\leq C\lVert V_{g}f-F_{n}\rVert_{L^{p,q}}.

(8.83)

If
p,q<\infty
, then
\lVert f-f_{n}\rVert_{M^{p,q}}\to 0
, thus
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is dense in
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
∎

Theorem 8.17
.

(a)

M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a Banach space for
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
.

(b)

M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is invariant under time-frequency shifts and
\lVert M_{\omega}T_{x}f\rVert_{M^{p,q}}\leq C\lVert f\rVert_{M^{p,q}}
.

(c)

If
p=q
, then
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is invariant under the Fourier transform.

Proof.

(a)

Let
V=\{F\in L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\mid F=V_{g_{0}}f\}
. Then
V
is a subspace of
L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
which is isometrically isomorphic to
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by definition. Therefore,
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
inherits its linear structure from
L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
. We only need to show the completeness of
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
or, equivalently, that
V
is a closed subspace of
L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.

Let
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence in
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
(V_{g_{0}}f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and so there exists a function
F\in L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert V_{g_{0}}f_{n}-F\rVert_{L^{p,q}}=0.

(8.84)

To see that
F
is of the form
V_{g_{0}}f
, define

f=\frac{1}{\lVert g_{0}\rVert_{2}^{2}}V_{g_{0}}^{*}F=\frac{1}{\lVert g_{0}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}F(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}g_{0}\,d(x,\omega).

(8.85)

By Proposition
8.15
we have
f\in M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
f_{n}=\lVert g_{0}\rVert_{2}^{-2}V_{g_{0}}^{*}V_{g_{0}}f_{n}
. Thus, we have the estimate

\lVert f-f_{n}\rVert_{M^{p,q}}=\lVert g_{0}\rVert_{2}^{-2}\lVert V_{g_{0}}^{*}(F-V_{g_{0}}f)\rVert_{M^{p,q}}\leq C\lVert F-V_{g_{0}}f_{n}\rVert_{L^{p,q}}.

(8.86)

Therefore, the limit of the Cauchy sequence
f_{n}
is
f
and thus
M^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is complete.

(b)

The invariance properties follow from the translation invariance of
L^{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
. In particular,
|V_{g}(M_{\omega}T_{x}f)|=|T_{(x,\omega)}V_{g}f|
. Thus, we have

\lVert M_{\omega}T_{x}f\rVert_{M^{p,q}}=\lVert T_{(x,\omega)}V_{g_{0}}f\rVert_{L^{p,q}}\leq C\lVert V_{g_{0}}f\rVert_{L^{p,q}}=C\lVert f\rVert_{M^{p,q}}.

(8.87)

(c)

For the invariance under the Fourier transform we use the fundamental identity (
2.18
) and the norm equivalence in Proposition
8.15
. We obtain that

\displaystyle\lVert\widehat{f}\rVert_{M^{p}}^{p}

\displaystyle=\lVert V_{g_{0}}\widehat{f}\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq C\lVert V_{\widehat{g_{0}}}\widehat{f}\rVert_{p}^{p}

(8.88)

\displaystyle=C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|V_{\widehat{g_{0}}}\widehat{f}(x,\omega)|^{p}\,d(x,\omega)

(8.89)

\displaystyle=C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|V_{g_{0}}f(-\omega,x)|^{p}\,d(x,\omega)

(8.90)

\displaystyle=C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|V_{g_{0}}f(x,\omega)|^{p}\,d(x,\omega)\leq C^{\prime}\lVert f\rVert_{M^{p}}^{p}.

(8.91)

∎

We remark that, for
1\leq p,q<\infty
, the dual space of
L^{p,q}
is
L^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}
, with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
and
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1
. The same holds for the modulation spaces
M^{p,q}
and the duality may be expressed by

\langle f,h\rangle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g_{0}}f(z)\overline{V_{g_{0}}h(z)}\,dz,

(8.92)

f\in M^{p,q}
,
h\in M^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}
.

8.2
Feichtinger’s Algebra

In this section we collect some facts about Feichtinger’s algebra which is often denoted by
S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Its definition is often given as follows.

Definition 8.18
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be fixed. Then
S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the set of all tempered distributions such that

\lVert f\rVert_{S_{0}}=\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{L^{1,1}}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|\,d(x,\omega)<\infty.

(8.93)

Thus
S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We note that there are several equivalent definitions of
S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see
[
31
]
). We will state an equivalent definition after the following result.

Lemma 8.19
.

Assume
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
V_{g}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, then, both,
f\in S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
g\in S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

The assumptions imply that
V_{g}f
is a well-defined continuous function on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. We will fix a window
\widetilde{g}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\langle f,\widetilde{g}\rangle\neq 0
and
\langle g,\widetilde{g}\rangle\neq 0
. Using the inversion formula for
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

f=\frac{1}{\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(x,\omega)M_{\omega}T_{x}\widetilde{g}\,d(x,\omega)

(8.94)

we obtain

V_{\widetilde{g}}f=\frac{1}{\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle}V_{\widetilde{g}}V^{*}_{\widetilde{g}}(V_{g}f).

(8.95)

By equation (
8.75
) we have

|V_{\widetilde{g}}V^{*}_{\widetilde{g}}(V_{g}f)(x,\omega)|\leq(|V_{g}f|*|V_{\widetilde{g}}\widetilde{g}|)(x,\omega).

(8.96)

Hence,

|V_{\widetilde{g}}f(x,\omega)|\leq\frac{1}{|\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle|}(|V_{g}f|*|V_{\widetilde{g}}\widetilde{g}|)(x,\omega).

(8.97)

By using Young’s convolution inequality, we obtain

\lVert f\rVert_{M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\lVert V_{\widetilde{g}}f\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\leq\frac{1}{|\langle\widetilde{g},g\rangle|}\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\lVert V_{\widetilde{g}}\widetilde{g}\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}<\infty.

(8.98)

Thus
f\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The result for
g
follows by interchanging the roles of
f
and
g
.
∎

This leads to the following definition of
S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is also quite natural from the time-frequency point of view.

Definition 8.20
.

Feichtinger’s algebra
S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the set of all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|V_{f}f(x,\omega)|\,d(x,\omega)<\infty.

(8.99)

Also,
S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is invariant under the action of the metaplectic group.

Proposition 8.21
.

If
S\in Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
, then
\mu(S)=\widehat{S}\in Mp(\mathbb{R},2d)
is an isomorphism from
S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Feichtinger’s algebra also possesses a remarkable minimality property. In fact, the search for the smallest Banach space that is invariant under time-frequency shifts led H. Feichtinger to the discovery of
M^{1}=S_{0}
.

In Banach space theory there is a standard procedure to construct minimal spaces which are to contain certain “atoms”. For time-frequency shifts this procedure works as follows. Given a non-zero function
g\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, define
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to be the vector space of all (non-uniform) Gabor expansions

f=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}\pi(\gamma)g,

(8.100)

where
\{\pi(\gamma)=M_{\gamma_{2}}T_{\gamma_{1}}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}\}
is an arbitrary countable set and

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}|c_{\gamma}|<\infty.

(8.101)

The norm on
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is

\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}=\inf\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}|c_{\gamma}|,

(8.102)

where the infimum is taken over all representations of (
8.100
). Then,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is indeed a Banach space. Moreover, it turns out that
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Consequently, every
f\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
has an expansion of type (
8.100
) and
\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}
is an equivalent norm on Feichtinger’s algebra.

Lastly, we state the minimality property of
M^{1}
. We start with the following result.

Theorem 8.22
.

Let
B
be a Banach space of tempered distributions with the following properties:

(i)

B
is invariant und time-frequency shifts, and

\lVert M_{\omega}T_{x}f\rVert_{B}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{B},

(8.103)

for all
f\in B
.

(ii)

M^{1}\cap B\neq\{0\}
.

Then
M^{1}
is embedded in
B
.

We also have the following chain of inclusions;

M^{1}\subset M^{p,q}\subset M^{\infty},\quad 1\leq p,q\leq\infty.

(8.104)

The minimal space
M^{1}
can be seen as a space of test functions with
M^{\infty}
as the associated space of distributions. Indeed, weighted versions of the modulation space
M^{1}_{v_{s}}
,
v_{s}=(1+|z|)^{s}
with
\lVert f\rVert_{M^{1}_{v_{s}}}=\lVert(1+|z|)^{s}f\rVert_{M^{1}}
are the building blocks of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in the sense that
\mathcal{S}=\bigcap_{s\geq 0}M^{1}_{v_{s}}
. Furthermore, the tempered distributions are obtained from
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\bigcup_{s\geq 0}M^{\infty}_{1/v_{s}}
. According to the above statements, any modulation space
M^{p,q}
may be analyzed within the pair
(M^{1},M^{\infty})
instead of
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
. Also, in contrast to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which are Fréchet spaces, the modulation spaces
M^{1}
and
M^{\infty}
are Banach spaces. They have a much simpler mathematical structure and are easier to use.

8.3
Wiener Amalgam Spaces

The Wiener space comes up in the treatment of periodic functions and the Poisson summation formula. We start with the definition of the Wiener space, which is named after N. Wiener. For this, we introduce the notation
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha}=[0,\alpha]^{d}
, which is the cube of with side length
\alpha
. If
\alpha=1
, we simply write
\mathcal{Q}

Definition 8.23
.

A function
g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
belongs to the Wiener space
W=W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if

\lVert g\rVert_{W}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\esssup_{x\in\mathcal{Q}}|g(x+k)|<\infty.

(8.105)

The subspace of continuous functions is denoted by
W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Observe that the norm can also be written as

\lVert g\rVert_{W}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\lVert g\,T_{k}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathcal{Q}}\rVert_{\infty}.

(8.106)

Functions in
W
are locally bounded and globally in
\ell^{1}
. Hence, the norm mixes (‘‘amalgamates”) a local property of a function, namely boundedness, with a global property. This space was first introduced by N. Wiener to study Tauberian theorems
22
22

22

Tauberian theorems play an important role in analytic number theory and give conditions under which a divergent series can be summed to yield a meaningful result. Similar methods have earlier been considered by N. H. Abel. N. Wiener also used Fourier analysis methods, in particular the insight that asymptotic (global; decay) properties are take to local (smoothness) properties.

[
52
]
. We note that the Wiener space contains all bounded functions with compact support and is therefore dense in any
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-space for
1\leq p<\infty
.

More generally, Wiener amalgam spaces are defined as follows.

Definition 8.24
.

We say a measurable function
f
belongs to the Wiener amalgam space
W(L^{p},\ell^{q})(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if

\lVert f\rVert_{W(L^{p},\ell^{q})}=\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\lVert f\,T_{k}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathcal{Q}}\rVert_{p}^{q}\right)^{1/q}<\infty.

(8.107)

Since
\mathcal{Q}
can be covered by a finite number of cubes
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha}
, we can take any size of the cube (and replace the translations by
T_{\alpha k}
) to obtain an equivalent norm. Hence, the Wiener amalgam spaces are independent of the choice of
\alpha
. Also, the integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
can be replaced by an arbitrary lattice with a similar argument.
