Furthermore, we note that the characteristic function
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathcal{Q}}
can be replaced by a bounded function
\phi
with compact support and/or the
\ell^{q}
-norm may as well be replaced by an
L^{q}
-norm;

\lVert f\rVert_{W(L^{p},L^{q})}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|(f\,T_{x}\overline{\phi})(\omega)|^{p}\,d\omega\right)^{q/p}\,dx\right)^{1/q}.

(8.108)

Again, we get an equivalent norm, i.e.,

\lVert f\rVert_{W(L^{p},L^{q})}\asymp\lVert f\rVert_{W(L^{p},\ell^{q})}.

(8.109)

So, we may simply write
W^{p,q}
without ambiguity, as long as we are only interested in general properties of Wiener amalgam spaces and not specific norms of specific functions. For more details we refer to
[
26
]
.

We note the following connection between Wiener amalgam spaces and modulation spaces. Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{W(\mathcal{F}L^{p},L^{q})}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\mathcal{F}(f\,T_{x}\overline{\phi})(\omega)|^{p}\,d\omega\right)^{q/p}\,dx\right)^{1/q}

(8.110)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|V_{\phi}f(x,\omega)|^{p}\,d\omega\right)^{q/p}\,dx\right)^{1/q}

(8.111)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|V_{\widehat{\phi}}\widehat{f}(\omega,-x)|^{p}\,d\omega\right)^{q/p}\,dx\right)^{1/q}

(8.112)

\displaystyle=\lVert\widehat{f}\rVert_{M^{p,q}}.

(8.113)

In shorter notation, we have
\mathcal{F}M^{p,q}=W(\mathcal{F}L^{p},L^{q})
.

In view of the Poisson summation formula, which was our motivation for introducing the Wiener space, we note that
W(\mathbb{R}^{d})=W^{\infty,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The space
W\cap\mathcal{F}W
is in a sense the largest natural space on which the Poisson summation formula holds point-wise. If both,
f
and
\widehat{f}
are in
W(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then they are also continuous and both series

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(x+k)\quad\text{ and }\quad\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\widehat{f}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot x}

(8.114)

converge absolutely by the definition of
W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Moreover, the proof of Proposition
4.3
shows that the sums are indeed equal for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which means that:

The Poisson summation formula holds pointwise with absolute convergence in both series whenever
f
and
\widehat{f}\in W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The above statement holds if we replace
W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by Feichtinger’s algebra
S_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, as is shown by the following embedding result for
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proposition 8.25
.

If
f\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
f\in W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\widehat{f}\in W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

We choose a window
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
0\leq g(x)\leq 1
(particularly,
g
is real-valued) for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
g(x)=1
for
x\in[-1,1]^{d}
. Then
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t)\leq T_{x}g(t)
for all
x\in\mathcal{Q}=[0,1]^{d}
and all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and

\lVert f\,T_{k}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathcal{Q}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert f\,T_{k+x}\overline{g}\rVert_{\infty}.

(8.115)

The converse of the Lemma of Riemann-Lebesgue in the form
\lVert h\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert\widehat{h}\rVert_{1}
provides the connection to the STFT of
f
with window
g
, as for
x\in Q
,

\lVert f\,T_{k+x}\overline{g}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert\mathcal{F}(f\,T_{k+x}\overline{g})\rVert_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|V_{g}f(k+x,\omega)|\,d\omega.

(8.116)

Summation over
k
yields

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\lVert f\,T_{k}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{Q}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{\mathcal{Q}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|V_{g}f(k+x,\omega)|\,d\omega\right)\,dx=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|\,d(x,\omega).

(8.117)

Thus, we have shown that

\lVert f\rVert_{W(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}=\lVert f\rVert_{M^{1}}

(8.118)

For the result involving
\widehat{f}
we argue similarly, but define
g
such that
\widehat{g}
has the necessary properties. Then, the fundamental identity of time-frequency analysis (
2.18
) yields the result.
∎

8.4
The WKNS Sampling Theorem Re-Visited

The following result is given in
[
11
, Chap. 10]
and is a generalization of the WKNS sampling theorem for band-limited functions. We state it only for the case of band-limited functions with
\supp(\widehat{f})\subset[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
, but the generalizations for other band-limits follow as in Section
4.1
. Before we can state the result, we need to define a nice class of band-limited functions.

Definition 8.26
.

For an interval
I\subsetneq[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
we set

B_{I}^{1}(\mathbb{R})=\{f\in W(\mathbb{R})\mid\supp(\widehat{f)}\subset I\}

(8.119)

As remarked in
[
11
, Chap. 10]
, it can be shown that

B_{I}^{1}(\mathbb{R})=\{f\in S_{0}(\mathbb{R})\mid\supp(\widehat{f})\subset I\}=\{f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\mid\supp(\widehat{f})\subset I\}.

(8.120)

The generalization of the classical sampling theorem, now with nice localization of the building blocks, reads as follows.

Theorem 8.27
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

f(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{f}(\omega)e^{2\pi i\omega t}\,dt,

(8.121)

with
\supp(\widehat{f})=I\subsetneq[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
. Let
g\in S_{0}(\mathbb{R})
,
\lVert g\rVert_{2}=1
, such that
\widehat{g}(\omega)=1
for
\omega\in I
and
\supp(\widehat{g})\subset[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
. Then, we have

f(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f(k)g(t-k),\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\quad\text{ and }\quad\forall f\in B_{I}^{1}(\mathbb{R}),

(8.122)

with absolute convergence in
(S_{0}(\mathbb{R}),\lVert.\rVert_{S_{0}})
,
(W(\mathbb{R}),\lVert.\rVert_{W})
, and
(C_{0}(\mathbb{R}),\lVert.\rVert_{\infty})
.

For more information on the Shannon sampling theorem with “nice” windows and a time-frequency point-of-view, the interested reader is also referred to
[
50
]
.

9
The Zak Transform and Density Principles

9.1
The Zak Transform

We are now going to study the Zak transform, which is often used in time-frequency analysis to study properties of the Gabor frame operator. It is a version of the Poisson summation formula and a popular tool in engineering for the analysis of Gabor frames.

We note that Zak transform is often defined with a (positive) parameter and that there are several versions of the Zak transform, which differ by normalization. We name the expository article by Janssen
[
32
]
or the textbook of Gröchenig
[
19
]
as references. Here, we will use the Zak transform without parameter (actually, with parameter
\alpha=1
).

Definition 9.1
.

The Zak transform of a function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the function on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
defined by

Zf(x,\omega)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(x+k)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot\omega}.

(9.1)

This definition is compatible with most versions of the Zak transform with parameter
\alpha
(which normally refers to a (non-unitary) dilation of the first or both arguments) for the case
\alpha=1
.

The Zak transform was first used by Gel’fand for a problem in differential equations
[
15
]
. The transform has been rediscovered several times and is now named after J. Zak, who used it for problems in solid state physics, e.g., in
[
54
]
.

We will first show that the Zak transform inherits continuity and decay properties from
f
.

Lemma 9.2
.

(a)

If
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
Zf\in L^{1}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})
.

(b)

If
f\in W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
Zf\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.

(c)

If
f\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
Zf
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

(d)

If
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
Zf
is defined almost everywhere and
Zf(x,\omega)\in L^{2}(\mathcal{Q},d\omega)
for almost all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

(a)

By the periodization trick we have

\int_{\mathcal{Q}}|Zf(x,\omega)|\,dx\leq\int_{\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|f(x+k)|\,dx=\lVert f\rVert_{1},

(9.2)

for all
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and therefore

\int_{\mathcal{Q}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{Q}}|Zf(x,\omega)\,dx\right)\,d\omega\leq\lVert f\rVert_{1}.

(9.3)

(b)

We have

|Zf(x,\omega)|\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|f(x+k)|\leq 2^{d}\lVert f\rVert_{W},

(9.4)

where the factor
2^{d}
stems from the fact that any interval of length one contains at most 2 distinct points of minimum distance 1 between each other. Hence, any translated cube
\mathcal{Q}+k
contains at most
2^{d}
points of the form
(x+k)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

(c)

Given
\varepsilon>0
, there is
N>0
such that

\sum_{|k|>N}\lVert f\,T_{k}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathcal{Q}}\rVert_{\infty}<\frac{\varepsilon}{4}.

(9.5)

Then, the main term
\sum_{|k|\leq N}f(x+k)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot\omega}
is uniformly continuous on compact sets of
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, and there exists a
\delta>0
, such that

\left|\sum_{|k|\leq N}f(x^{\prime}+k)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot\omega^{\prime}}-\sum_{|k|\leq N}f(x+k)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot\omega}\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2},

(9.6)

for
|x^{\prime}-x|+|\omega^{\prime}-\omega|<\delta
. Consequently
|Zf(x^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})-Zf(x,\omega)|<\varepsilon
if
|x^{\prime}-x|+|\omega^{\prime}-\omega|<\delta
.

(d)

The periodization of the
L^{2}
-norm of
f
yields

\int_{\mathcal{Q}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|f(x+k)|^{2}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x)|^{2}\,dx<\infty.

(9.7)

This implies that the sequence
\left(f(x+k)\right)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
for almost all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Consequently,
Zf(x,.)
is a Fourier series with square-summable coefficients and therefore in
L^{2}(\mathcal{Q},d\omega)
. In particular,
Zf
is defined almost everywhere.

∎

We collect a few more properties of the Zak transform. The interpretation of the equalities depends on the function space from which
f
comes. First, we note its quasi-periodicity, that is, for
l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

Zf(x,\omega+l)=Zf(x,\omega)

(9.8)

and

Zf(x+l,\omega)=e^{2\pi il\cdot\omega}Zf(x,\omega).

(9.9)

Thus,
Zf
is completely determined by its values on the (unit) cube
[0,1]^{d}\times[0,1]^{d}=\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. It also behaves nicely with time-frequency shifts.

Z(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}f)(x,\omega)=e^{2\pi i\eta\cdot x}Zf(x-\xi,\omega-\eta),

(9.10)

as can be shown by a direct computation. Equation (
9.10
) resembles the covariance principle of the STFT and is one reason why the Zak transform is considered a joint time-frequency representation. For the special choice
\xi=k
,
\eta=l
with
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we obtain

\displaystyle Z(M_{l}T_{k}f)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi il\cdot x}Zf(x-k,\omega-l)

(9.11)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi il\cdot x}e^{-2\pi ik\cdot\omega}Zf(x,\omega).

(9.12)

Note that
T_{k}
and
M_{l}
commute since
(k,l)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2d}
, which is self-dual. Thus the operators
T_{k}
and
M_{l}
are mapped to multiplication operators by the Zak transform.

Example 9.3
.

(i)

Let
d=1
and consider the characteristic function
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[0,1]}
. Then
23
23

23

The floor function is
\lfloor x\rfloor=\max\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\mid k\leq x\}
.

Zf(x,\omega)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[0,1]}(x+k)e^{-2\pi ik\omega}=e^{2\pi i\lfloor x\rfloor\omega}.

(9.13)

In particular,
Z\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[0,1]}(x,\omega)=1
for almost all
(x,\omega)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]
and
|Z\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{[0,1]}|^{2}\equiv 1
. Similarly, if
d\geq 1
, we have
Z\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathcal{Q}}(x,\omega)=1
, for almost all
(x,\omega)\in\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}
.

(ii)

For
\varphi_{s}(t)=e^{-\pi st^{2}}
,
s>0
, we have

Z\varphi_{s}(x,-\omega)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\pi s(x+k)^{2}}e^{2\pi ik\omega}.

(9.14)

This has close connections to the Jacobi theta functions in analytic number theory. For example

\vartheta_{3}(\tau;z)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\pi i\tau k^{2}}e^{2\pi ikz},\quad(\tau,z)\in\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}.

(9.15)

Therefore, the restrictions
\tau=is
,
s>0
and
z=\omega\in\mathbb{R}
yield

\vartheta_{3}(is;\omega)=Z\varphi_{s}(0,-\omega).

(9.16)

\diamond

We also have an inversion formula for the Zak transform. Let
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
Zf(x,\omega)\in L^{1}(\mathcal{Q},d\omega)
for almost all
x
. Therefore, the following computations are justified.

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Q}}Zf(x,\omega)\,d\omega

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Q}}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(x+k)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot\omega}\right)\,d\omega

(9.17)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{\mathcal{Q}}f(x+k)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot\omega}\,d\omega=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(x+k)\int_{\mathcal{Q}}e^{-2\pi ik\cdot\omega}\,d\omega

(9.18)

\displaystyle=f(x),

(9.19)

for almost all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Also, if
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
Zf(x,\omega)\in L^{1}(\mathcal{Q},dx)
for almost all
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Thus, a similar computation shows that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Q}}Zf(x,\omega)e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\omega}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Q}}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(x+k)e^{-2\pi i(k+x)\cdot\omega}\right)\,dx

(9.20)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{\mathcal{Q}}f(x+k)e^{-2\pi i(k+x)\cdot\omega}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\omega}\,dx

(9.21)

\displaystyle=\widehat{f}(\omega).

(9.22)

The next result is the Poisson summation formula in disguise and relates the Zak transform of a function with the Zak transform of its Fourier transform.

Proposition 9.4
.

Let
f\in W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\widehat{f}\in W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(so
f
and
\widehat{f}
are actually continuous). Then

Zf(x,\omega)=e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}Z\widehat{f}(\omega,-x),

(9.23)

for all
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

Proof.

We apply the Poisson summation formula to the function
g(t)=M_{-\omega}T_{-x}f(t)
.

\displaystyle Zf(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}g(k)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\widehat{g}(k)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}T_{-\omega}M_{x}\widehat{f}(k)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\widehat{f}(k+\omega)e^{2\pi ix\cdot(k+\omega)}

(9.24)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}Z\widehat{f}(\omega,-x).

(9.25)

∎

The Zak transform can also be seen as a unitary operator from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
L^{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})
, which is shown in the following Plancherel-like result.

Theorem 9.5
.

If
f\in W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

\iint_{\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}}|Zf(x,\omega)|^{2}\,d(x,\omega)=\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(9.26)

Consequently,
Z
extends to a unitary operator from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
onto
L^{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})
.

Proof.

For fixed
x
, the Zak transform is a Fourier series with coefficients
f(x+k)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. If
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then these coefficients are in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
for almost all
x
. Hence, Plancherel’s theorem for Fourier series implies

\int_{\mathcal{Q}}|Zf(x,\omega)|^{2}\,d\omega=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|f(x+k)|^{2}.

(9.27)

For the
x
-integration, the periodization trick yields

\int_{\mathcal{Q}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{Q}}|Zf(x,\omega)|^{2}\,d\omega\right)\,dx=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{\mathcal{Q}}|f(x+k)|^{2}\,dx=\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(9.28)

Thus,
Z
is an isometry on the subspace
W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By a density argument,
Z
therefore extends to an isometry on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Next, note that
Z\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathcal{Q}}(x,\omega)=1
for almost all
(x,\omega)\in\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}
and by (
9.11
)

Z(M_{l}T_{k}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathcal{Q}})(x,\omega)=e^{2\pi i(l\cdot x-k\cdot\omega)},

(9.29)

for
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
(x,\omega)\in\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}
. This means that the Zak transform
Z
maps the orthonormal basis
\mathcal{G}(\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathcal{Q}},\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
onto the orthonormal basis
\{e^{2\pi i(l\cdot x-k\cdot\omega)}\mid k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}
of
L^{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})
. Therefore,
Z
is surjective and, hence, a unitary operator.
∎

We are now going to study the Zak transform and its relationship to Gabor frames. We start with a result on the Gabor frame operator.

Theorem 9.6
.

Let
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and consider the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{2d}
and write
S_{g}
for the associated Gabor frame operator. Then
24
24

24

We may use different windows
g
and
\widetilde{g}
for the analysis and synthesis process. In this case the proof shows that
Z(S_{g,\widetilde{g}}f)=\overline{Zg}Z\widetilde{g}Zf
.

Z(S_{g}f)=|Zg|^{2}Zf.

(9.30)

Thus, the Gabor frame operator is equivalent to the multiplication operator
ZS_{g}Z^{-1}
with multiplier
|Zg|^{2}
on
L^{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})
.

Proof.

We have

Z(M_{l}T_{k}g)(x,\omega)=e^{2\pi i(l\cdot x-k\cdot\omega)}Zg(x,\omega).

(9.31)

Hence,

\displaystyle Z(S_{g}f)(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\langle f,M_{l}T_{k}g\rangle\,Z(M_{l}T_{k}g)(x,\omega)

(9.32)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\langle Zf,Z(M_{l}T_{k}g)\rangle Z(M_{l}T_{k}g)(x,\omega)

(9.33)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\iint_{\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}}Zf(\xi,\eta)\overline{Zg(\xi,\eta)}e^{-2\pi i(l\cdot\xi-k\cdot\eta)}\,d(\xi,\eta)\,e^{2\pi i(l\cdot x-k\cdot\omega)}\right)Zg(x,\omega).

(9.34)

Since
\{e_{k,l}(x,\omega)=e^{2\pi i(l\cdot x-k\cdot\omega)}\mid k,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}
is an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})
, the expression in the brackets is just the orthonormal expansion of

Zf\overline{Zg}=\sum_{k,l}\langle Zf\overline{Zg},e_{k,l}\rangle\,e_{k,l}

(9.35)

in
L^{2}(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})
. Therefore, the result follows.
∎

Corollary 9.7
.

Let
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and consider the Gabor system
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
.

(a)

S_{g}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if and only if
|Zg|^{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.

(b)

\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
is a frame if and only if

0<a\leq|Zg(x,\omega)|\leq b<\infty

(9.36)

for almost all
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. In this case the optimal frame bounds are

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\essinf_{(x,\omega)\in\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}}|Zg(x,\omega)|^{2},

(9.37)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\esssup_{(x,\omega)\in\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}}|Zg(x,\omega)|^{2}.

(9.38)

(c)

\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
is an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if and only if
|Zg(x,\omega)|^{2}=1
for almost all
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

Proof.

We note that a multiplication operator
f\mapsto mf
is bounded if and only if
m\in L^{\infty}
, and is invertible if and only if
m^{-1}\in L^{\infty}
(see, e.g.,
[
6
]
).
Therefore
(a)
and
(b)
follow immediately, since the conjugation
ZS_{g}Z^{-1}
preserves the spectrum.

As for
(c)
, if
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
is an orthonormal basis, then
S_{g}=I_{L^{2}}
, and consequently
ZS_{g}Z^{-1}
amounts to multiplication by
|Zg|^{2}=1
. Conversely, if
|Zg|^{2}=1
a.e., then by
(b)
,
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
is a tight frame with bounds
A=B=1
. By Theorem
9.5

\lVert g\rVert_{2}^{2}=\iint_{\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}}|Zg(x,\omega)|^{2}\,d(x,\omega)=1.

(9.39)

Now, since
\lVert M_{l}T_{k}g\rVert_{2}^{2}=\lVert g\rVert_{2}^{2}=1
, the Gabor system
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
is an orthonormal basis by Lemma
6.16
.
∎

9.2
The Balian-Low Theorem

The Balian-Low Theorem can be seen as a “no-go result” in the sense that it tells us that an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
cannot come from a Gabor system with a nice window. Now, there are many versions of Balian-Low type results and they can also be seen as uncertainty principles. We will state two versions of the Balian-Low theorem, one for functions in the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
(
d=1
), which is the original version, and one for the Wiener space
W(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Theorem 9.8

(Balian-Low, Hilbert space version)
.

If
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}^{2})
is an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, then either
x\,g(x)\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
or
g^{\prime}(x)\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
25
25

25

From the time-frequency analysis point of view, it would be more appropriate to say
either
x\,g(x)\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
or
\omega\,\widehat{g}(\omega)\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

The proof relies on the commutation relations for the position and momentum operators, which are defined as

Xg(x)=xg(x)\quad\text{ and }\quad Pg(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\,g^{\prime}(x).

(9.40)

Recall that
X
and
P
are self-adjoint and that

(PX-XP)g=\frac{1}{2\pi i}g,

(9.41)

for
g\in\text{dom}(XP)\cap\text{dom}(PX)
. Furthermore, we have
\mathcal{F}(Pg)=X\mathcal{F}g
.

We prove the theorem by contradiction. Suppose
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z})
is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, in particular
g\neq 0
, and suppose that
Xg\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
Pg\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Then, using the orthonormal expansion of
Xg
, we have

\langle Xg,Pg\rangle=\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle Xg,M_{l}T_{k}g\rangle\langle M_{l}T_{k}g,Pg\rangle.

(9.42)

We will now re-write the inner products in the series expansion. Since

XM_{l}T_{k}g(x)=(k+x-k)e^{2\pi il\cdot x}g(x-k)=kM_{l}T_{k}g(x)+M_{l}T_{k}Xg(x),

(9.43)

we have

\displaystyle\langle Xg,M_{l}T_{k}g\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle g,XM_{l}T_{k}g\rangle

(9.44)

\displaystyle=k\langle g,M_{l}T_{k}g\rangle+\langle g,M_{l}T_{k}Xg\rangle

(9.45)

\displaystyle=0+\langle T_{-k}M_{-l}g,Xg\rangle,

(9.46)

where we used the fact that
\langle g,M_{l}T_{k}g\rangle=0
because we assume
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z})
to be an orthonormal basis. Similarly,

\displaystyle\langle PM_{l}T_{k}g,g\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle XT_{l}M_{-k}\widehat{g},\widehat{g}\rangle

(9.47)

\displaystyle=l\langle T_{l}M_{-k}\widehat{g},\widehat{g}\rangle+\langle T_{l}M_{-k}X\widehat{g},\widehat{g}\rangle

(9.48)

\displaystyle=\langle M_{l}T_{k}Pg,g\rangle.

(9.49)

Combining the above results, we obtain

\langle Xg,Pg\rangle=\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle Pg,T_{-k}M_{-l}g\rangle\langle T_{-k}M_{-l}g,Xg\rangle=\langle Pg,Xg\rangle.

(9.50)

If we knew that
g\in\text{dom}(PX)\cap\text{dom}(XP)
, then we could rewrite this identity as

0=\langle(PX-XP)g,g\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lVert g\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(9.51)

We choose a sequence
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
, such that
\lVert g_{n}-g\rVert_{2}\to 0
,
\lVert Xg_{n}-Xg\rVert_{2}\to 0
and
\lVert Pg_{n}-Pg\rVert_{2}\to 0
(such a sequence exists). Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\langle Xg_{n},Pg_{n}\rangle-\langle Pg_{n},Xg_{n}\rangle\right)=\langle Xg,Pg\rangle-\langle Pg,Xg\rangle=0.

(9.52)

On the other hand, since
g_{n}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\subset\text{dom}(PX)\cap\text{dom}(XP)
, this limit is also

\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle(PX-XP)g_{n},g_{n}\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert g_{n}\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\lVert g\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(9.53)

Thus,
g=0
, contradicting the assumption that
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z})
is an orthonormal basis.
∎

Remark
.

While the classical uncertainty principle provides a lower bound on the deviations in time and frequency, i.e.,

\lVert Xg\rVert_{2}\lVert Pg\rVert_{2}=\lVert Xg\rVert_{2}\lVert X\widehat{g}\rVert_{2}\geq\frac{1}{4\pi}\lVert g\rVert_{2}^{2},

(9.54)

the Balian-Low theorem (BLT) implies that a window of an orthonormal Gabor basis possesses the maximal uncertainty

\lVert Xg\rVert_{2}\lVert Pg\rVert_{2}=\infty.

(9.55)

For the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, this result holds for the conjugate variables
(x_{k},\omega_{k})
,
k=1,\ldots,d
.

There are also more general versions for (symplectic) lattices and more general index sets. For more details on this we refer, e.g., to
[
22
]
.

\diamond

We will now state the Wiener amalgam version of the BLT.

Theorem 9.9

(BLT, Wiener amalgam version)
.

If
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
is a frame for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then both
g\notin W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\widehat{g}\notin W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

To prove the amalgam version of the BLT we show a quite special property of the Zak transform.

Lemma 9.10
.

If
Zf
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, then
Zf
has a zero in
\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}
.

Proof.

Assume that
Zf(x,\omega)\neq 0
for all
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. Since
Zf
is continuous and
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
is simply connected, there exists a continuous function
\varphi(x,\omega)
, such that

Zf(x,\omega)=|Zf(x,\omega)|e^{2\pi i\varphi(x,\omega)}.

(9.56)

We note that in this particular case a continuous logarithm exists. The quasi-periodicity

Zf(x+k,\omega+l)=e^{2\pi ik\cdot\omega}Zf(x,\omega)

(9.57)

implies the existence of a function
\kappa:\mathbb{Z}^{2d}\to\mathbb{Z}
, such that

\varphi(x+k,\omega+l)=\varphi(x,\omega)+k\cdot\omega+\kappa(k,l).

(9.58)

We compute
\varphi(k,l)=\varphi(0,0)+\kappa(k,l)
in two ways:

\displaystyle\varphi(k,l)

\displaystyle=\varphi(0,l)+k\cdot l+\kappa(k,0)

(9.59)

\displaystyle=\left(\varphi(0,0)+\kappa(0,l)\right)+k\cdot l+\kappa(k,0)

(9.60)

and

\varphi(k,l)=\varphi(k,0)+\kappa(0,l)=\left(\varphi(0,0)+\kappa(k,0)\right)+\kappa(0,l).

(9.61)

The two resulting expressions for
\kappa
yield the contradiction

\kappa(k,l)=\kappa(0,l)+\kappa(k,0)+k\cdot l=\kappa(0,l)+\kappa(k,0).

(9.62)

This contradiction shows that the initial assumption
Zf(x,\omega)\neq 0
cannot hold.
∎

Proof.

(
BLT, Wiener amalgam version
).
If the window
g\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
Zg
is continuous by Lemma
9.2
. Hence,
Zg
has a zero in
\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q}
by the above lemma. Therefore, by Corollary
9.7
the lower frame bound vanishes and
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
cannot be a frame. Hence, if
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
is a frame, then
g\notin W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

We note that
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
is a frame if and only if
\mathcal{G}(\widehat{g},\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
is a frame. Therefore,
\widehat{g}\notin W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as well.
∎

At this point, it should be mentioned that neither of the presented versions of the BLT implies the other
[
19
, Chap. 8.4, Rem. 3]
. We note that there are similar results for other function spaces as well (re-call also metatheorems
A
,
B
,
C
). The common theme is the impossibility of constructing an orthonormal Gabor basis from a nice function with a nice Fourier transform. This impossibility of the existence of ONBs of the form
\mathcal{G}(g,\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z})
with (mild) decay and smoothness conditions on
g
is the very reason why over-complete Gabor systems are studied and used in applications.

9.3
Density of Gabor Frames

The Balian-Low theorem states that we cannot obtain an orthonormal basis from a Gabor system where the window
g
has good time-frequency concentration. It is accompanied by the density principle, which should be seen as an uncertainty principle for Gabor systems. In order to prove the density principle, we will introduce necessary results (partially) without proof. The results are part of a general duality theory for Gabor systems
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
and
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda^{\circ})
. We refer to the treatise
[
23
]
(see also
[
38
]
).

The
Fundamental Identity of Gabor Analysis
(FIGA) is a main tool to relate a Gabor system
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
to its adjoint system
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda^{\circ})
. This is of interest for the characterization of Gabor frames. So far, we have seen the frame inequality, which gives the condition on a set to be a frame. On the other hand, there are Riesz sequences, which are defined in a similar, but contrary manner. A sequence (or set)
\{f_{\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}
in a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is a Riesz sequence if and only if there exist positive constants
0<A\leq B<\infty
such that

A\lVert c\rVert_{\ell^{2}}^{2}\leq\lVert\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c_{\gamma}f_{\gamma}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq B\lVert c\rVert_{\ell^{2}}^{2},\qquad\forall c=(c_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\in\ell^{2}(\Gamma).

(9.63)

A Riesz sequence is called Riesz basis if it is complete in
\mathcal{H}
, i.e., its linear span is dense in
\mathcal{H}
.

The completeness of a Riesz basis is an additional assumption and does not follow from the norm equivalence (
9.63
): Take an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{H}
and remove one element. This family still satisfies (
9.63
), but its linear span is clearly not dense in
\mathcal{H}
. In particular, a Riesz sequence need not be a frame since the latter is always (over)complete.

Conversely, a frame need not be a Riesz sequence: The union of two orthonormal bases is a frame, but there exists a non-trivial linear combination of zero since this system is linearly dependent. This contradicts the lower bound in (
9.63
).

We will not further elaborate on the general theory of Riesz sequences and frames, but state “the duality” between the Gabor systems
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
and
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda^{\circ})
in terms of frames and Riesz sequences.

The first important result is the following.

Theorem 9.11

(Bessel Duality)
.

Let
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
be a lattice. Then
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
is a Bessel sequence if and only if
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda^{\circ})
is a Bessel sequence.

The structures of
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
and its dual system
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda^{\circ})
are intimately related: A Gabor frame on one side corresponds to a Riesz sequence on the other. Furthermore, dual windows of
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
are characterized by a biorthogonality condition along the adjoint lattice.

We introduce a more general notion of the frame operator. The frame operator with analysis window
g
and synthesis window
\widetilde{g}
and index set
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
is given by

S_{g,\widetilde{g},\Gamma}f=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\langle f,\pi(\gamma)g\rangle\pi(\gamma)\widetilde{g}.

(9.64)

Definition 9.12
.

Let
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
be a Bessel sequence. We call
\widetilde{g}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
a dual window to
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
, if
\mathcal{G}(\widetilde{g},\Lambda)
is a Bessel sequence and the reconstruction property
S_{g,\widetilde{g},\Lambda}=S_{\widetilde{g},g,\Lambda}=I
is satisfied.

Now, if
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
is a Gabor frame, then there exists a dual window
\widetilde{g}
, such that
S_{g,\widetilde{g},\Lambda}=S_{\widetilde{g},g,\Lambda}=I
. We state the result without proof and refer to
[
19
]
or
[
21
]
.

Theorem 9.13

(Wexler-Raz Biorthogonality relations)
.

Let
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
be a lattice. Then, the following are equivalent.

(i)

\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
is a frame for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(ii)

\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda^{\circ})
is a Bessel sequence and there exists a dual window
\widetilde{g}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\mathcal{G}(\widetilde{g},\Lambda^{\circ})
is a Bessel sequence and

\langle\widetilde{g},\pi(\lambda^{\circ})g\rangle=\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)\delta_{0,\lambda^{\circ}},\quad\forall\lambda^{\circ}\in\Lambda^{\circ}.

(9.65)

We remark that the proof relies on the Fundamental Identity of Gabor Analysis, which we will state (and prove) after the following lemma, which we state without proof (see
[
38
, Chap. 2]
for the details).

Lemma 9.14
.

Let
g,\widetilde{g}\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
f\in M^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
h\in M^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Then, the product of the STFTs
\overline{V_{g}f}\,V_{\widetilde{g}}h
is in
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.

Theorem 9.15

(Fundamental Identity of Gabor Analysis)
.

Let
f,h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
g,\widetilde{g}\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
a lattice. Then

\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}V_{g}f(\lambda)\,\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}h(\lambda)}=\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\lambda^{\circ}\in\Lambda^{\circ}}V_{g}\widetilde{g}(\lambda^{\circ})\,\overline{V_{f}h(\lambda^{\circ})},

(9.66)

with absolute convergence on both sides.

Proof.

The technical assumptions for the Poisson summation formula to hold point-wise are fulfilled by Lemma
9.14
. We will use the symplectic Fourier transform and the symplectic version of the Poisson summation formula to prove the result.

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}V_{g}f(\lambda)\,\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}h(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\lambda^{\circ}\in\Lambda^{\circ}}\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}\left(V_{g}f\,\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}h}\right)(\lambda^{\circ})

(9.67)

We write the symplectic Fourier transform explicitly as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}\left(V_{g}f\,\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}h}\right)(z)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(z^{\prime})\,\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}h(z^{\prime})}e^{-2\pi i\sigma(z^{\prime},z)}\,dz^{\prime}.

(9.68)

Now, from the commutation relations (
1.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\pi(z^{\prime})^{-1}\pi(z)\pi(z^{\prime})=e^{2\pi i\sigma(z^{\prime},z)}\pi(z)\quad\text{ and }\quad V_{\pi(z^{\prime})g}(\pi(z^{\prime})f)(z)

\displaystyle=\langle f,\pi(z^{\prime})^{-1}\pi(z)\pi(z^{\prime})g\rangle

(9.69)

\displaystyle=e^{-2\pi i\sigma(z^{\prime},z)}V_{g}f(z).

(9.70)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}\left(V_{g}f\,\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}h}\right)(z)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(z^{\prime})\,\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}h(z^{\prime})}e^{-2\pi i\sigma(z^{\prime},z)}\,dz^{\prime}

(9.71)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}V_{g}f(z^{\prime})\,\overline{V_{\pi(-z)\widetilde{g}}(\pi(-z)h)(z^{\prime})}\,dz^{\prime}

(9.72)

\displaystyle=\langle f,\pi(-z)h\rangle\overline{\langle g,\pi(-z)\widetilde{g}\rangle},

(9.73)

where we used the orthognoality relation (
2.27
) to obtain the last line in the above equation. Plugging this into (
9.67
), we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}V_{g}f(\lambda)\,\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}h(\lambda)}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\lambda^{\circ}\in\Lambda^{\circ}}\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}\left(V_{g}f\,\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}h}\right)(\lambda^{\circ})

(9.74)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\lambda^{\circ}\in\Lambda^{\circ}}V_{h}f(-\lambda^{\circ})\overline{V_{\widetilde{g}}g(-\lambda^{\circ})}

(9.75)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\lambda^{\circ}\in\Lambda^{\circ}}V_{g}\widetilde{g}(\lambda^{\circ})\overline{V_{f}h(\lambda^{\circ})}.

(9.76)

∎

Remark
.

The frame inequality combined with Theorem
9.15
yields the following bounds on the sharp upper frame bound.

Let
g\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\lVert g\rVert_{2}=1
and
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
a lattice. Let
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
be a Gabor system. Then
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
is actually a Bessel sequence with sharp Bessel bound
B
and we have

\lVert(V_{g}g(\lambda))\rVert_{\ell^{2}(\Lambda)}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|V_{g}g(\lambda)|^{2}\leq B\leq\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\lambda^{\circ}\in\Lambda^{\circ}}|V_{g}g(\Lambda^{\circ})|=\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\lVert(V_{g}g(\lambda^{\circ}))\rVert_{\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{\circ})}.

(9.77)

The lower bound follows immediately from the frame inequality by setting
f=g
. The upper frame bound follows by using the FIGA with
h=f
and
\widetilde{g}=g
;

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|V_{g}f(\lambda)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}V_{g}f(\lambda)\overline{V_{g}f(\lambda)}

(9.78)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\lambda^{\circ}\in\Lambda^{\circ}}V_{g}g(\Lambda^{\circ})\overline{V_{f}f(\lambda^{\circ})}

(9.79)

\displaystyle\leq\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\lambda^{\circ}\in\Lambda^{\circ}}|V_{g}g(\Lambda^{\circ})||Af(\lambda^{\circ})|

(9.80)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\lambda^{\circ}\in\Lambda^{\circ}}|V_{g}g(\lambda^{\circ})|\right)\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(9.81)

Here, we used the fact that
|V_{f}f(x,\omega)|=|Af(x,\omega)|\leq Af(0,0)=\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}
from Lemma
3.3
. Setting

\widetilde{B}=\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)^{-1}\sum_{\lambda^{\circ}\in\Lambda^{\circ}}|V_{g}g(\lambda^{\circ})|

(9.82)

we see that

\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|V_{g}f(\lambda)|^{2}\leq\widetilde{B}\lVert f\rVert,\quad\forall f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(9.83)

Hence,
\widetilde{B}
is a Bessel bound (not necessarily the sharpest one) for the Gabor system
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda^{\circ})
and we have
B\leq\widetilde{B}
, which finishes the proof of (
9.77
).

For Gabor systems of the form
\mathcal{G}(g,\alpha\mathbb{Z}\times\beta\mathbb{Z})
with
g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
the result was proven by Tolimieri and Orr
[
51
]
. Therefore,
\widetilde{B}
might also be referred to as the Tolimieri-Orr bound.

\diamond

The Wexler-Raz biorthogonality relations yield a characterization of all dual windows in the lattice case.

Corollary 9.16
.

Suppose
g,\widetilde{g}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
a lattice such that
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
and
\mathcal{G}(\widetilde{g},\Lambda)
are Bessel sequences. Then
\widetilde{g}
is a dual window to
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
if and only if the Wexler-Raz biorthogonality relations (
9.65
) are satisfied.

We can now state and proof the density theorem for Gabor systems.

Theorem 9.17

(Density Theorem)
.

Let
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
be a lattice. If
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
is a frame, then
\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)\leq 1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
be a frame and write
S_{\mathcal{G}}
for the associated Gabor frame operator. Since in this case the frame operator is a positive invertible operator, the operator
S_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1/2}
is a well-defined positive operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In particular it is self-adjoint.

By Corollary
9.16
, the canonical dual window
g^{\circ}=S^{-1}_{\mathcal{G}}g
fulfills the Wexler-Raz biorthogonality relations (
9.65
). This yields

\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)=\langle S^{-1}_{\mathcal{G}}g,g\rangle=\langle S^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{G}}g,S^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{G}}g\rangle=\lVert S^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{G}}g\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(9.84)

By Lemma
6.16

\mathcal{G}(S^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{G}}g,\Lambda)
is a tight frame with frame bounds
A=B=1
. Therefore

\displaystyle\lVert S_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1/2}g\rVert_{2}^{4}

\displaystyle=|\langle S^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{G}}g,S^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{G}}g\rangle|^{2}

(9.85)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|\langle S^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{G}}g,S^{-1/2}_{\mathcal{G}}\pi(\lambda)g\rangle|^{2}=\lVert S^{-1/2}g\rVert_{2}^{2}.

(9.86)

Hence, we obtain

\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)=\lVert S_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1/2}g\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 1.

(9.87)

∎

Note that while the density principle gives a necessary condition (which might be far from sufficiency), the BLT states that this necessary density condition is never sharp for “nice” windows.

Corollary 9.18
.

If
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)
is a frame for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and if either
g\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
or
\widehat{g}\in W_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
\textnormal{vol}(\Lambda)<1
.

In particular, if we consider the standard Gaussian window
g_{0}(t)=2^{d/4}e^{-\pi t^{2}}
, then we see that
\mathcal{G}(g_{0},\mathbb{Z}^{2d})
is not a frame for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. This holds more generally for any set
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with lower Beurling density
D^{-}(\Gamma)=1
.

10
The Bargmann Transform

For many reasons the Gaussian function is one of the most popular windows in time-frequency analysis and it is of special interest to study the STFT and Gabor systems with a Gaussian window.

Recall that the
L^{2}
-normalized standard Gaussian on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is given by

g_{0}(t)=2^{d/4}e^{-\pi t^{2}}.

(10.1)

Then

\displaystyle V_{g_{0}}f(x,\omega)

\displaystyle=2^{d/4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)e^{-\pi(t-x)^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(10.2)

\displaystyle=2^{d/4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)e^{-\pi t^{2}}e^{2\pi x\cdot t}e^{-\pi x^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\omega\cdot t}\,dt

(10.3)

\displaystyle=2^{d/4}e^{-\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(x^{2}+\omega^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)e^{-\pi t^{2}}e^{2\pi t\cdot(x-i\omega)}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(x-i\omega)^{2}}\,dt.

(10.4)

Let us convert
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
into a complex vector
z=x+i\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
. Recall that, for complex vectors,
w\cdot z=w^{T}z
does not yield the inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
, so
z^{2}=(x+iy)\cdot(x+iy)
, whereas
|z|^{2}=\overline{z}\cdot z=(x-iy)\cdot(x+iy)
. Then, comparing with the formula above, the following definition appears quite naturally.

Definition 10.1
.

The Bargmann transform of a function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the function
Bf
on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
defined by

Bf(z)=2^{d/4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t)e^{2\pi t\cdot z-\pi t^{2}-\frac{\pi}{2}z^{2}}\,dt.

(10.5)

The (Bargmann-)Fock space
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
is the Hilbert space of all entire functions
F
on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
for which the norm

\lVert F\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{d}}|F(z)|^{2}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}\,dz

(10.6)

is finite. The inner product on
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
is given by

\langle F,G\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{d}}F(z)\overline{G(z)}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}\,dz.

(10.7)

By means of the small calculation in (
10.2
), we may re-write several of the statements about the STFT by means of the Bargmann transform.

Proposition 10.2
.

(a)

If
f
is a function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with polynomial growth, then its Bargmann transform is an entire function on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
. If we write
z=x+i\omega
, then

V_{g_{0}}f(x,-\omega)=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}Bf(z).

(10.8)

(b)

If
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{d}}|Bf(z)|^{2}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}\,dz=\lVert Bf\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}^{2}.

(10.9)

Thus,
B
is an isometry from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
.

Proof.

(a)

If
|f(t)|=\mathcal{O}(|t|^{N})
, then the integral in (
10.5
) converges absolutely for every
z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
and uniformly over compact sets in
\mathbb{C}^{d}
. Therefore, one can differentiate under the integral (with respect to
z
) and, hence,
Bf(z)
is an entire function.

Equation (
10.8
) is just (
10.2
) rewritten in another notation.

(b)

The statement follows form the fact that

\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{2}=\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}

(10.10)

and
\lVert g_{0}\rVert_{2}=1
.

∎

The next aim is to show that
B
is a unitary mapping from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
onto
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
. In view of the above proposition, we only need to show that the range of
B
is dense in
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
. For this purpose we will carry out a study of entire functions of several complex variables. Due to the use of the multi-index notation, the difference to the theory in one variable will almost not be visible.

Theorem 10.3
.

(a)

The collection of monomials of the form

e_{\alpha}(z)=\left(\frac{\pi^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\right)^{1/2}z^{\alpha}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\left(\frac{\pi^{\alpha_{k}}}{\alpha_{k}!}\right)^{1/2}z_{k}^{\alpha_{k}},

(10.11)

\alpha=(a_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})
,
\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, forms an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
.

(b)

\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
is a reproducing kernel Hilbert space, that is

|F(z)|\leq\lVert F\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}\,e^{\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}},\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}.

(10.12)

The reproducing kernel is
K_{w}(z)=e^{\pi\overline{w}\cdot z}
, i.e.,

F(w)=\langle F,K_{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}.

(10.13)

Proof.

(a)

We write each variable in polar coordinates;
z_{k}=r_{k}e^{i\theta_{k}}
. We start with computing the inner product of
z^{\alpha}
with
z^{\beta}
restricted to the poly-disc

P_{R}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}\mid|z_{k}|\leq R,\,k=1,\ldots d\}:

(10.14)

\displaystyle\langle z^{\alpha},z^{\beta}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{P_{R}}z^{\alpha}\overline{z^{\beta}}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}

(10.15)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\int_{|z_{k}|\leq R}z_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}\overline{z_{k}}^{\beta_{k}}e^{-\pi|z_{k}|^{2}}\,dz_{k}

(10.16)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{R}\int_{0}^{2\pi}r_{k}^{\alpha_{k}+\beta_{k}+1}e^{i(\alpha_{k}-\beta_{k})\theta_{k}}e^{-\pi r_{k}^{2}}\,d\theta_{k}\,dr_{k}

(10.17)

If
\alpha\neq\beta
, then this integral equals 0 for all
R>0
and therefore

\langle z^{\alpha},z^{\beta}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{P_{R}}z^{\alpha}\overline{z^{\beta}}e^{-\pi|z^{2}|}\,dz=0.

(10.18)

On the other hand, if
\alpha=\beta
then

\int_{P_{R}}|z^{\alpha}|^{2}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}\,dz=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\left(2\pi\int_{0}^{R}r^{2\alpha_{k}+1}e^{-\pi r_{k}^{2}}\,dr_{k}\right)=\mu_{\alpha,R}

(10.19)

Consequently
\{\mu^{-1/2}_{\alpha,R}z^{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\}
is an orthonormal system in
L^{2}(P_{R},e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}\,dz)
. For
R=\infty
, by making the change of variables
s=\pi r^{2}
(
dr=\frac{ds}{2\pi r}
), we can continue as follows:

\mu_{\alpha,\infty}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{s}{\pi}\right)^{\alpha_{k}}e^{-s}\,ds\right)=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{k})}{\pi^{\alpha_{k}}}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\frac{\alpha_{k}!}{\pi^{\alpha_{k}}}=\frac{\alpha!}{\pi^{|\alpha|}}.

(10.20)

In particular, we see that
\{e_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\}
is an orthonormal system in
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
.

To prove completeness of the system in
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
, we start from the power series expansion of
F
in
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
, which has the form

F(z)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}c_{\alpha}z^{\alpha}.

(10.21)

Suppose that
\langle F,e_{\beta}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}=0
for all
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
. Then

\langle F,e_{\beta}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\left(\frac{\pi^{|\beta|}}{\beta!}\right)^{1/2}\int_{P_{R}}\left(\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}c_{\alpha}z^{\alpha}\right)\overline{z^{\beta}}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}\,dz.

(10.22)

Since the power series expansion in the integral converges uniformly on compact sets, we can interchange the order of integration and summation to obtain

\int_{P_{R}}F(z)\overline{z^{\beta}}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}\,dz=\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}c_{\alpha}\int_{P_{R}}z^{\alpha}\overline{z^{\beta}}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}\,dz=c_{\beta}\,\mu_{\beta,R}.

(10.23)

Thus, by assumption

\langle F,e_{\beta}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}=\left(\frac{\pi^{|\beta|}}{\beta!}\right)^{1/2}c_{\beta}\lim_{\mathbb{R}\to\infty}\mu_{\beta,R}=0.

(10.24)

This implies that
c_{\beta}=0
for all
\beta
and thus
F\equiv 0
. As
\{e_{\alpha}\}
is an orthonormal system in
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
, it now follows that it is an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
.

(b)

Since
F(z)=\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}\langle F,e_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}\,e_{\alpha}(z)
, we obtain by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

|F(z)|\leq\left(\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}|\langle F,e_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}\frac{\pi^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}|z^{\alpha}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}=\lVert F\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}\,e^{\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}.

(10.25)

Thus, point evaluations are continuous linear functionals on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
. By the Riesz representation theorem it follows that for each
w\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
there is a function
K_{w}\in\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
such that

F(w)=\langle F,K_{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}

(10.26)

Expanding
K_{w}
with respect to the orthonormal basis
e_{\alpha}
and using the above equation, we obtain
K_{w}
explicitly as

\displaystyle K_{w}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}\langle K_{w},e_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}\,e_{\alpha}(z)

(10.27)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}\overline{e_{\alpha}(w)}\,e_{\alpha}(z)

(10.28)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\geq 0}\frac{\pi^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\overline{w^{\alpha}}\,z^{\alpha}

(10.29)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi\overline{w}\cdot z}.

(10.30)

∎

Equipped with the reproducing kernel
K_{w}
for
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
, we can prove that the Bargmann transform maps onto
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
.

Theorem 10.4
.

The Bargmann transform is a unitary operator from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
onto
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
.

Proof.

In Proposition
10.2
we have proved that the Bargmann transform is an isometry. Thus its range is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
. Therefore, if we show that
B(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is dense in
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
, then it follows that
B(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))=\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
, so
B
is surjective, which proves the claim.

Using Example
2.3
together with the orthogonality relations
2.7
we obtain

V_{g_{0}}(M_{\eta}T_{\xi}g_{0})(x,\omega)=e^{-\pi i(x+\xi)\cdot(\omega-\eta)}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}((x-\xi)^{2}+(\omega-\eta)^{2})}.

(10.31)

On the other hand, writing
z=x+i\omega
and
w=\xi+i\eta
, we obtain

\displaystyle B(M_{-\eta}T_{\xi}g_{0})(z)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}V_{g_{0}}(M_{-\eta}T_{\xi}g_{0})(x,-\omega)

(10.32)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{\frac{\pi}{2}(x^{2}+\omega^{2})}e^{-\pi i(x+\xi)\cdot(-\omega+\eta)}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}((x-\xi)^{2}+(-\omega+\eta)^{2})}

(10.33)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i\xi\cdot\eta}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(\xi^{2}+\eta^{2})}e^{\pi(x\cdot\xi+\omega\cdot\eta+i(\xi\cdot\omega-x\cdot\eta))}

(10.34)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i\xi\cdot\eta}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}e^{\pi\overline{w}\cdot z}.

(10.35)

We can rewrite this in short as

B(M_{-\eta}T_{\xi}g_{0})(z)=e^{-\pi i\xi\cdot\eta}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}K_{w}(z).

(10.36)

This shows that the reproducing kernel of
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
is in the range of
B
. Now suppose that for some
F\in\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
we have
\langle F,Bf\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}=0
for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In particular, by the above equation we have for all
w\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
that

\displaystyle 0=\langle F,B(M_{-\eta}T_{\xi}g_{0})\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}=e^{\pi i\xi\cdot\eta}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}\langle F,K_{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}=e^{\pi i\xi\cdot\eta}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}F(w).

(10.37)

Therefore,
F\equiv 0
and therefore the range of
B
is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
∎

Note that the Bargmann transform sends the collection of time-frequency shifted Gaussians
\{M_{-\eta}T_{\xi}g_{0}\}
to the normalized reproducing kernel (up to a phase factor).

\displaystyle\lVert B(M_{-\eta}T_{\xi}g_{0})\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}K_{w}(z)\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{d}}|e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}K_{w}(z)|^{2}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}\,dz

(10.38)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{d}}e^{-\pi|w|^{2}}e^{\pi(\overline{w}\cdot z+w\cdot\overline{z})}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}\,dz

(10.39)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{d}}e^{-\pi|w-z|^{2}}\,dz=1,\quad\forall w\in\mathbb{C}^{d}.

(10.40)

The material after this point has not been presented in the lecture course. The only exception is Theorem
11.4
(ii).

Since the Bargmann transform is a unitary operator, the pre-image of the orthonormal basis
\{e_{\alpha}\}
consisting of the functions
h_{\alpha}=B^{-1}e_{\alpha}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The functions
h_{\alpha}
are the Hermite functions. Even without an explicit formula we can derive their most important property for Fourier analysis.

Proposition 10.5
.

The Hermite functions are eigenfunctions of the Fourier transform; specifically, for all
\alpha\geq 0
we have

\mathcal{F}h_{\alpha}=(-i)^{|\alpha|}h_{\alpha}.

(10.41)

Proof.

For the proof we combine the fundamental identity (
2.18
) with Proposition
10.2
.

Writing
z=x+i\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
, we have

V_{g_{0}}h_{\alpha}(x,-\omega)=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}Bh_{\alpha}(z)e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}e_{\alpha}.

(10.42)

On the other hand, using (
2.18
) and the property
\mathcal{F}g_{0}=g_{0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle V_{g_{0}}\widehat{h_{\alpha}}(x,-\omega)

\displaystyle=V_{\widehat{g_{0}}}\widehat{h_{\alpha}}(x,-\omega)

(10.43)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g_{0}}h_{\alpha}(\omega,x)

(10.44)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{-\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}Bh_{\alpha}(\omega-ix)

(10.45)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}\left(\frac{\pi^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\right)^{1/2}(\omega-ix)^{\alpha}

(10.46)

\displaystyle=e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}\left(\frac{\pi^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\right)^{1/2}(-iz)^{\alpha}

(10.47)

\displaystyle=(-i)^{|\alpha|}e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}e_{\alpha}(z).

(10.48)

Since
V_{g_{0}}
is one-to-one, it follows that
\mathcal{F}h_{\alpha}=(-i)^{|\alpha|}h_{\alpha}
.
∎

We note that if
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a finite linear combination of Hermite functions, then
\lVert f\rVert_{2}=\lVert\widehat{f}\rVert_{2}
. However, this is not an independent proof of Plancherel’s theorem because the argument is circular. We have used Plancherel’s theorem to show that the Bargmann transform is an isometry. In order to derive the result we also used that
\lVert V_{g}f\rVert_{2}^{2}=\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}^{2}
, which is a corollary of the orthogonality relations (
2.27
) and in order to derive these, we used Plancherel’s theorem. The insight of this statement is that the unitarity of the Fourier transform and of the Bargmann transform are equivalent. The results in this section show how special the Gaussian function is, because the STFT with a Gaussian window is, except for a weighting factor, an entire function. Hence, we can switch to complex analysis to prove statements about
V_{g_{0}}
. This is the main reason why much more results are known for the STFT with a Gaussian window than with other windows. The fact that complex analysis in several variables also differs greatly from the one-dimensional theory is the main reason why, despite knowing much about STFTs with one-dimensional Gaussian windows, higher dimensional Gaussian Gabor Systems still keep many mysteries.

Getting back to unitary representations of the Heisenberg Group, we may now write the Heisenberg group as
\mathbf{H}=\mathbb{C}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
. For
z=x+i\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
and
w=\xi+i\eta
, we compute
B\rho(z,\tau)f
for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We start by writing
Bf(w)
as a representation coefficient. Recall that
\langle f,\rho(x,\omega,\tau)g\rangle=e^{-2\pi i\tau}e^{\pi ix\cdot\omega}V_{g}f(x,\omega)
. Then

\displaystyle Bf(w)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i\xi\cdot\eta}e^{\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}V_{g_{0}}f(\xi,-\eta)

(10.49)

\displaystyle=e^{-\pi i\xi\cdot\eta}e^{\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}e^{-\pi i\xi\cdot(-\eta)}\langle f,\rho(\xi,-\eta,0)g_{0}\rangle

(10.50)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}\langle f,\rho(\overline{w},0)g_{0}\rangle.

(10.51)

Consequently,

\displaystyle B(\rho(z,\tau)f)(w)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}\langle\rho(z,\tau)f,\rho(\overline{w},0)g_{0}\rangle

(10.52)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}\langle f,\rho(-z,-\tau)\rho(\overline{w},0)g_{0}\rangle

(10.53)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi i\tau}e^{\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}e^{-\pi i\Im(-z\cdot\overline{\overline{w}})}\langle f,\rho(\overline{w}-z,0)g_{0}\rangle

(10.54)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi i\tau}e^{\pi i\Im(w\cdot z)}e^{\frac{\pi}{2}|w|^{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|w-\overline{z}|^{2}}Bf(w-\overline{z})

(10.55)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi i\tau}e^{\pi w\cdot z}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}B(f(w-\overline{z})).

(10.56)

Here, we used the fact that
|w|^{2}-|w-\overline{z}|^{2}=2\Re(\overline{w}\cdot z)-|z|^{2}

26
26

26

Also, note that the standard symplectic form is
\sigma(z,w)=\sigma((x,\omega),(\xi,\eta))=\Im(z\cdot\overline{\omega})
.
. Setting

\beta(z,\tau)F(w)=e^{2\pi i\tau}e^{\pi w\cdot z}e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}F(w-\overline{z}),

(10.57)

we have derived the Bargmann representation of the Heisenberg group. Moreover, we have shown that it is unitarily equivalent to the Schrödinger representation via the Bargmann transform, i.e.,

\beta(z,\tau)=\beta(x,\omega,\tau)=B\rho(x,\omega,\tau)B^{-1}.

(10.58)

11
The Frame Set of a Window Function

One main issue in Gabor analysis is to determine all Gabor systems
\mathcal{G}(g,\Gamma)
which form a Gabor frame. This question, however, is far too general to be answered completely. At the moment, the best we can hope for is to determine all sets
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
such that for a fixed window
g
the system
\mathcal{G}(g,\Gamma)
is a Gabor frame. Even this task is extremely challenging and no general method is known to solve this problem. It is a case-by-case study and the tools to be used may differ largely for different (classes of) windows. In order to have a more systematic approach to this, still, very general problem, the notion of the frame set has been introduced (see e.g.,
[
21
]
).

However, we may distinguish between several types of frame sets and we are now going to introduce the list which was set up in
[
9
]
. This generalizes the ideas from
[
21
]
.

For a fixed dimension
d
and a fixed window
g
we define:

•

The
\alpha
-frame set

\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}(g)=\{\alpha\mathbb{Z}^{2d}\mid G(g,\alpha\mathbb{Z}^{2d})\text{ is a frame}\}

(11.1)

•

The separable or
(\alpha,\beta)
-frame set

\mathfrak{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}(g)=\{\alpha\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\beta\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mid G(g,\alpha\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\beta\mathbb{Z}^{d})\text{ is a frame}\}

(11.2)

For
d>1
, we allow
\alpha
and
\beta
to be multi-indices (
\alpha\mathbb{Z}^{d}=\alpha_{1}\mathbb{Z}\times\ldots\times\alpha_{d}\mathbb{Z}
, same for
\beta
).

•

The lattice or
\Lambda
-frame set

\mathfrak{F}_{\Lambda}(g)=\{\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}\text{ lattice}\mid\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda)\text{ is a frame}\}

(11.3)

•

The frame set

\mathfrak{F}(g)=\{\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}\mid\mathcal{G}(g,\Gamma)\text{ is a frame}\}

(11.4)

We note that we have the following chain of inclusions

\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}(g)\subset\mathfrak{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}(g)\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\Lambda}(g)\subset\mathfrak{F}(g).

(11.5)

We remark that in
[
9
]
also the symplectic frame set has been introduced

\mathfrak{F}_{\sigma}(g)=\{\Lambda_{\sigma}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}\text{ symplectic lattice}\mid\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda_{\sigma})\text{ is a frame}\}.

(11.6)

The chain of inclusion given in
[
9
]
is however wrong. It is only true that

\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}(g)\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\sigma}(g)\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\Lambda}(g)\subset\mathfrak{F}(g),

(11.7)

but, in general,
\Lambda_{(\alpha,\beta)}
need not be in
\mathfrak{F}_{\sigma}(g)
even if
\mathcal{G}(g,\Lambda_{(\alpha,\beta)})
is a frame. However, if
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
(and not multi-indices), then
\mathfrak{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}(g)\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\sigma}(g)
.

It is of current interest to better understand Gabor systems with lattices which are not symplectic. This is due to the fact that it is unknown how to classify those lattices and how to build the proper metaplectic-like operators. This problem does not occur in dimension
d=1
as any 2-dimensional lattice is symplectic.

In the case
d=1
, some frame sets are known, even though there has been little progress until the work of Gröchenig and Stöckler
[
25
]
. The only frame set of a (family of) function(s) where the entire frame set is known, is the Gaussian, which is why we will treat it in a separate section. But before, we will give a list of functions (ignoring the Gaussian) where at least the separable frame set is known.

•

The one-sided exponential
e^{-t}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(t)\;(\notin M^{1}(\mathbb{R}))

[
33
]
(Janssen, 1996):

\mathfrak{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}(e^{-t}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(t))=\{\alpha\mathbb{Z}\times\beta\mathbb{Z}\mid\alpha\beta\leq 1\}

(11.8)

•

The hyperbolic secant
\tfrac{1}{\cosh(\pi t)}\;(\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}))

[
37
]
(Janssen and Strohmer, 2002):

\mathfrak{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}(\cosh(\pi t)^{-1})=\{\alpha\mathbb{Z}\times\beta\mathbb{Z}\mid\alpha\beta<1\}

(11.9)

•

The two-sided exponential
e^{-|t|}\;(\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}))

[
35
]
(Janssen, 2003):

\mathfrak{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}(e^{-|t|})=\{\alpha\mathbb{Z}\times\beta\mathbb{Z}\mid\alpha\beta<1\}

(11.10)

We say that the above frame sets are
full
, as the necessary condition implied by the corresponding Balian-Low theorem is also sufficient. We note that if
\mathcal{G}(g,\alpha\mathbb{Z}\times\beta\mathbb{Z})
is a frame, then so is
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{F}g,\beta\mathbb{Z}\times\alpha\mathbb{Z})
by the results on the symplectic and the metaplectic group in Section
7

27
27

27

Also re-call the Fundamental Identity of Time-Frequency Analysis
2.5
, which is often interpreted in the way that the Fourier transform rotates the time-frequency plane by
90^{\circ}
.
. Therefore, the functions

•

\mathcal{F}(e^{-t}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(t))(\omega)=\frac{1}{1+2\pi i\omega}

•

\mathcal{F}(e^{-|t|})(\omega)=\frac{2}{1+4\pi^{2}\omega^{2}}=\frac{2}{(1+2\pi i\omega)(1-2\pi i\omega)}

possess full frame sets as well. The hyperbolic secant is a fixed-point of the Fourier transform and, hence, its Fourier transform does not provide a new window function with a full frame set. Gröchenig and Stöckler
[
25
]
realized that the above mentioned window functions are so-called totally positive functions of finite type.

Definition 11.1
.

A non-constant, measurable function
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is called totally positive, if for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
and any two sets of increasing real numbers

x_{1}<x_{2}<\ldots<x_{N}\qquad\textnormal{ and }\qquad y_{1}<y_{2}<\ldots<y_{N},

(11.11)

the matrix

M_{g}=\left(g(x_{j}-y_{k})\right)_{j,k=1}^{N}

(11.12)

is positive semidefinite, i.e.,

x\cdot M_{g}x\geq 0,\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}.

(11.13)

There is a nice characterization of totally positive functions by means of their Fourier transform due to I. Schoenberg
[
45
,
46
]
28
28

28

Schoenberg’s characterization was actually in terms of the two-sided Laplace transform, but the characterization is basically the same.
, which might as well serve as their definition.

Theorem 11.2

(Schoenberg)
.

A function
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is totally positive if and only if its Fourier transform can be written as

\widehat{g}(\omega)=C\,e^{-\gamma\omega^{2}}e^{-2\pi i\delta\omega}\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{e^{2\pi i\delta_{k}\omega}}{1+2\pi i\delta_{k}\omega},

(11.14)

with
C>0
,
\gamma\geq 0
,
\delta,\delta_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<\gamma+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\delta_{k}^{2}<\infty
.

As we are interested in Gabor systems with totally positive functions, we may as well assume that
\delta=0
, as it corresponds to a time-shift of the function, which has no effect on the properties of the Gabor system. Hence, our functions of interest can be characterized by

\widehat{g}(\omega)=C\,e^{-\gamma\omega^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{e^{2\pi i\delta_{k}\omega}}{1+2\pi i\delta_{k}\omega}.

(11.15)

If
\gamma=0
and only finitely many
\delta_{k}
are different from zero, then we call
g
a totally positive function of finite type;

\widehat{g}(\omega)=C\,\prod_{k=1}^{M}\frac{e^{2\pi i\delta_{k}\omega}}{1+2\pi i\delta_{k}\omega},\qquad M\in\mathbb{N}.

(11.16)

If
\gamma\neq 0
and, still, only finitely many
\delta_{k}
are different from zero, we call
g
a totally positive function of finite Gaussian type;

\widehat{g}(\omega)=C\,e^{-\gamma\omega^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{M}\frac{e^{2\pi i\delta_{k}\omega}}{1+2\pi i\delta_{k}\omega},\qquad M\in\mathbb{N}.

(11.17)

The following result is due to Gröchenig and Stöckler
[
25
]
.

Theorem 11.3

(Gröchenig and Stöckler)
.

Let
g
be a totally positive function of finite type
M\geq 2
, then

\mathfrak{F}_{(\alpha,\beta)}(g)=\{\alpha\mathbb{Z}\times\beta\mathbb{Z}\mid\alpha\beta<1\}.

(11.18)

We note that any totally positive function of finite type
M\geq 2
is in the modulation space
M^{1}(\mathbb{R})
(i.e., in Feichtinger’s algebra
S_{0}(\mathbb{R})
). Hence, the frame set of totally positive functions of finite type is full.

Now, one might get the impression that the Balian-Low theorem is the only restriction for separable Gabor systems. However, there is another function for which the separable frame set is known, namely the characteristic function of an interval. The first parts of the frame set where described by Janssen and became known as Janssen’s tie
[
36
]
. The gaps where finally filled by Dai and Sun
[
7
]
. Their work describes in 17 different cases and on more than 100 pages for which parameters the Gabor system with the characteristic function of an interval and a separable lattice is a frame.

11.1
The Frame Set of a Gaussian

As we have seen, for some window classes, it is known that the necessary condition imposed by the Balian-Low theorem is also sufficient for the Gabor system
\mathcal{G}(g,\alpha\mathbb{Z}\times\beta\mathbb{Z})
to be a frame. If we ask for general point sets
\Gamma
, or even only lattices
\Lambda
, then there is precisely one window (or actually one class of windows) for which a full characterization of Gabor frames is known, namely the Gaussian window
g_{0}
(and its metaplectic deformations as well as scalar multiples). In dimensions
d>1
, hardly anything is known about (general) characterizations of Gabor frames.

We will exploit the properties of the Bargmann transform to formulate the question on determining the frame set of a 1-dimensional (standard) Gaussian as a sampling problem in the Bargmann-Fock space
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
. We consider relatively separated (also called uniformly discrete) sets
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}
, that is
\inf_{k\neq l}|z_{k}-z_{l}|>0
for all
z_{k},z_{l}\in\Gamma
. The set
\Gamma
is called a set of sampling for the Bargmann-Fock space
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
if there exist positive constants
0<A\leq B<\infty
such that

A\lVert F\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}^{2}\leq\sum_{z\in\Gamma}|F(z)|^{2}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}\leq B\lVert F\rVert_{\mathcal{F}^{2}}^{2},\quad\forall F\in\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C}).

(11.19)

The condition that
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}
is relatively separated already ensures that the upper frame bound is finite. Since the Bargmann transform
B
maps
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
unitarily to
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
by Theorem
10.4
, we see that
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}
is a set of sampling for
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
if and only if the real version of
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
yields a Gabor frame for the standard Gaussian
g_{0}

29
29

29

Again, we identify
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in the natural way;
\mathbb{C}\ni z=x+iy\,\leftrightarrow(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
.

11.1.1
The Theorem of Lyubarskii and Seip and Wallstén

The following theorem is so important that it deserves to have an own section. It still is one of the most celebrated results in Gabor analysis and was proven independently by Lyubarskii
[
41
]
and Seip and Wallsten
[
47
]
,
[
48
]
in the year 1992.

Theorem 11.4
.

(i)

A discrete set
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}
is a set of sampling for
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
if and only if
\Gamma
can be expressed as a finite union of relatively separated sets and if it contains a relatively separated subset
\Gamma_{0}
with
D^{-}(\Gamma_{0})>1
.

(ii)

The Gabor system
\mathcal{G}(g_{0},\Gamma)
is a frame for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
if and only if
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is relatively separated and its lower Beurling density satisfies
D^{-}(\Gamma)>1
.

We note that the two statements are equivalent by the Bargmann transform. The proof relies, as pointed out above, on the Bargmann transform, which allows to switch to complex analysis (in one variable) and the sampling problem in the Bargmann-Fock space. In
\mathcal{F}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
, entire functions with prescribed zeros can be constructed explicitly by means of (a version of) the Weierstrass sigma function

\sigma(z)=z\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma\backslash 0}\left(1-\frac{z}{\gamma}\right)e^{\frac{z}{\gamma}+\frac{z^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}}.

(11.20)

The function
\tau(z)=\frac{\sigma(z)}{z}
then takes the role of an interpolating function
30
30

30

Note the similarity of
\tau(z)
to the role of sinc function
\frac{\sin(\pi x)}{\pi x}
in the WNKS sampling theorem;

\sin(\pi z)=\pi z\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty\{0\}}\left(1-\frac{z}{k}\right)e^{\frac{z}{k}},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}.

. Then, from the lower Beurling density of the zero set, growth estimates for
\sigma
are obtained. This leads to the result that
\Gamma
is a set of sampling if and only if its lower Beurling density is (strictly) greater than 1.

11.2
The Frame Set in Higher Dimensions

As already mentioned, only few results on Gabor frames for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
d>1
, are known. We state here the main result on tensor products of frames from
[
3
]
(see also Appendix
A.7
).

Theorem 11.5
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be two countable sets. The sequence
(e_{\gamma_{k}})_{\gamma_{k}\in\Gamma_{k}}
is a frame for the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{k}
,
k=1,2
, if and only if
(e_{\gamma_{1}}\otimes e_{\gamma_{2}})_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}}
is a frame for
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

One consequence of the above theorem for higher dimensional Gabor systems is that the necessary density condition can no longer be sufficient.

Example 11.6
.

Consider the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cong L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and the corresponding standard Gaussian
31
31

31

We are a bit sloppy with our notation here as
g_{0}(t)=2^{d/4}e^{-\pi|t|^{2}}
denotes the standard Gaussian in any dimension.

g_{0}(t_{1},t_{2})=2^{1/2}e^{-\pi(t_{1}^{2}+t_{2}^{2})}=g_{0}(t_{1})\otimes g_{0}(t_{2}).

(11.21)

and consider the two Gabor systems
\mathcal{G}_{1}(g_{0},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}(g_{0},\alpha\mathbb{Z}^{2})
with
\alpha<1
. Then, the Gabor system
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{2}
is a (standard) Gaussian Gabor system in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with arbitrarily high density, but it can never be a Gabor frame for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
because
\mathcal{G}_{1}
is not a Gabor frame for its respective Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

\diamond

It is of course possible to construct a wealth of Gabor systems (not only Gaussian) with arbitrarily high density which fail to be frames. Also, it is straight forward to extend the method to tensor product Hilbert spaces of the form
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{N}
.

Appendix A
Appendices

A.1
More Uncertainty Principles

This is a collection of some more uncertainty principles without proof. The interested reader is referred to
[
20
]
and
[
19
]
.

Theorem A.1

(Lieb’s inequalities)
.

Assume
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|^{p}\,d(x,\omega)\begin{cases}\leq\left(\tfrac{2}{p}\right)^{d}(\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g_{2}\rVert)^{p}&2\leq p<\infty\\
\geq\left(\tfrac{2}{p}\right)^{d}(\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g_{2}\rVert)^{p}&1\leq p\leq 2\\
\end{cases}

(A.1)

These inequalities are quite deep results and their proof requires the sharp constants in Hölder’s inequality. A consequence of Lieb’s inequalities is the following result.

Theorem A.2

(Gröchenig)
.

Suppose that
\lVert f\rVert_{=}\lVert g\rVert_{2}=1
. If
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and
\varepsilon\geq 0
are chosen such that

\iint_{U}|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|^{2}\,d(x,\omega)\geq(1-\varepsilon).

(A.2)

Then, we have

|U|\geq\left(\tfrac{p}{2}\right)^{\frac{2d}{p-2}}(1-\varepsilon)^{\frac{p}{p-2}},\qquad\forall p>2.

(A.3)

In particular,

|U|\geq\sup_{p>2}\left(\tfrac{p}{2}\right)^{\frac{2d}{p-2}}(1-\varepsilon)^{\frac{p}{p-2}}\geq 2^{d}(1-\varepsilon)^{2},\qquad(p=4).

(A.4)

The classical Heisenberg-Pauli-Weyl uncertainty principle is minimized by dilated, time-frequency shifted Gauss functions. As the standard Gaussian is a fixed point of the Fourier transform, one may therefore suspect that Gaussian decay is the fastest possible simultaneous decay for a pair of Fourier transforms
(f,\widehat{f})
. This guess is answered affirmatively by Hardy’s Theorem.

Theorem A.3

(Hardy)
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and assume that

f(x)=\mathcal{O}(e^{-a\pi x^{2}})\quad\text{ and }\quad\widehat{f}(\omega)=\mathcal{O}(e^{-b\pi\omega^{2}}),

(A.5)

for some
a,b,>0
. Then, three cases occur;

(i)

If
ab=1
, then
f(x)=ce^{-a\pi x^{2}}
.

(ii)

If
ab>1
, then
f\equiv 0
.

(iii)

If
ab<1
, then any finite linear combinations of Hermite functions satisfies these decay conditions.

Once again, this uncertainty principle can be rephrased in terms of the Rihaczek distribution.

Assume that
|Rf(x,\omega)|=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\pi(ax^{2}+b\omega)^{2}})
. If
ab=1
, then
f(x)=Ce^{-a\pi x^{2}}
. If
ab>1
, then
f\equiv 0
.

According to Metatheorem
C
we may conjecture a Hardy-like uncertainty principle for other time-frequency representations as well.

Theorem A.4

(Gröchenig, Zimmermann)
.

Assume that

|V_{g}f(x,\omega)|=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(ax^{2}+b\omega^{2})}),

(A.6)

for some constants
a,b>0
. Then three cases can occur;

(i)

If
ab=1
and
V_{g}f\not\equiv 0
, then, both,
f
and
g
are scalar multiples of a time-frequency shift of a Gaussian of the form
e^{-a\pi x^{2}}
.

(ii)

If
ab>1
, then either
f\equiv 0
or
g\equiv 0
(or both).

(iii)

If
ab<1
, then the decay condition is satisfied whenever
f
and
g
are finite linear combinations of Hermite functions.

A.2
Hilbert-Schmidt Operators

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space. A bounded operator
K:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
is called a Hilbert-Schmidt operator if

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert Ke_{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}<\infty,

(A.7)

for some orthonormal basis
\{e_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
of
\mathcal{H}
. The Hilbert-Schmidt norm of
K
is given by

\lVert K\rVert_{H.S.}=\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert Ke_{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

(A.8)

This quantity is then independent of the choice of basis. The Hilbert-Schmidt norm dominates the operator norm. Let
f=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{n}e_{n}
, where
c_{n}=c_{n}(f)
,
\{e_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
an orthonormal basis and
\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}=1=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}|c_{n}|^{2}
, then

\lVert K\rVert_{op}^{2}=\sup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f\in\mathcal{H}}}{{\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}=1}}}\lVert Kf\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}|c_{n}|^{2}\lVert Ke_{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}|c_{n}|^{2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert Ke_{n}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\lVert K\rVert_{H.S.}^{2}.

(A.9)

If
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
K
is an integral operator with integral kernel
k
, that is

Kf(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}k(x,y)f(y)\,dy,

(A.10)

then
K
is Hilbert-Schmidt if and only if
k\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
. In this case

\lVert K\rVert_{H.S.}=\lVert k\rVert_{2}.

(A.11)

A.3
Neumann Series

If a linear operator
U
on a Banach space
\mathcal{B}
is close enough to the identity operator
I
, then it is invertible. To be more precise, if
\lVert U-I\rVert_{op}<1
, then U is invertible on
\mathcal{B}
and the inverse operator is given by the Neumann series

U^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(I-U)^{k}.

(A.12)

Furthermore,

\lVert U^{-1}\rVert_{op}\leq\frac{1}{1-\lVert I-U\rVert_{op}}

(A.13)

The convergence of the series should be understood in the sense of the operator norm

\lVert U^{-1}-\sum_{k=1}^{n}(I-U)^{k}\rVert_{op}\to 0,\quad n\to\infty.

(A.14)

For details we refer to
[
5
, Thm. 2.2.3]
,
[
19
, App. A.3]
or
[
27
, p. 48]
.

A.4
Toeplitz Matrices and Laurent Operators

For details on this section and further reading we refer to
[
4
]
. A Toeplitz matrix
M
is a (not necessarily square) matrix, which is constant on the diagonals, i.e.,

M=\begin{pmatrix}m_{0}&m_{-1}&m_{-2}&\ldots&\ldots&m_{-k}\\
m_{1}&m_{0}&m_{-1}&m_{-2}&\ldots&m_{-(k-1)}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
m_{l}&\ldots&\ldots&\ldots&\ldots&\cdot\end{pmatrix}

(A.15)

A Laurent operator is a bi-infinite (square) Toeplitz matrix acting on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
;

L=\begin{pmatrix}\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\\
\ddots&m_{1}&m_{0}&m_{-1}&m_{-2}&m_{-3}&\ddots\\
\ddots&m_{2}&m_{1}&m_{0}&m_{-1}&m_{-2}&\ddots\\
\ddots&m_{3}&m_{2}&m_{1}&m_{0}&m_{-1}&\ddots\\
\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{pmatrix}

(A.16)

The following theorem summaries the main results from
[
4
, Chap. 1]

Theorem A.5
.

Let
m(t)\in C(\mathbb{T})
with Fourier expansion

m(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}m_{k}e^{2\pi ikt},

(A.17)

and let
L(m)
be the associated Laurent operator. Then

\lVert L(m)\rVert_{op}=\lVert m\rVert_{\infty}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lVert L(m)^{-1}\rVert_{op}\leq\lVert L(m^{-1})\rVert_{op}=\lVert m^{-1}\rVert_{\infty}

(A.18)

If
m
is “nice” enough, then the bound for the inverse frame operator is sharp as well.

A.5
Proof of Hudson’s Theorem

We will now prove that
W(f,f)=Wf>0
if and only if
f
is a Gaussian function of the form

f(t)=e^{-\pi t\cdot At+2\pi b\cdot t+c},

(A.19)

where
A\in GL(\mathbb{C},d)
,
\Re(A)>0
(i.e., positive definite) and
A^{*}=\overline{A}^{T}=A
,
b\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
,
c\in\mathbb{C}
.

For the sufficiency, we note that any Gaussian function
f
can be written as a scalar multiple of a finite composition of metaplectic operators applied to the standard Gaussian
g_{0}(t)=2^{d/4}e^{-\pi t^{2}}
and an appropriate time-frequency shift. By the symplectic covariance principle for the Wigner transform, i.e.,
W(f,g)(Sz)=W(\widehat{S}^{1}f,\widehat{S}^{-1}g)(z)
,
S\in Sp(\mathbb{R},2d)
and
\widehat{S}=\mu(S)
, it is therefore sufficient to establish the sufficiency for the standard Gaussian. As

Wg_{0}(x,\omega)=2^{d}e^{-2\pi(x^{2}+\omega^{2})},

(A.20)

this part is done.

For the necessity part, we make use of the Bargmann transform and use some complex analysis. Assume that
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(non-zero) and
Wf\geq 0
. We take the inner product of
Wf
with the Wigner distribution of
g_{0}
and note that, by using the covarince principle in Proposition
3.7
,

W(M_{-\omega}T_{x}g_{0})>0,\quad\forall(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}.

(A.21)

Therefore,

\langle Wf,W(M_{-\omega}T_{x}g_{0})\rangle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}Wf(\xi,\eta)W(M_{-\omega}T_{x}g_{0})(\xi,\eta)\,d(\xi,\eta)>0,\quad\forall(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}.

(A.22)

Now, we apply Moyal’s formula (
3.65
) and Proposition
10.2
for the Bargmann transform to identify the inner product
\langle Wf,W(M_{-\omega}T_{x}g_{0})\rangle
as the Bargmann transform of
f
. Writing
z=x+i\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\langle Wf,W(M_{-\omega}T_{x}g_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=|\langle f,M_{-\omega}T_{x}g_{0}\rangle|^{2}

(A.23)

\displaystyle=|V_{g_{0}}f(x,-\omega)|^{2}

(A.24)

\displaystyle=|Bf(z)|^{2}e^{-\pi|z|^{2}}.

(A.25)

Since the entire function
Bf
does not vanish by (
A.22
), there exists an entire function
q(z)
, such that

Bf(z)=e^{q(z)}.

(A.26)

Furthermore, since

|Bf(z)|e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}\leq\lVert V_{g_{0}}f\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g_{0}\rVert_{2}=\lVert f\rVert_{2},

(A.27)

Bf
satisfies the growth estimate

|Bf(z)|\leq\lVert f\rVert_{2}e^{\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}}.

(A.28)

By taking the logarithm, we obtain the estimate

|\Re\left(q(z)\right)|\leq c+\frac{\pi}{2}|z|^{2}.

(A.29)

It follows by Caratheodory’s inequality of complex analysis (see Appendix
A.6
) that
q
itself satisfies

|q(z)|\leq C_{1}+C_{2}|z|^{2}.

(A.30)

Therefore,
q
must be a quadratic polynomial of the form

q(z)=\pi A^{\prime}z^{2}+2\pi b^{\prime}\cdot z+c^{\prime}.

(A.31)

The restriction of
Bf
to vectors in
i\mathbb{R}^{d}
is

Bf(i\omega)e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\omega^{2}}=e^{q(i\omega)-\frac{\pi}{2}\omega^{2}}=e^{-\pi(\frac{1}{2}+A^{\prime})\omega^{2}+2\pi ib^{\prime}\cdot\omega+c^{\prime}},

(A.32)

which is a Gaussian function. Next, we express the restriction of
V_{g_{0}}f
to
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}
in two different ways. On one hand,

V_{g_{0}}f(0,-\omega)=\langle f,M_{-\omega}g_{0}\rangle=\mathcal{F}(f\,g_{0})(-\omega).

(A.33)

On the other hand, by using Proposition
10.2
once more, we see that

V_{g_{0}}f(0,-\omega)=Bf(i\omega)e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\omega^{2}}.

(A.34)

Therefore,
\mathcal{F}(f\,g_{0})
is in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and is a Gaussian function. It follows, by using the operator
\mu(J)=(-i)^{d/2}\mathcal{F}
, that the function
f\,g_{0}
is itself a Gaussian function. This implies that
f
itself is a Gaussian function and since
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\Re(A)>0
is necessary.

\square

A.6
Caratheodory’s Inequality

This inequality provides size estimates for an analytic function
f
when size estimates are known only for the real part of
f
. As a special case we have the following theorem for entire functions of one variable
[
2
, p. 3]
.

Theorem A.6
.

If
f
is entire on
\mathbb{C}
and
\Re f(z)\leq A_{\varepsilon}|z|^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
, then
f
is a polynomial of degree at most
\alpha
.

Corollary A.7

(Caratheodory’s inequality)
.

If
f(z)=f(z_{1},\ldots,z_{d})
is entire on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
and

\Re f(z)\leq D+E|z|^{2},

(A.35)

then
f
is a quadratic polynomial of the form
f(z)=Az^{2}+b\cdot z+c
for some
d\times d
matrix over
\mathbb{C}
,
b\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
and
c\in\mathbb{C}
.

A.7
Tensor Hilbert Spaces

We repeat a few of the details on tensor Hilbert spaces given in
[
3
]
. Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
be two Hilbert spaces with inner product
\langle.,.\rangle_{k}
and norm
\lVert.\rVert_{k}
,
k,=1,2
, respectively. By
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes_{a}\mathcal{H}_{2}
we denote the algebraic tensor product of the two spaces. We can define an inner product on
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes_{a}\mathcal{H}_{2}
in the following way;

\langle f_{1}\otimes f_{2},g_{1}\otimes g_{2}\rangle=\langle f_{1},g_{1}\rangle_{1}\langle f_{2},g_{2}\rangle_{2},\quad\forall f_{1},g_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1},\;\forall f_{2},g_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}.

(A.36)

The completion under this inner product is a Hilbert space, which we denote by
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
. This is called the topological tensor product of the Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
.
